1915. MARY GOGGIN was indicted for stealing, on the 25th of May, 72lbs. weight of iron, value 6 s., the goods of James Barclay.
GEORGE OSBORN ( police-constable K 129.) I met the prisoner on the night of the 25th of May in Robert-street, Limehouse, about half-past nine o'clock—she was drawing this piece of iron, weighing 72lbs., on the ground with this piece of cloth round it—I spoke to her, and she said she found it is Robert-street—she said, "You won't be so cruel as to take an old woman like me; I have had nothing to eat to-day, you did not see me steal it, you can neither hang me nor transport me for it."
JAMES FINLEY . I am apprentice to James Barclay—his premises are about half a minute's walk from Robert-street. This is his iron—it had been an area—the prisoner could have gone in and shoved it through—the railings an five feet high—it exactly corresponds with the bar it was cut off.
Prisoner. I saw this iron standing by a brick wall—I merely stooped down to see what it was, and the officer took me.
GEORGE OSBORN re-examined. I saw her move six or seven yards, and she had got her hands behind her drawing it by this rag, which is her own apron—she was about 300 yards from the prosecutor's—I did not see any mark on the ground where she had drawn this.
GUILTY . Aged 45.— Confined Two Months.
[trialAccount t18430612-1916]
1916. THOMAS BRIGHTMAN was indicted for stealing, on the 1st of February, 140lbs. weight of printed paper, value 7 l., the goods of Luke Marrow, his roaster.
LUKE MARROW . I live in Cecil-street, Strand. The prisoner was my porter or errand-boy for about six years—in consequence of one of my customer, Mr. Bone, of York-street, Covent-garden, calling on me, I desired the prisoner to walk into the counting-house—Mr. Bone charged him with robbery—I had been employed to fold and stitch catalogues for Mr. Bone—the prisoner gave me some information.
Cross-examined by MR. HUDDLESTON. Q. Did you not say something to the prisoner before he said any thing about the catalogues? A. I did not—I did not say I would not hurt him if he would tell all about it, nor any thing to that effect—I do not know that Mr. Bone did—he might—Mr. Bone is not here.
COURT. Q. Did you send the prisoner out of the counting-house? A. Yes, and while I was speaking to Mr. Bone the prisoner went off—he was apprehended on the following day—these are the catalogues—I know them—they had been taken off my premises—I have no idea of their value—they weigh about 140lbs.—I was employed to stitch and fold a work for the Musical
Library, which was printed by Mr. Norman, of Maiden-lane—I had a quantity of that publication by me—the prisoner had no authority to remove or take any of the catalogues from my premises—I was responsible for them—they were in the rough, as these are now—I could not miss them—I received them from time to time, and they ought to have remained with me till they were stitched—the catalogue contains twelve sheets—it is a catalogue of the publications sold by Mr. Bone—no one has them but me, but I cannot say that these were ever on my premises—there is nothing of my business done to these except folding—I have no one here to prove that they folded any of these—Mr. Bone merely told the prisoner to confess what he had done with the things—I cannot say that he used the word "confess," but it was to the same purpose—Mr. Bone inquired of him where the property was—that was after he told him it would be better for him to confess—the officer found some catalogues—I did not give him any information where to go, nor did Mr. Bone in my presence.
MR. HUDDLESTON. Q. Who was the first that spoke to the prisoner? A. Mr. Bone—he was speaking to me—I then called the prisoner in, and Mr. Bone said to him he had lost some catalogues, and he mentioned the number—I heard him say it would be better for him to confess.
GEORGE PALMER . I am a chandler, and live in Bedfordbury. The prisoner has been to my shop during the last three or four months—he has brought paper similar to this now produced—the paper I received from the prisoner I giye up to Mr. Bone and the officer—I cannot say how often I have purchased paper of the prisoner—I paid him 2 d. a pound for the greater part; but he brought so much that I told him I was almost stocked, and for the latter put of it 1 only gave him 1 1/2 d.—it did not occur to me that these were catalogues, and might be of value to the party—I considered them as waste paper, which I purchase a great quantity of—I did not know the prisoner, but he told me he was a waste-paper dealer—I had about lcwt.—they were not perfect catalogues, not the whole book—they were just as they are here apparently—I pointed out to him, on one occasion, that they seemed to have a great deal of these catalogues—he said, "We are regular waste-paper dealers; we bought as much as 5 cwt. yesterday"—I bought some music-paper of him—the largest quantity he brought at one time was from 17lbs. to 19lbs.—from the beginning of this year to the latter end of April he used to come sometimes once or twice a week, sometimes once a fortnight.
Cross-examined. Q. You buy a good deal of paper of this sort? A. I do—it is the custom for persons who stitch to have the outside sheets.
JAMES SOUTHWELL . I am a grocer, and live in Moor-street, Soho. I bought 71bs. weight of paper of the prisoner twice—I gave him 1 1/2 d. a pound for it—I gave 91bs. of it up to the policeman.
LUKE MARROW re-examined. These came to me in 5000 sheets in one bundle—I can identify these as being the sort of catalogues that I was employed to fold for Mr. Bone—these are folded, but not stitched.
Cross-examined. Q. You know that Mr. Norman is the printer, by his name being at the bottom of these catalogues? A. Yes; be is an extensive printer, and Mr. Bone is an extensive publisher—I always bind for Mr. Norman and Mr. Bone—they never print on outside paper—very often the first impression is not correct—there are very often spoiled sheets—they send me waste sheets to keep the others clean—I collect that waste at times, and sell it—I have sold some of it, but not any quantity—I do not recollect that I have employed the prisoner to sell any paper for me—he has been to Mr. Bone's and Mr. Norman's for me.
COURT. Q. You have never sold any like these? A. No—these are perfect and unsoiled—they would not have been folded if they had not been ready to stitch.
WILLIAM WEST ( police-constable F 106.) I apprehended the prisoner—I afterwards went with Mr. Bone, and fetched this paper from Mr. Palmer's.
(The prisoner received a good character.)
GUILTY. Aged 18.— Recommended to mercy .— Confined Three Months.
[trialAccount t18430612-1917]
1917. ROBERT ESSEX was indicted for stealing, on the 5th of June, 1 watch, value 3 l. 10 s.; 1 guard-chain, 10 s.; and 1 key, 1 d.; the goods of Samuel Demaid; and FREDERICK WARREN for receiving the same, well knowing them to have been stolen.
MARY EGLINTON . I am servant to Mr. Demaid, of Southampton-rot, New-road. About half-past four o'clock in the afternoon, on the 5th of June, Essex came down the area with some fish which had been ordered—he asked for some water—there was some beer in a jug, and I gave him some—I had occasion to turn round for it—he took the beer, and said he would wait for orders—I said I was sure there was nothing wanted—he said he would was and see, till the lady came down stairs—a lady came down, and asked for a glass of beer—I went away with her, leaving Essex in the kitchen—I came back in about two minutes—he then said he would go—I then missed a watch, which I had seen on a nail on the dresser about half an hour before he came in—no one else had been in—it belonged to Mr. Demaid.
SAMUEL DEMAID . I keep the house, No. 8, Southampton-row—I missed the watch from the kitchen when I returned home on the 5th of June—I went with a policeman directly to Essex's master, Mr. Rowell—I said to him, in Essex's presence, "I accuse him of robbing my kitchen of a watch, guard, and chain, and I give him in charge"—Essex said, "You may, and welcome, if you like; I have not robbed your kitchen"—the policeman unbuttoned his jacket, and out fell a lobster, which was bis master's—he then found on him this guard-chain and key of my watch.
JOHN BAINBRIDGE ( police-constable D 105.) I went with Mr. Demaid to Mr. Rowell—I found this guard-chain and key in Essex's waistcoat-pocket, and a lobster in his jacket—I felt something in his pocket, which proved to be a bag, with buttons in it—I said, "Here is the watch"—he said, "No, it is not; the other boy has got it; a boy named Warren, you will find him is the mews"—I then watched for Warren, and Hooper brought me this watch—I afterwards found Warren—I said, "Your name is Warren"—he said, "Yes—I said, "I apprehend you for a watch"—he said, "Yes, I know that"—I said, "The charge against you is for being with Essex, and stealing a watch"—he said, "That is a lie; he gave it me to take care of; I was always told I should be transported for seven years, and if I am I must serve it."
WILLIAM GEORGE HOOPER . I live in Davis's Mews with my father, and Warren lodges at ray father's—I went home to breakfast one morning, and Warren said to me, "I have got something in my pocket worth 5 l."—I said, "What is that?"—he said, "Oh, it is a watch"—I said, "Where did you get it?"—he said, "I did not get it anywhere; Bob Essex gave it me to mind til he came out"—I afterwards saw a boy named Whiffin, and he said to Warren, "The policeman says, if you give up the watch, there will be no more to do with you"—on that Warren gave me a watch—I took it over to the policeman immediately.
Warren. Essex said he would give me a shilling if I would mind it for about five minutes.
JOHN BAINBRIDGE re-examined. I did not say anything to Whiffin, nor
authorise any body to say if he would give up the watch there would be no more said about it.
ESSEX — GUILTY . Aged 14.— Confined Nine Months.
WARREN — NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18430612-1918]
1918. ELIZABETH COX, alias Williams, alias Burrows , was indicted for bigamy.
CAROLINE COX . I live in Waterloo-place, Church-street, Chelsea, and am single. On the 26th of February last, I was present at the parish-church of St. Luke, Chelsea, when my brother, John Richard Cox, was there married to the prisoner—he was a single man—the prisoner was married to him by the name of Elizabeth Burrows—she described herself as single—they were married by banns, by Mr. Kingsley the clergyman of Chelsea—my brother is now living—he was at the time he was married a servant in Charles-street, Grosvenor-square—I had seen the prisoner before that day, but I had no acquaintance with her—I have heard she had been a nurse-maid at Mrs. Pugh's, in Regent-street—when they were married they went home to No. 17, Manor-street, King's-road, Chelsea—I went and remained there that day, but did not stay that night—they had apartments there—I saw them there the next morning—my brother was not out of a situation then—he returned to his situation the next morning, and the prisoner stopped at home at Manor-street—my brother was porter at a large milliner's—he slept there and had charge of the premies at night.
PETER SPURR PARKINS . I am deputy-clerk of the parish of Chelsea—I produce a certificate of the marriage of John Richard Cox to Elizabeth Burrows—I have examined this with the register-book myself—I recollect the fact of the marriage of the parties perfectly well—I was present—their names were put up by banns, and published on three consecutive Sundays—( read)—"John Richard Cox, batchelor, and Elizabeth Burrows, spinster, were married in the parish church of St. Luke, Chelsea, after banns by me, Charles Kingsly, on the 26th of February, 1843."
Prisoner. He is not my husband, I never slept with Mr. Cox.
CAROLINE COX re-examined. They slept together one night—I know that by seeing them there when I went up on the Monday morning between eight and nine—I had left them together between ten and eleven the night before—the landlady was in the house also—I found them up and dressed the next morning—I breakfasted with them—whether they had staid in that house all night I cannot tell—the prisoner took the lodging—she was living there before the marriage for a little time—I had seen her there occasionally when I came home—I dined and drank tea with them on the day of the marriage—my father and mother were there—they went away between ten and eleven at night—the marriage had been talked of in the family for five or six months before.
PETER SPURR PARKINS re-examined. Q. Did the parties give you their names? A. Yes—I cannot say from whom I received the names for the banns, whether it was a man or a woman—the names that were put up were the same names that were afterwards put to the register, otherwise they would not have been married.
ARTHUR WILLIAMS . I am a draper, and live at the Gloucester Arms, Gloucester-row, Kensington. I am at present out of employ—on Tuesday, the 30th of May, I was at the parish church of St. Mary Abbot's, Kensington—that is the name of the parish as it was taken down by the deputy-registrar, who granted me the license—I left the license at the church—the clerk of the parish is not here—I married the prisoner there—I was married
in the name of Arthur Williams, and she in the name of Elizabeth Eleanore Burrows—I had been acquainted with her about nine months—about three months before this she told me that the had been to Henley-on-Thames, and had gone through a kind of ceremony in a private house where her aunt and another person was present and no more, independent of the person who represented himself as a clergyman, and the man named Cox who represented himself as her husband—she said there was nothing took place—she declared to me over and over again that there was no marriage ceremony repeated, but a ring placed on her finger, which she thought was a joke, and she instantly threw it in the fire—she stated nothing else that I am aware of.
Q. Had you seen her in company with the man that she described herself to have gone through this ceremony of the ring with? A. Never, but on one occasion, when I visited her at No. 17, Manor-street—before we were married a person came in and described himself to be her husband—but she met me the same evening and declared to me that the ceremony that was gone through was what I have described, and on the faith of that I kept company with her—she declared that there had only been the ceremony of the ring and that had taken place at Henley-upon-Thames—she absconded from me the day week after our marriage—I had been living in the same house with her up to that time—I produce a certificate of the marriage—I have not examined it with the register—I was present when it was drawn in the vestry, and I have not the smallest doubt of its being faithfully copied—I am acquainted with the parish—it is the parish church where people go on Sundays—I have heard it called by the name of St. Mary Abbot's, Kensington.
Prisoner. I never told him about going to Henley at all. Witness. I am quite positive the transaction she spoke of was at Henley—it was spoken of twenty times, and more than that, during a lapse of more than three months.
Prisoner. He knew I was married before he married me, for Mr. Cox and Caroline Cox told him so, and I told him so myself. Witness. Caroline Cox was in the room when the prisoner's husband made his appearance, and it was said in his presence—she was quite willing to marry me—we were all four in the room, the prisoner, and Caroline Cox, Richard Cox, and myself—I went there without any appointment—I called promiscuously as I passed, and Richard Cox told me she was his wife—he did not tell me under what circumstances the marriage took place—I met her that evening, and she told me what I have stated, that it was a ceremony which she supposed only amounted to a joke, at Henley-on-Thames—she said she knew the ring was borrowed on the occasion from her aunt, who lived there, and she threw it into the fire on ascertaining what it had been for.
Prisoner. He wanted me to be married, and I said I could not, but I met him one day, and he would not let me go till we were married. Witness. We got into an omnibus, and went to the church and were married—the clerk, the pew-opener, and her son, a person of the name of Parsons, were present—none of them are here.
JURY. Q. Did some conversation take place between yourself and Cox? A. Yes—I entered the room—the prisoner and Caroline Cox were there—I had not been there five minutes before in came a person whom I had never seen before, and the two females rushed out of the room—the man told me that the person I had been sitting by the side of was his wife—I appointed to meet him, but in the evening I met the prisoner, and she told me how the ceremony had taken place, and I never kept the appointment—did not put any question to the man when he said this was his wife—he was enraged, as I suppose, against his wife, and instantly the two females rushed
out of the room—I believe he asked what I was doing there—I asked why or upon what authority he ordered me out of the room—he told me the young person by my side was his wife—I asked him to see me again—he said he would be there on the Sunday—I was to have called on him on the Sunday at three o'clock—it was on the Wednesday or Thursday when I saw him—this could not have been more than three weeks or a fortnight after her marriage with him—I married her three or four months afterwards—I called on her frequently at the same lodgings, and never saw Cox there before or after that time—I saw his sister there and elsewhere, but I never had any conversation with her respecting the marriage ceremony, nor has she ever told me.
COURT to CAROLINE COX. Q. Did you ever tell the witness that your sister was a married woman? A. I did a long time back—I told him he had no right to come there, she was a married woman—I said so in her presence, and I told my brother, but he never could see Mr. Williams—my brother came out every other Sunday—Mr. Williams was in the habit of coming down during the week—he never came on Sundays—the prisoner has an aunt—I never saw her, but I heard the prisoner say she had an aunt.
GUILTY. Aged 17.— Recommended to mercy by the Jury, in consequence of Williams's laxity of conduct. — Confined Six Months.
[trialAccount t18430612-1919]
1919. MARY RIPPINGHAM was indicted for stealing, on the 6th of June, 1 spoon, value 2 s., the goods of Thomas Watson.—2nd COUNT, stating it to be the goods of Ann Richards.
WILLIAM HORNE . I am a silversmith. On the 6th of June the prisoner came and offered me a broken silver spoon for sale—I asked her where she got it—she said, "My mistress gave it me for my day's wages"—I asked who her mistress was—she said, "That is my business, not yours"—I sent my young man for a policeman, and she walked out, but before that she said, "If you won't buy it give it me back"—I said, "No, you wait"—my young man brought the policeman, and he took her.
GEORGE MUMFORD ( police-constable K 207.) I took the prisoner—she said she took the spoon from her mistress's premises, in Jubilee-place—I went there, and saw her mistress.
Prisoner. I said I would show him where it was; the Magistrate dismissed the case.
ANN RICHARDS . I am a widow, and live in Jubilee-place. The prisoner worked as a charwoman for my daughter—I have known her nearly four years—she left my house on the 6th of June, and was brought back in about half an hour—this spoon is my daughter's, Margaret Watson—her husband's name is Thomas—they live in the same house with me—I had not given the spoon to the prisoner for wages—it was not broken.
Prisoner. I accidentally broke it; I sat down with it in my pocket.
(The prisoner received a good character.)
GUILTY . Aged 44.— Confined Fourteen Days.
[trialAccount t18430612-1920]
1920. WILLIAM RUMBOLD was indicted for stealing, on the 9th of June, 3 bushels of soot, value 1 s. 2 d.; and 1 sack, 4 d.; the goods of Francis Cullender.
FRANCIS CULLENDEE . I am a chimney-sweeper, and live at Limehouse. I left a sack of soot in charge of Mrs. Dorrell on the 9th of June, and when I returned in the evening I missed it—I afterwards met the prisoner near Cannon-street—I said, "Have you taken my sack and soot?"—he said, "I have not; where was it?"—I said at the corner of Dorset-street—he said, "I have not been that way to-day, I have been at work at the brew-house all day"—I gave him in charge.
MARY DORRELL . I live in Wade's-place. Cullender left the sack of soot with me—the prisoner came up to take it away—I said, "That does not belong to you"—he said, "It does"—I said, "It does not"—he said, "It does, and if you don't let me go I will give you something for yourself"—he took it, and when the prosecutor came up I told him which way he went, and he brought him back with him—he was very tipsy.
JOHN PIERCE ( police-constable K 178.) I took the prisoner—he said he had not taken it, but had been at work at Richmond's brewery.
Prisoner's Defence. I went to Mr. Richmond's, and did a job; the men paid me, and I went straight up to Back-lane, and stopped two hours; I was in liquor; I had not been that way.
GUILTY . * Aged 33.— Confined Two Months.
[trialAccount t18430612-1921]
1921. JOHN SAVAGE was indicted for stealing, on the 4th of June, handkerchief, value 3 s., the goods of Samuel Thomas Sutton, from his person.
SAMUEL THOMAS SUTTON . On Sunday, the 4th of June, I was passing down Shadwell—the prisoner followed me—I felt my pocket move—I turned, and collared the prisoner—he had my handkerchief in his right hand, and was putting it into his pocket—directly I took him, his friends got up, and get him from me—he went round the corner—the policeman seized him, and he was taken—I had laid hold of him—my handkerchief was hardly out of my pocket—he begged of me to let him go—he put it up to his left-breast pocket—I pulled it from his hand.
Prisoner. I had no pocket, I could not put it in.
JOHN COOPER . I am a fisherman, and live at Barking. I was with Sutton—I saw him collar the prisoner, and take the handkerchief out of his hand.
GEORGE PAVITT ( police-constable K 260.) I heard an alarm of "Stop thief"—I saw the prisoner running, and caught him.
Prisoner. I saw it on the ground, and took it up—the prosecutor turned—I said, "Here is your handkerchief"—I said, "There he goes"—I pointed to a man running.
GUILTY . ** Aged 21.— Transported for Ten Years.
[trialAccount t18430612-1922]
1922. WILLIAM VICE was indicted for stealing, on the 6th of June, 1 handkerchief, value 4 s., the goods of Henry Hammond Ogden, from his person.
HENRY HAMMOND OGDEN . I am a master mariner, and live in White Horse-street, Stepney. On the 6th of June I passed through Stepney fair, between six and seven o'clock—I felt my handkerchief leave my pocket—I turned round, and collared the prisoner—I commenced feeling about his body to see if he had it concealed—I was told it was under his feet—I picked it up—I went with him till I found an officer, and handed him the handkerchief.
Prisoner. It was two or three yards from me when you took it up. Witness. It was immediately under his feet.
JOHN KINGSTON . I was in the fair, and curiosity led me to observe the prisoner—he was rolling a red coloured handkerchief under his waistcoat, and casting his eye down to the ground—the prosecutor came up presently, and attempted to collar him—the prisoner cast his eye round, drew the handkerchief from his person, turned round, and faced the prosecutor.
Prisoner. When the prosecutor came and tapped me on the shoulder I was in a crowd of people, and this witness was four or five yards off. Witness. There was a crowd, but I was close to him as I am now.
JAMES PARSONS ( police-constable K 179.) I took the prisoner, and have the handkerchief—he said he did not take it—it was on the ground.
( Property produced and sworn to.)
GUILTY . * Aged 21.— Transported for Ten Years.
[trialAccount t18430612-1923]
1923. THOMAS BOWLER was indicted for stealing, on the 12th of June, 2 pairs of shoes, value 5 s., the goods of John Perkins.
JOHN PERKINS . I live in Field-lane. The prisoner lodged in the same house with me—on the 12th of June I lost a pair of my wife's boots, and a pair my own—these are them—they are called high-lows, or shoes—I saw them the night before, when I went to bed.
ELLEN BARRY . I live in Field-lane. On the 12th of June I saw the prisoner at the pawnbroker's, offering some boots there for 3 s.—I told the pawnbroker to detain the boots, as I thought I knew them—I fetched the prosecutor's wife, and while I was gone the prisoner went away.
Prisoner. I am quite positive I was not in the shop. Witness. I am sure it was you—these are the boots he offered—I took very great notice of them, from the toes to the heels, when I had seen the prosecutor's wife wear them for about ten days.
WILLIAM LEE . I slept in the next bed to the prisoner, at the lodging in Fieid-lane—I saw him on the Wednesday morning after this in Newgate-street—he had a pair of boots on his feet—I gave information to the officer who took him.
BOHLE HARME ( City police-constable. No. 288.) I stopped the prisoner in Newgate-street, about eleven o'clock in the morning, on the 14th—he had these shoes on his feet.
JOHN PERKINS re-examined. These shoes, found on the prisoner's feet, are mine.
Prisoner's Defence. I purchased the shoes on my feet. I did not offer the others to pawn.
GUILTY . * Aged 18.— Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18430612-1924]
1924. ANN PHILLIPS was indicted for stealing, on the 5th of June, 1 carriage, value 1 l., the goods of George Pipe.
GEORGE PIPE . I live in Great Chapel-street I lost this child's chaise on the afternoon of the 5th of June—it had been kept in the back kitchen of the house where I lodge—the prisoner is a stranger.
CHARLES MANSELL WHITROE . I live in Strutton-ground, and am a broker. On the 6th of June, between ten and eleven o'clock in the morning, I saw the prisoner and another woman at my place—they had nothing with them, but the prisoner asked if I would buy a child's chaise—she said she had borrowed 7 s. on it of a Mr. Marchant, a neighbour just by—her face was disfigured, and I said to her, "What have you been at?"—she said her brother had ill used her, and she wished to get a warrant against him with the money—I went to Marcbant's, in Old Pye-street—the prisoner took me up into the one-pair back room—I saw the chaise, and paid him 7 s., and gave the prisoner 12 s. 6 d. more—Marchant is a pig-dealer chiefly—the chaise might fetch me, perhaps, 18 s.—I know the woman who was with the prisoner—she lives in Orchard-street.
JOHN HUDSON ( police-constable B 80.) I took the prisoner—I told her the charge—she said she knew all about the chaise, but did not mean to give me any information—Whitroe gave it up.
Prisoner. I did not know it was stolen, or I should not have taken it where I was known.
CHARLES MANSELL WHITROE re-examined. She said she had it built for her little girl, and that she had been in better circumstances.
GUILTY . Aged 28.— Confined Three Months.
[trialAccount t18430612-1925]
1925. HENRIETTA HAYWOOD was indicted for stealing, on the 20th of May, 10 cigar-cases, value 3 s.; 3 tobacco-pipes, 1 s.; 3 pipe-bowls, 9 d.; 2 snuff-boxes, 8 d.; 34 cigars, 2 s. 6 d.; 1 umbrella, 1 s.; 3 ounces of snuff, 9 d.; 1 cup, 3 d.; 1 saucer, 3 d.; 21 cards, 3 d.; 3 cigar-tubes, 1 s.; 2 snuff-boxes, 9 d.; 1 walking-stick, 9 d.; and 1 pound of tobacco, 4 s. 6 d.; the goods of Solomon Phillips, her master: and WILLIAM HAYWOOD , for feloniously receiving the same; well knowing them to have been stolen; against the Statute, &c.; to which
HENRIETTA HAYWOOD pleaded GUILTY . Aged 18.— Transported for Seven Years.
SOLOMON PHILLIPS . I live in High Holborn, and am a tobacconist; Henrietta Haywood lived with me for two months as servant of all work; the other prisoner is her father. On the 3rd of June she had the charge of my house—I went out, and came home about a quarter before seven o'clock—the door of my parlour, in my passage, was open—I had left it locked—I found the drawer open in which I kept my cash-box—I had left that drawer locked—I did not look into my cash-box at that time, but on going to the drawer some time afterwards, (after having gone out in the mean time)—I missed all the money from the cash-box except a fourpenny-piece—I had lost about 25 l. in gold and silver—there were sovereigns, half-sovereigns, half-crowns, and a great many fourpenny-pieces—I got an officer, and found Henrietta Haywood at her father's at Holloway—I saw her give the officer a reticule with a portion of the money in it, saying, "There is part of your property"—when we took her to the station we went back to her father's house, and there we found these ten cigars, three tobacco pipes, and the rest of the things that are mentioned in the indictment.
William Haywood. I had no knowledge that there was anything stolen on my premises—they were found in a box. Witness. They were in two boxes, one locked, the other not—William Haywood refused to give the key of the box till the officer insisted on it—the greater part of these things were found in the box that was locked—in the box that was not locked there were two or three books—he had only one room—there was nobody in the room at first, but William Haywood came in while we were in the room—I know he lived in that room, because, when he came home, the people in the house said, "Here comes the father"—he came into that room of his own accord, and produced the key of the locked box—I know these things—I cannot say when I lost them—I did not miss them till the 3rd of June—it was owing to the other discovery that I went to the house and found these things—William Haywood had some duplicates, which he dropped down by his side—the policeman picked them up.
William Haywood. They were of things that my daughter had pawned above twelve months; the box was locked; I had got nothing in it; my daughter had free access to it, and had a key of it; I had no knowledge of these things being there; I had some gunpowder in it, and locked it, or my children would have got at it. Witness. There was some gunpowder; almost all the articles in the box were mine, except the gunpowder; there were some glasses for watches which were not mine; there was an umbrella standing up in the room, belonging to me; some of the duplicates related to cane and two snuff boxes.
William Haywood. I have no knowledge of anything of the kind; I had lost two umbrellas; I took this umbrella and put it in its place again; I was not positive it was mine; I asked no question about it; the pawnbroker does not identify me as the person who pawned these things; my daughter has frequently pawned things, and I have found tickets in my room; she has got with many bad characters at coffee-houses, which has led her to this.
GEORGE JOHN RESTIEAUX ( police-constable E 49.) I went to the lodging of William Haywood on the 3rd of June—what was found in the box was worth about 10 s.—while I was searching the box William Haywood threw down these duplicates intentionally—he had taken them out of his pocket—I heard a rustling, and turned and saw them in his hand—they enabled me to trace two snuff boxes and a cane.
William Haywood. These tickets you must have polled out of the box. Witness. No such thing; I had not pulled anything out of that side—I put the things out for Mr. Phillips to see them—I felt you were rustling—I turned, these were in your hand, and you dropped them—these were not in the box—I found seven duplicates besides these.
JAMES LAW . I am shopman to a pawnbroker in Turnmill-street, Clerkenwell—I produce these two snuff boxes, pawned for 4 d., and a cane for 2 d.—the officer produces the duplicates which relate to them—they were pledged on the 24th of May, in the name of John Haywood, by a man, but not the prisoner.
SOLOMON PHILLIPS re-examined. Henrietta Haywood was in my service on the 24th of May, and had the opportunity of taking these—I can identify them.
GEORGE JOHN RESTIEAUX re-examined. I am not mistaken about the tickets—I am positive that William Haywood dropped them out of his hand—I immediately took these five cigars out of his pocket—I found thirty-four cigars in the box, and some of them are the same as those I took from his pocket.
SOLOMON PHILLIPS re-examined. These cigars are mine, I am sure—I had missed a great number of them off my counter, and could not tell in what manner they were going.
William Haywood's Defence. The children told me my daughter had access to the box, and she had got a key to go to it at any time. I had given to good advice. I have been in noblemen's families, and have had a good character. I had the Austrian ambassador's service of plate under my care, and Sir Watkin Wyun's, and Lord Aberdeen's.
(John Fox, clerk to a brewer, and Matthew Franklin, of the Barley Mow, in Bunhill-row, gave William Haywood a good character.)
WILLIAM HAYWOOD GUILTY . Aged 59.— Transported for Seven Years.
Fifth Jury, before Edward Bullock, Esq.

[trialAccount t18430612-1927]
1927. WILLIAM SHARP was indicted for breaking and entering the dwelling-house of John Graham Lough, on the 17th of May, at St. Maryle-bone, and stealing therein 1 breast-pin, value 1 l. 5 s.; 1 razor-case, 6 d.; and 7 razors, 2 l. 9 s.; his property.
MR. RYLAND conducted the Prosecution.
JOHN GRAHAM LOUGH . I reside at No. 13, Melcombe-place, Dorset-square, and am a sculptor—I dwell in the house—I left my house on the 17th of May about seven o'clock, and returned about half-past eleven—I found it had been entered from the attic window—there are some new buildings going on close by, and I suppose they had got up by them—my servant had locked the attic door, and the box of the lock was unscrewed and taken off, and the door left open—I cannot tell whether they were safe when I left the house—I searched that night, but did not miss anything—next morning, about a quarter
before seven o'clock, I found that my razor-case and seven razors had been taken from the dressing-table in the front room, on the second floor—a diamond pin was left on the dressing-table, I had seen it there the day before, and that was gone—I have seen the rasors in possession of the police, but not the case—I recognised the razors instantly—these are them—I have no hesitation in saying they are mine—I am quite sure they were in the house the day before—I use them every day.
Cross-examined by MR. HORRY. Q. It was between seven and twelve o'clock was it? A. I went out about seven, and when I came back it must have been between half-past eleven and twelve.
ELIZABETH CURZON . I am in the service of Mr. Lough. On the 17th of May I fastened up the house—I went to the attic that evening a few minutes before twelve o'clock—there was a lock on that door, and I had seen it safe some time before—I found the door open, and the lock taken off—it was safe at ten o'clock that morning, which was the last time I had been in the room—the window was open—the key was in the door, as I had left it, but the lock was off—I had seen the prisoner about the premises about a month before that.
Cross-examined. Q. Was the prisoner remanded, that a shoemaker might speak to him as a man who had been seen about the premises? A. No, I recollect nothing of that kind.
JOHN THOMAS CRESWELL . I am shopman to Mr. Wood, a pawnbroker, in St. John-street, Clerkenwell. On the 19th of May, about half-past eleven o'clock, the prisoner came and produced these razors to pledge—I had received information, and asked him if they were his own—he said, "Yet," and asked me to advance 5 s. on them—I detained him under frivolous pretences, and said I would send to ascertain the value of them, while I sent for a policeman—when the policeman came, I asked the prisoner again if they were his own—he said no, they were given to him by a man with whom he had been playing at skittles at the Sun Dials—he went away with the policeman.
Cross-examined. Q. He did not offer to go away? A. No.
MR. RYLAND. Q. Would you have let him go if he had tried? A. I think not—there were two young men in the shop.
JOSEPH PRIOR ( police-constable G 65.) I was called to the pawnbroker's—I found the prisoner there—Mr. Creswell said he had brought these razors to pledge—I asked where he got them—he said he had been playing at skittles with a man at the Sun Dials, and he had given them him to get some money on them—I took him to the station, and there he told the same story—he repeated it before the Magistrate, and the Magistrate said I had better go there—I proposed to the prisoner to go, but he would not—I went to the publican to make inquiries, and he had not seen him.
Cross-examined. Q. Are you quite certain that he did not say he would not go handcuffed, and he would not go without a cab? A. He said he would not go unless he was carried—I was in the office when a shoemaker was sent for, and the prisoner was put with some other persons—the shoemaker pointed out another man, who he said was very like the man he had seen on the premises—there is a public-house in Sutton-street—I do not know that it is the Sun—I know the Sun Dials—it is about 200 yards from the pawnbroker's, and about the same distance from the public-house in Sutton-street.
MR. RYLAND. Q. What house did the prisoner say he had been at? A. The Sun Dials—he repeated it two or three times—he said he had just left it with these razors to get some money on them, for the man he had been playing at skittles with.
THOMAS PARKER . I am the landlord of the Sun Dials, in Goswell-street. I never saw the prisoner till the other day at the police-court—he was not at my house on the 19th of May, and I had no skittles playing at my house that day—the ground was closed the night before, and not opened at all for play that day—it is a private subscription ground, and no subscribers were there that day, before the evening—I do not know any other public-house in the neighbourhood that is called the Sun Dial or the Sun—I know the public-house in Sutton-street, but I do not know the sign.
Cross-examined. Q. Have you looked at the front of that house in Sutton street? A. Never to notice it—the George and the Pitt's Head are the only two that I know the signs of—I have never said that the public-house in Sutton-street was the Sun, or that I believed it was—I believe there are two or three other skittle-grounds in my neighbourhood—I am not aware whether the public-house in Sutton-street has a skittle-ground.
MR. HORRY called
HENRY ADAMS . I work for my father, who is a butcher, at No. 11, Granby-street, Waterloo-road—I have known the prisoner since the 2nd of May. On Wednesday night, the 17th of May, I saw him at six o'clock, and I was with him the rest of the evening in the gallery of the Surrey Theatre—he was with me till twelve, and went home with me to my father's, where he was living—I recollect it because it was the night of the prisoner's birthday.
MR. RYLAND. Q. Are you apprenticed to your father? A. No, I am his assistant—the prisoner has been lodging at my father's two months—I will undertake to swear that we got home about twelve o'clock at night—it was nearer twelve than eleven—I do not know the prosecutor's premises—we came out just before the curtain dropped, and we went straight home—he slept not in the same bed with me, but in the same room—Mrs. Sharp, his wife, told me in the morning that it was his birthday—she is lodging at my father's—she slept that night in another room with a friend, on the first floor, and we slept on the second floor, and a gentleman named Parsons—I saw Mr. Sharp and the lady go to their chamber door—I believe the lady is a widow—she was there on a visit—the lower part of the house is my father's shop—my father and his family slept in the first floor back room—my father has two children at home besides me—they slept in the back room on the second floor—I never was at the Surrey Theatre with the prisoner before, nor any where else—it may be ten minutes' walk from our house—I saw the prisoner at my father's the next day—I did not see him after the Friday morning till now—Mrs. Sharp desired me to come here, to say that her husband was with me at the Surrey Theatre that evening—she did not remind me of the day—I am not to get anything for it.
MR. HORRY. Q. Are you sure he was with you there that day? A. Yes—no one went there with me but the prisoner and Mrs. Sharp—when the prisoner went out on the Friday morning he was a little in liquor.
MR. RYLAND. Q. When you saw him on Friday morning, was he in perfectly good health? A. Yes—he had a black eye, nothing else that I am aware—he was not out of my sight more than five minutes—the Elephant and Castle is not more than five minutes' walk from the Surrey Theatre—there are plenty of omnibuses there—some of them go to the Alpha-road.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18430612-1928]
1928. HENRY ALEXANDER HARDY was indicted for embezzling 8 l. 15 s., which he had received on account of his master, Peter Annandale; to which he pleaded
GUILTY . Aged 24.— Confined Twelve Months.
(The prisoner received a good character.)
[trialAccount t18430612-1929]
1929. HANNAH MANSHIP and SARAH VICKERS were indicted for stealing, on the 25th of April, 1 spoon, value 3 s.; 2 plates, 5 s.; 1 bottle, 2 s.; 1 cup, 9 d.; saucer, 9 d.; and 1 stopper, 6 d.; the goods of Henry Armour.
ANN ARMOUR . I am the wife of Henry Armour, a cabinet-maker, we live in Old Cavendish-street—on Friday, the 21st of April, Vickers called at our house to look at some lodgings which she said she wanted for a family—she said a lady would arrive that evening, and the rest of the family on the Tuesday following, that she, herself, was the lady's maid—I showed her our apartments—it was then about three o'clock, and she went out in the afternoon and ordered goods, which were brought to the house by the trades people—she left at six o'clock, and said she was going to meet her mistress—she came back afterwards with another woman, whom I did not see till the next evening, and it turned out to be Manship—Vickers was up and down, or in attendance on what she called her mistress—on the Saturday evening the drawing room bell rang, and in the absence of Vickers, the maid, I answered it, and saw Manship in the room alone—she inquired for her maid, I told her she was absent, and I believed giving orders to trades people for the next day—I offered to send her any thing she wanted—she said she was alarmed about her maid being out so late in the evening, as she was perfectly unacquainted with London—(I think it was then about ten o'clock)—I said, if it would be any satisfaction to her to know that her maid was not alone, I had permitted my daughter to walk with her—on the following Tuesday Vickers left the kitchen about half-past three o'clock, saying she had to meet another part of the family, and about five o'clock that evening I found the prisoners were both gone—I missed a tea spoon, two India China plates, a water bottle, and a stopper for the ink stand—they were all my husband's, and were in the apartments let to the prisoners—they went up for their use when they required them—these are them.
Cross-examined by MR. DOANE. Q. When Vickers came, did she not tell you that she took the lodgings for Mrs. Barker? A. Yes—Mrs. Barker and family—she went out, and about six o'clock she brought Manship as Mrs. Barker—no one else came while they were there—I do not believe any gentleman slept in the house—if there was a man in the house they must have let him in themselves—Vickers never told me there was a gentleman visited the lady—she did not say I need not let my husband know it—they took five rooms furnished—they had two bed rooms, and another was to be fitted up for them—we were to turn a drawing room into a temporary bed room when the family arrived—there is a shop at our house which is kept by a gentleman who is a house agent, but it is quite unconnected with us—I think these things before me were valued at 15 s. at the station—I do not know whether any one would advance that on them—the prisoners brought a small carpet bag—I do not know what it contained—I saw no boxes of clothes—their luggage was to have arrived by a porter, as they said, but it never came—I have no mark or initials on my spoons, but on this spoon there is a small particular bend on the upper part near the handle—I can account for its being done—I have no mark on any of this property, but I have others to compare with it—I believe these plates are not common—I have others exactly like them, and I have had them many years—I believe them to be mine.
COURT. Q. How many of these plates had you? A. Six—I had only four left after I missed these two—I know them perfectly well—I have had them fourteen or fifteen years—they were kept in a china closet, and I saw them from time to time—these were sent up for the prisoners' use on the day they quitted.
Manship. This property is mine; I bought this bottle in Tottenham-court-road; she says she saw me in her house, and I never saw her in my life till I was at the station; I could prove where I was, but the gentleman is out of town. Witness. I am sure these things are my property—I saw Manship in my house on the second night, and she had an opportunity of seeing me when I went into her room.
JOHN EATON ( police-constable S 193.) On Thursday evening, the 4th of May, I was sent for, and found Manship in a pawnbroker's shop, in the Hampstead-road—I told her she was charged with a robbery in Old Cavendish-street—she said, "I never was in Old Cavendish-street in my life"—I took her to the station, and the inspector asked where she lived—she said, "I will not tell anybody"—on the Saturday following, I went to a lodging in George-street, Hampstead-road—I found this water-bottle there, two china plates, this cup and saucer and stopper, and a duplicate for a silver spoon.
ELIZABETH WEST . I live in George-street, Hampstead-road. On Saturday, the 29th of April, the two prisoners came to my house for apartments—I showed them some, and let them to Manship—she brought some things is between eight and nine o'clock the same evening—on the following Wednesday, I said to Vickers, who went by the name of Smith at my house, "I think I know your face somewhere"—she said, "Oh, dear, no"—I said, "I am sure I do; is not your name Vickers?"—she said, "No"—I said, "Do you know Mrs. Stevens, of Sussex-street?"—she said, "No"—I afterwards went with Vickers to the station, to inquire after Manship—I remember the constable coming, and finding these articles in the room, which I let to the prisoners—I was present when he found them—Manship told me Vickers was her sister.
THOMAS BARRETT . I am shopman to Mr. Crouch, a pawnbroker, in Grafton-street East. This silver tea-spoon was pawned on the 29th of April, by Vickers—I am positive it was her—I knew her very well—this is the duplicate of it—I knew Manship.
Manship. Q. Have I ever sent any thing to your shop that was not my own? A. Not to my knowledge—you have a great deal of plate at our shop—that is how I became acquainted with both the prisoners.
ELIZABETH MARTIN . I am sister to Mrs. Armour. On the 25th of April, I was at her house in Old Cavendish-street, about ten o'clock in the evening—I saw Vickers in the kitchen, and Manship going up the stairs of the first floor—I am sure of the prisoners' persons.
Manship. I never was in the house in my life. Witness. I will take my oath it was you.
Manship's Defence. The plates I received of an aunt of mine, at Turnham-green, named Carter; I had them before I was married; I had five or six; they have been all broken but three; when I moved to Mrs. West's I had the three; I bought the cup and saucer, and the bottle in Tottenham-court-road; the tea-spoon is mine; I had half-a-dozen of them; they are not marked, but I had some that are—I kept a very large and respectable house.
MANSHIP — GUILTY . Aged 33.— Confined Twelve Months.
VICKERS — GUILTY . Aged 22.— Confined Three Months.
(There were other indictments against the prisoners.)
NEW COURT.— Monday, June 19 th, 1843.
Fifth Jury, before Mr. Common Sergeant.

1934. WILLIAM BARKER KILPIN was indicted for embezzling the sums of 2 l. 10 s., 1 l. 4 s. 4 d., 13 s. 4 d., 15 s., 13 s. 4 d., 11 s. 8 d., 15 s., 1 l. 6 s. 8 d., 10 s., 11 s. 8 d., 15 s., 12 s. 6 d., and 558 l. 2 s. 1 d., the monies of the trustees of the poor of the parish of St. Leonard's, Shoreditch. —3rd and 4th COUNTS, stating it to belong to Samuel Beaumont Cheesewright and others; to all of which he pleaded
GUILTY. Aged 63.— Recommended to mercy.
( William Hall, law-stationer, in Hungerford-street, Strand; John Robinson, Westmoreland-place, City-road; Samuel Beaumont Cheesewright, one of the trustees of the poor of St. Leonard, Shoreditch; William Coles Fuller, dressing-case maker, Brownlow-street, Holborn; and Thomas Thomas, Robert-street, Hoxton, gave the prisoner an excellent character.)
Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18430612-1935]
1935. JOHN SMITH was indicted for stealing, on the 7th of June, three shillings, the monies of Thomas Lloyd; and THOMAS RABONE as an accessory.
MR. DOANE conducted the Prosecution.
ELIZABETH LLOYD . I am the wife of Thomas Lloyd, a gun-maker, in Henry-street, Stepney. About half-past seven o'clock on the 7th of June, Smith came to me and said, "Mr. Lloyd has sent me for 3 s."—I said, "Where is Mr. Lloyd's own lad?"—he said, "He is out"—I asked his name and where he lived—he said, "In Berner's-street, Commercial-road"—I asked what Mr. Lloyd wanted the money for—he said, "To buy some tools"—I believed him, and gave him three shillings—my husband came soon after, and I told him.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. I suppose your husband would have had no objection to your parting with the money supposing this had been a true story? A. No.
THOMAS LLOYD . I am the husband of Elizabeth Lloyd. I never saw Smith before this affair—I did not send him to my wife to get 3 s. for me—Rabone has worked for me about seven months—I have frequently seen him since—Smith was brought to my house in custody, by Bowles—I asked him
who sent him for the 3 s.—he said a man had sent him, and told him he should have 2 d. if he got it—he said he had the 2 d. from the man, and he gave the man the 3 s.—I went with him to the station—he pointed out the corner of Russell-street, as where the man was—I asked if he had any money about him—he produced several things, but no money—I afterwards thought I heard something fall—I looked round, and saw a shilling close behind him, on the pavement—I said to him, "How could you tell me you had not any money, and you have now dropped that shilling?"—he said, "They can search me now, I have not any more"—when I got to the station, I asked him again who sent him for the money—he told me the same as before—I fetched his father to the station—Rabone was not in custody then—Smith told his father he and Rabone had divided it between them.
Cross-examined by MR. PRENDERGAST. Q. You told him it would be much better for him to tell the truth? A. No, I do not believe I did.
ROBERT BOWLES . I am in the service of the prosecutor. I heard something from him about this—I obtained a description of Smith, and about half-past eight the next morning I saw him in the street, and asked where he was last night—he said, "Playing in King-street"—I asked if he was with Tom Rabone—he said, "No," that he was at Mr. Haggerty's, an iron shop, in Back Church-lane—I then let him go—I afterwards had some conversation with other persons, and took Smith, and asked if he knew a person named Lloyd—he said, "No"—I asked if he was sure he did not—he said, "Yes"—I asked if he was sure he was not at Lloyd's last night, he said, "No"—I took him to Mr. Lloyd's—as we went along, I said if he knew anything about it, he bad better save a bother, and after that he told me he did go—I took Rabone in Gloucester-place.
JOHN BURKE ( police-constable H 81.) I remember the prisoners being brought to the station—I heard Smith say he received 3 s. from Mrs. Lloyd, and gave 1 s. 6 d. to Rabone—Rabone did not say anything to that—when I took Rabone to the police-court, he said something, which was taken down—I believe I know Mr. Norton's handwriting—I do not think this is his—I do not know it.
NICHOLAS PEARCE . I am a police-inspector. This is Mr. Norton's handwriting—( read)—"The prisoner, Rabone, on being asked whether he wished to say anything in his defence, says, 'I had half of the 3 s. obtained from Mrs. Lloyd.' The prisoner Smith, on being asked whether he wished to say anything, says, 'I did not have the half-crown, but the 3 s. I did.'"
NOT GUILTY .

[trialAccount t18430612-1937]
1937. GEORGE MUNROE, JOHN MUNROE , and JAMES WRIGHT , were indicted for stealing, on the 29th of May, 20 feet of leaden pipe, valuer 20 s., the goods of William Parsons, and being fixed to a certain building; to which
G. MUNROE pleaded GUILTY . Aged 16.
WRIGHT pleaded GUILTY . Aged 14.
Transported for Seven Years. Ship.
WILLIAM PARSONS . I live in Queen's Head-court, Giltspur-street, and have some small houses in Palestine-place, Stoke Newington. On the 1st of June I received information—I went to one of the houses, and observed the pipe was gone—I had not seen it since I saw it in its place a fortnight before
Christmas—the policeman produced some pipe to me—it was cut to pieces—I believe it to be mine—it was fixed.
WILLIAM PETT . I live in Prospect-place. About three o'clock, on the 29th of May, I saw Wright—he asked if I would go down to Mr. Munroe's house, to see if they were coming out—I said, "Yes," and we went down, and saw the two Munroes at the window, and found there was nobody at home—they asked me and Wright if we were willing to get some lead—we said "Yes,"—we went into the other house, and saw the lead—it had been cut off before, and we carried it down stairs—we had not time to cut it to pieces—we were disturbed by a constable—about half-past twelve o'clock that night we were all taken—we had been to the Standard Theatre.
JAMES BRENNAN ( police-constable N 69.) I received information, an went to this house in Palestine-place, and found a quantity of pipe had been taken from the kitchen—I traced a footstep from there to No. 4, where the Munroes live—I found a quantity of lead in the copper-hole—the door of it was shut—I have compared this lead with the pipe left in the wall at Mr. Parsons, and they correspond exactly.
JAMES COWARD ( police-constable N 20.) I received information, and met the prisoner returning home, at half-past twelve o'clock, with Pett and his brother—I asked where they had been—they said to the Standard Theatre—I caught John and George Munroe, and Wright said, "I know what you want us for; it is the job we have done to-day; I hope this will send us over the water. Brennan little thought, while he was at the back of the house, that we ran out at the front"—on taking them to the station, there was a piece of the brass cock on the floor, and Wright said to George, "That is the part you got hold of "—George said to John, "No, it is the part you got hold of"—next morning I found part of this pipe in the back room.
JOHN MUNROE GUILTY . * Aged 13.— Transported for Seven Years.—Convict Ship.
[trialAccount t18430612-1938]
1938. JOHN EDWARDS was indicted for stealing, on the 7th of June, 1 handkerchief, value 3 s., the goods of Charles Johnson, from his person.
Charles Johnson. I am a broker, and live in Old-street, St. Luke's. Between one and two o'clock in the morning of the 7th of June, I went to the Rising Sun at Stepney—it was Stepney-fair—I called for gin-and-water—I went into the parlour—the prisoner made way for me to sit down—he introduced a portrait of himself, and said, "Will you allow me to take yours?"—I said, "No"—he said, "Will you allow me to sip out of your glass?"—I said, "I will pour you out a little in your glass, if you want it" he said he was about singing a song—he called a respectable man, who sat two or three persons off to sing a duet—after that I felt a tug at my pocket—I thought I had sat on my coat—I felt another tug, and then found my handkerchief was gone—I seized the prisoner, and said, "You have robbed me"—he said, "You are wrong"—I said, "You have my handkerchief "—he said, "I have not got it"—he made a push, and put it on the table, and said, "I only took it for a joke"—it came from under his jacket—he made his way to the door, and the officer took him.
COLIN M'KENZIE ( police-constable R 371.) On the 7th of May a woman called me, and told me to take the prisoner, who was coming out of the Rising Sun—I stopped him, and took him inside—he then said it was all right—the prosecutor said he had taken his handkerchief, but he was inclined to forego the prosecution, as he had admitted he took it—the prisoner said, "I never admitted anything of the sort"—the prosecutor said, "Take him"—the prisoner then said, "I beg your pardon."
Prisoner's Defence. He left his handkerchief on the seat; I had it in my hand; he said, if I would own I took it he would let me go. I said I did got take it.
GUILTY . Aged 19.— Confined Three Months.
[trialAccount t18430612-1939]
1939. ELIZABETH WOOD was indicted for stealing, on the 6th of June, 3 sovereigns, the monies of Elizabeth Percival.
ELIZABETH PERCIVAL . I am single, and live in Little Chapel-street, Soho. On the 3rd of June I received my income, 18 l. odd—I had a little to drink, and was taken with a giddiness in my head, and lost my senses—when I awoke, I was in bed with the prisoner—I never was in that place before—I was in a strange place—we had some breakfast—the prisoner said the would go and see me home—I went down stairs, and pulled out a sovereign—I gave it to her, and asked her if she would go and get something to drink—she went out, and did not return—I had eight sovereign there, and missed two more, besides the one I gave her—she had an opportunity of taking them—there was no one else there when I went in.
Prisoner's Defence. I brought back the silver, and gave it her. Witness. I never received it.
ROBERT TAILOR ( police-constable F 37.) I know the prosecutrix—she is represented to me by her landlord as not in her right senses—I have conversed with her, and think she is rather weak—the landlord called me to the door, and I told him if he thought proper to give the prisoner into custody I would take her—he declined it—I found the prosecutrix half drunk—she said she had eight sovereigns in her box, and then there were only fire—I then went to look for the prisoner, and found her counting a handful of money—she had 3 l. 4 s. 6 d.—there were two sovereigns, and the rest in silver.
CHARLES JAMES LANCEFEILD . I live in Little Chapel-street. The prosecutrix, who lodges with me, came home with the prisoner at half-past ten o'clock that morning—I went down to expostulate with her—I heard some sovereigns on the table and heard them quarrelling about putting them into the box—I went for a policeman, and during that time the prisoner went out for change by the prosecutrix's order—she returned and went down—I then listened on the stairs—I never heard her give her the change—she then came up—I said to her, "Do you know that woman?"—she said, "Yes, for some years"—I said, "Give me your address"—she said, "Mrs. Wood, Oxford-street"—the policeman then came, and we let the prisoner go—we then went down, and saw the prosecutrix—I asked if she had lost any money—she said no, she had got eight sovereigns in a box—we looked, there was only five,—the policeman ran after the prisoner, brought her back, and she was quite drunk and very abusive—there was no silver found in the prosecutrix's room, although the place was well searched directly.
Prisoner's Defence. The money found on me was my own; I have kept a stall in Oxford-street many years.
GUILTY . Aged 55.— Confined Two Months.
[trialAccount t18430612-1940]
1940. MARY WILLIAMS was indicted for stealing, on the 2nd of May, 1 shawl, value 6 d.; the goods of Matthew Creasey; 1 coat, value 5 s.; 1 pair of boots, 5 s.; 10 yards of carpet, 5 s.; 3/4 of a yard of linen cloth, 1 s.; 1 pepper-box, 1 s.; 1 petticoat, 1 s.; 2 aprons, 1 s.; 1 kettle, 1 s.; 1 pair of stockings, 8 d.; 7 plates, 6 d.; 1 loaf of bread, 6 d.; 1 window-blind, 6 d.; I shovel, 2 d.; and 1/2lb. weight of soft sugar, 3 d.; the goods of George Freeman.
MARY ANN FREEMAN . I am the wife of George Freeman, and live in
King-street, Long-acre. At half-past five o'clock, on the 2nd of May, I was in bed—I was awoke by a person taking the pillow from the other side of the bed—I jumped out of bed immediately, and saw the prisoner by my bedside with a small bundle under her left arm—she got out—I screamed and ran to the street door—the market people were going by, and two or three ran, but they could not find her—she took the key of the back parlour with her—I then missed these things—I had the lock taken off the back parlour door, and the door bolted inside—at a quarter before three o'clock in the morning, of the 27th of May, I awoke my husband, and said there were footsteps in the passage—he said, "Lie still, you are always fancying something"—in about seven minutes I heard the key put into the back parlour door—I turned and put my hand to the street door, and the key was going round the wood—I said, "It is there now, come and hold the person"—he came and held her, till I slipped on my gown, and got the policeman—it turned out to be the prisoner—I swear she is the same person that came before—she had on this shawl which is mine, and which I lost on the 2nd of May—this key was found on her, and it is the one I lost on the 2nd of May, and fits the lock of the door.
Prisoner's Defence. I was not near the place on the 2nd of May; I bought this shawl in Monmouth-street; the key belonged to my room.
GUILTY . Aged 45.— Confined Nine Months.
[trialAccount t18430612-1941]
1941. JOSEPH STOKES and THOMAS LEACH were indicted for stealing, on the 7th of June, 1 cask, value 5 s. 6 d.; and 5 bushels of cement, 7 s., 6 d.; the goods of Elisha Allsop and another, their masters, and JOHN NICHOLAS for feloniously receiving the same, well knowing them to have been stolen, against the statute, &c.
ELISHA ALLSOP . I am a builder, and live in Back-road, St. George's. I was at work at St. John-street, Sidney-square—Leach was foreman—I sent him two casks of cement at half-past eight o'clock in the morning of the 7th of June—I received some information, and went to John-street—I missed a cask of cement—I had sent two that morning, and they both ought to have been there—I then went in search of Leach, and found him at the Tom and Jerry shop in Charles-street, with Stokes—I called Leach out, and asked him to bring back the cement which he had taken away—he said he had taken nothing away—I said if he did not bring it back, I should give him and Stokes in charge—I met Squires, I told him, and gave Leach in his charge—I then sent Squires to the Tom and Jerry shop for Stokes—Leach then said he had sold the cement to Nicholas for three pots of beer—Nicholas was at the Tom and Jerry shop at that time—we proceeded to Nicholas's house, and at the back of his premises found the empty cask, which I identify by a mark on the bottom—it was one that I sent that morning by Leach—I did not see Nicholas till he was at the station—he said he knew nothing about it, and Stokes said he was innocent—I have seen some cement which I think was the same, but I would not swear to it—Leach and Stokes were very drunk—if Leach had been sober he would not have done it—he worked for my father many years.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. Did not Nicholas say he had left his shop in charge of somebody? A. No—Mrs. Nicholas lives there, and when her husband is out she has charge of the shop.
DAVID ALLSOP . I went to the job at Sidney-square, and saw there were no men—I looked into the cask of cement, and found there was about a peck gone—there was no cement used there that day—there were two casks there.
EDWARD HOLSWELL . I was working at this house—I remember Leach and Stokes coming before breakfast—after breakfast Leach said he was going to take some cement to another job—I lent him a hand with the cask into the truck—Stokes was there, and lent a hand—he went on the scaffold again to work—I did not see the truck go away.
JOHN IRVIN . My father keeps the Tom and Jerry. About half-past two o'clock on this day I served Nicholas with three pots of ale—he sat on one side of the fire-place, and Leach and Stokes on the other—Nicholas drank, and passed it round to them and two or three other bricklayers—Nicholas was at work at our house all day, from about eight o'clock till half-past two, when the policeman took him.
THOMAS SQUIRES ( police-constable K 14.) Between four and five o'clock Leach was given to me for stealing a cask of cement—I said, "You hear what they say, you need not answer any questions if you don't think proper"—he said, "It is all right, it is at Nicholas's for three pots of beer"—I went to the beer-shop, and took Stokes—he said he knew nothing about it—I took them to Nicholas's house, and in the back yard I found this empty cask—I returned to the house again, and under tha stairs found a sack full of cement—I went to the beer-shop, and found Nicholas there—I said, "I want you on suspicion of receiving a cask of cement from Leach and Stokes; you need not say anything if you don't think proper"—he then stated that he took a bushel and a half of cement to his job at the beer-shop—I said, "Have you any cement at home?"—he said, "Yes, I have"—I said, "How much?"—he said, "I can't say"—I said, "Have you a bushel?"—he said, "Yes, two bushels, and I generally keep it under the stairs"—I said, "You have a cask full of cement under the stairs now"—he said, "No," if he had some one must have put it there—I did not find any cement at the beer-shop.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18430612-1942]
1942. JOHN BRADY and PATRICK HARRINGTON were indicited for stealing, on the 1st of June, 240 pence, the monies of John Newton; and that Brady had been before convicted of felony.
MR. WILDE conducted the Prosecution.
JOHN NEWTON . I am a chandler, and live in Buckeridge-street, St. Giles's. On the 1st of June the prisoners came into my shop—Brady bought a pennyworth of cheese, and a halfpennyworth of bread—Harrington bought a farthing pipe—I had 2 l. worth of copper in 5 s. packets behind a counter—Harrington walked up behind the side of the counter, and went out with something—I came out of my room, and missed 1 l. worth of copper—Harrington walked out of the shop, and handed something to Brady, who was at the door—I then went to the door—Brady stopped in the passage, then turned round, and faced me—I called out that I knew them both, and it would do them no good—at the instant part of the money fell from Brade.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. How long after they had been to buy the bread and cheese did you see Harrington in the shop? A. Not above ten minutes—I was in the room behind the shop—I saw Brady standing at the door—I swear there was nothing between me and him to prevent my seeing him—I did not see his face after the money was given him—I did when he came into the shop, and at the door—I am sure of that.
JOHN ROBERTS . I am a waiter. Between five and six o'clock in the morning of the 1st of June I saw the prisoners near the prosecutor's shop—about ten minutes after I heard some pence rattle on the pavement—the person who dropped them was three or four doors from the prosecutor's—I do not know who it was.
Cross-examined. Q. Is this a house which keeps open part of the night? A. Yes—it is in the Rookery, St. Giles's.
THOMAS KEYS ( police-constable E 153.) On the 1st of June I was standing at the station-house door, St. Giles's—I saw both the prisoners in Buckeridge-street—they were running—Brady was carrying something which appeared heavy, with his two hands to his left side, and Harrington was running in the rear of him—in about ten minutes I heard that the prosecutor had been robbed of a 1 l. worth of copper.
Cross-examined. Q. What is the prosecutor? A. A general dealer—he keeps open till one or two o'clock in the morning, and opens again about five.
CHARLES BROWN ( police-constable F 128.) On the 1st of June I took Brady, and another officer took Harrington—I was present before the Magistrate—Brady said he picked up 5 d. in halfpence.
Harrington's Defence. I went about six o'clock to get my mother's breakfast, and about nine I went to call my mother—the policeman took me—I know nothing of this.
ALEXANDER KERR ( police-constable F 49.) I produce a certificate of Brady's former conviction, which I got from Mr. Clark's office—( read)—he is the person.
Cross-examined. Q. How long is that ago? A. Two years and eight months.
BRADY — GUILTY . Aged 19.— Transported for Ten Years.
HARRINGTON — GUILTY . Aged 18.— Transported for Seven Years.

1943. SARAH WEBB and WILLIAM SMITH were indicted for stealing, on the 11th of May, 2 coats, value 25 s.; 2 waistcoats, 11 s.; 2 pairs of trowsers, 20 s.; and 1 handkerchief, 3 s.; the goods of Alexander Webb; to which
WEBB pleaded GUILTY . Aged 18.— Confined Four Months; the last week in each Month Solitary.
ALEXANDER WEBB . I am a labourer, and live in New Inn-square, Shoreditch—on the 11th of May I placed a box under the bed, in my bed room—next day I went to get a waistcoat, and missed my clothes—the door of the room was not locked—I gave information, and on the Saturday I met Smith in Shoreditch with the handkerchief round his neck—it is marked—Webb is my daughter—she was at my house on the evening of the robbery—I did not see her again till she was taken.
LOUISA PALMER . I am going on for nine years old—on Thursday, the 11th of May, I saw the prisoner Webb come out of her father's house and two men with her—I never saw the men before—they all ran down the court—one of the men was the same size as Smith.
CHARLES HOGDEN . I saw Webb that night, about a quarter to nine o'clock, with two young lads—Gill, who has been tried and acquitted, was one of them.

[trialAccount t18430612-1944]
1944. JANE YOUNG was indicted for stealing, on the 3rd of June, 1 jacket, value 5 s.; and 2 s.; the property of Jacob Santner, from his person.
JACOB SANTNER . I am a sailor, and lodge in Palmer's Folly—on the 3rd of June, about eleven o'clock at night, I fell in with the prisoner, and she took me to No. 3, Bluegate-place, Bluegate-fields—she asked me for 1 s., which I gave her, and she went out to get something to drink—I fell asleep, and did not awake till two in the morning—my jacket, which I had on, was then gone—I went to look for a policeman and could not find one—I came back and turned in again—when I awoke I got a policeman and found the
prisoner it the Bell public-house.—she said she did not care.—this it my jacket.
Prisoner. Q. If your jacket was on how could I take it off? A. I do not know, I was the worse for grog.
DAVID POWELL . I am a pawnbroker, I produce this jacket.—the prisoner brought it to pawn in the morning, on the 3d of June.
Prisoner. Q. Did I not say it belonged to a young man, at Mrs. Taylor's, who gave it me to pawn? A. Yes
JACOB SANTNER re-examined. Mrs. Taylor is my mother—I did not tell the prisoner to pawn this jacket—I did not know it was pawned—I expected to have it on when I awoke—I live close to the prisoner.
ROBERT FROST SMITH ( police-constable K 277.) I took the prisoner, and asked where the jacket was—she said she had pawned it, and he could not hurt her, as she had only taken 1 s. 3 1/2 d. out of his pocket.
Prisoner. He acknowledged that he gave me 1 s. 3 1/2 d., and the next morning he looked through the window, and there were other individuals who had parted with their jackets in the same way; he laughed and said he had done the same as others did.
JOHN GEARY . I am a general dealer, and live at No. 3, Bluegate-place. I was up next morning between five and six o'clock, and saw the prisoner and the prosecutor looking out of window and laughing at persons who had gone by without their jackets—it is a common thing in that neighbourhood if a sailor does not pay the female, that she takes his jacket and pledges it till the next morning.
MARGARET GEARY . I am the wife of the last witness. We live in the house the prisoner does.—on the 3rd of June she came in with a young man who was in liquor; he sat down on a chair; she asked if he would give her a pint of ale, he said yes—he gave me 1 s.—I brought in a supper and a pot of ale—they then went up stairs, and she called me up to help to undress him—I untied the handkerchief from his neck—his jacket was off at that time—about half-past two o'clock the prosecutor came down, accusing her of robbing him of 2 sovereigns—he went out, and could not find an officer—in the morning he got up about half-past seven, and he said "Will you give me a drop of grog?"—she said, "Not till I have pawned the jacket"—he said, "Then I will pin you"—my husband was out at the door.—I saw three or four men without jackets, and the prosecutor looked out at the window and said he had lost his jacket.
JACOB SANTNER re-examined. I did not go down and accuse her of robbing me of two sovereigns—I had no gold.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18430612-1945]

1946. JANE EVES was indicted for stealing, on the 7th of June, 2 breast-pins and chain, value 4 l., the goods of Charles John Willett, from his person.
CHARLES JOHN WILLETT . I am an auctioneer's clerk. On the 7th of June, I was walking down Vinegar-lane with a rose in my button-hole—the prisoner asked me to let her smell it—I took no notice—she came behind me, put her hand round my neck, and took the pins out of my scarf—I looked round, and could not see any one—I did not miss the pins till she was gone—I lost sight of her for half an hour.
WILLIAM BROOKS ( police-constable K 364.) I went with the prosecutor to Vinegar-lane, and saw the prisoner standing at the door—we went past her, and she called to the prosecutor, and said she had got his diamond pin, that she had tried to take the rose, and had taken the pin by mistake—the prosecutor said he would give her in charge—she ran into the house, and through into another house—I went and took her.
Prisoners Defence. I asked him to let me smell his rose; he would not; I put my arm round his neck, and the pin caught the sleeve of my gown; I said I could not run after him, but when he passed I would give it him.
NOT GUILTY .

1947. ANN CHILTON was indicted for stealing, on the 1st of June, 2 table cloths, value 2 s. 6 d.; 1 shift, 1 s. 6 d.; and 2 handkerchiefs, 2 s.; the goods of Margaret Collins; and that she had been previously convicted of felony.
MARGARET COLLINS . I live in St. George's-in-the-East. My servant was ill, and I employed the prisoner—I missed a sovereign, two tablecloths, two handkerchiefs, and a shift—these are them.
FREDERICK JOHN WOOD . I am a pawnbroker, I took in these two tablecloths of the prisoner on the 1st of June—she said she brought them for her sister—I have the other articles, but I do not remember whether I took them of the prisoner.

[trialAccount t18430612-1948]
1948. THOMAS FINN was indicted for stealing, on the 2nd of June, 6 yards of printed cotton, value 1 s. 6 d., the goods of Ellen Murphy; and 6 yards of printed cotton, 1 s. 6 d., the goods of Catherine Sullivan.
ELLEN MURPHY . I now live in King David Fort, Shadwell. Six or seven weeks ago I and Sullivan took a room at the prisoner's—on the night of the 2nd of June, between seven and eight o'clock, he came into our room very tipsy, and told us to be off out of his place—he broke down the line, and took two gown pieces, two shifts, and a pair of black stockings—he took them down stairs—next night I asked him if he would give me the things or the tickets of them—he had a candlestick in his hand, and he said, "If you don't be off, I will split your head open"—I gave him in charge—this gown piece is mine, and this other is Sullivan's—I owed the prisoner nothing—I owed his wife 2 1/2 d.
Prisoner. I took the gown-pieces out of her mother's hand, and stopped them for rent.
HENRY GOULD ( police-constable K 79.) I took the prisoner. I said it was for stealing two gown pieces—he said he brought them down stairs, and hung them across the line in the kitchen—next day his wife brought these two gown pieces to the Thames police.
CATHERINE SULLIVAN . I had this gown piece there—the prisoner came up stairs and told us to go out of the room—I went down and left him—I owed him nothing.
Prisoner. Between the mother and the daughter there was 9 s. 6 d. due.
NOT GUILTY .

1950. JOHN LACEY was indicted for stealing, on the 3rd of June, I saw, value 3 s. 6 d., the goods of Robert Nicholls.
ROBERT NICHOLS . I live in King's Head-court, Long Acre. About six o'clock on the 3rd of June, I was at tea—the prisoner came and asked the price of a tub—I told him, and he went away—in a quarter of an hour a little dog barked in the workshop—I went to see, and the prisoner was in the act of coming up stairs—I asked what he wanted—he said, "To go to the water-closet"—he came up—I saw a saw under his coat, and collared him—he struck at me—I sent for a policeman—it is my saw—I had used it about twenty minutes before.

1951. SARAH COX was indicted for stealing, on the 2nd of June, 1 breast-pin, value 8 s.; 1 scarf, 3 s. 6 d.; and 1 handkerchief, 3 s. 6 d.; the goods of John Squires, from his person.
JOHN SQUIRES . I live in Upper Grafton-street, Fitsroy-square. On the 2nd of June I had been drinking, and about a quarter to three o'clock in the morning, I sat down on a step in Green-street, Hampstead-road, to rest—I dozed off to sleep—in about ten minutes I was awoke by the prisoner drawing my scarf off my neck—I said, "Halloo, that is my scarf; where is the pin that was in it?"—she said, "I don't know"—she had my scarf in her hand—I said, "Where is my pin?"—she said, "I have not got it"—I said, "You must have taken it out before you could have taken the scarf off"—the policeman was near, and I gave her in charge—the pin was found in the area of the house where I was sitting.
JOSEPH HOPKINS ( police-Constable C 110.) I saw the prosecutor standing up—in taking the prisoner to the station, I gave the other officer one hand, and I took the other—the prisoner said, "Let me pin my shawl"—I let go of her hand, and she pulled this pocket-handkerchief from her bosom—I came back, and found the pin in the area where the prosecutor had been sitting.
JOHN BIRKINYRE ( police-constable E 152.) The prosecutor was standing, leaning his back against the door—the prisoner was standing by with his scarf in her hand—he did not appear ill, or as if he was drunk.
Prisoner's Defence. He was very sick, and his hat rolled on the pavement; I took it up and gave it him; his handkerchief fell out of his hat; I did not have the scarf.
NOT GUILTY .
