1952. ELLEN PALMER was indicted for stealing, on the 6th of June, 1 sovereign, 2 shillings, and 3 sixpences, the monies of Stephen Golding, from his person.
STEPHEN GOLDING . I am a sailor, lodging in Bow-lane, Poplar. At four o'clock on the 6th of June, I went into the Harrow, at Poplar—we had something to drink—I met the prisoner there, and went home with her about five to Red Lion-court—I had one sovereign, a half-crown, and some silver—I threw myself on the bed, and in less than three minutes the prisoner came in and deprived me of my money—I am certain it was her—I was not asleep—I made no disturbance, but after she went out, I went to sleep, and in the morning when I got up, I found my money was gone, and then I recollected that the prisoner took it.
Prisoner. Q. Did you not give me the money? A. No.
ELIZA MASTERSON . The prosecutor and the prisoner came to my house, and, about five minutes after the prisoner came down and gave me a half-crown to pay for the bed, and she had some other money in her hand—she went out—I said, "Don't go out and leave that man"—she said, "I shan't be long"—I never saw her any more.
JOHN ABBOTT . The prisoner came in front of my bar about five o'clock in the morning, and asked me to take care of the sovereign—she walked into the tap-room, and a short time after brought me 4 s., and asked me to put them with the sovereign—she called for a quartern of gin, and I did not see her again.
THOMAS SMITHERS ( police-sergeant K 3.) I found the prisoner concealed under the bedstead at her mother's—she said she knew nothing of the prosecutor or his money.
GUILTY . Aged 20.— Confined Three Months.

1954. GEORGE CANNON was indicted for stealing on the 17th of May, 9 yards of silk, Value 3 l. 5 s.; 1 couch-cover, 4 s.; 1 squab-cover, 1 s. 6 d.; 1 pillow-case, 1 s.; 1 stool-cover, 1 s.; 2 cushion-covers, 5 s.; and 6 yards of merino, 2 s. 6 d.; the goods of Elizabeth Wells, his mistress; and that he had been before convicted of felony.
ELIZABETH WELLS . I am a widow, and am a calico-glazer—I live in William-street, Curtain-road. On the 17th of May the prisoner came as my errand-boy—I had no character with him—I sent him with nine yards of calico and these other things to the Cross Keys, St. John-street—he was to book them—he came back in a short time, and went away, and did not return—I have not heard anything about the things—they were to have gone to Mr. Stracey, a dyer and scourer, at St. Alban's—the prisoner did not give any notice about his leaving.
WILLIAM HAWKINS . I am a book-keeper at the Cross Keys, St. John-street. On the 17th of May the prisoner did not come there—he did not bring any parcel for Mr. Stracey—I am quite sure of it.
THOMAS FOWLER ( City police-constable, No. 138.) I took the prisoner at
the bottom of Golden-lane—at the station he said he carried the bundle to the booking-office, and gave it to a man in the yard, or the gateway, that the man pulled a book out of his pocket, and marked it with a pencil.
Prisoner's Defence. I did so, and the man asked if I had a book or a bit of paper, that he might write upon it; I said no; he said it couldl not go before half-an-hour; I gave him 2 d. to book it.
THOMAS HARRINGTON ( police-constable G 204.) I produce a certificate of the prisoner's former conviction, which I got from Mr. Clark's office—( read)—the prisoner is the person.
GUILTY . * Aged 28.— Transported for Seven Years.

[trialAccount t18430612-1956]
1956. THOMAS HARLOCK was indicted for stealing, on the 30th of May, 3 window-blinds, value 3 l.; the goods of James Richard Stedman, and fixed to a building.
JAMES JOHN STEDMAN . I live with my father, James Richard Stedman, in Mile End Old Town, Stepney. On Tuesday, the 30th of May, I went to an empty house of his opposite ours, which was under repair, and missed two short Venetian blinds, and one long one, from the parlour window—I was there before on the Saturday—they were then safe, and fixed with screws to the window.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. Have you any share in this house? A. No—I never saw the prisoner before—they were stone-coloured blinds.
JOHN SYMONS . I lodge at the World's end. On Friday night, the 30th of May, I was in the tap-room, between six and seven o'clock—the prisoner came in and asked for a man named Taylor—the reply was he was not there—he then went over to an empty house belonging to Mr. Stedman, which was under repair—I saw him go in at the front entrance, and come out at the back, in about half an hour, with the blinds under his arm, and a flannel jacket wrapped round them—I said, "Halloo, old chap "—he said, "I am waiting for the master"—I went and got my supper.
Cross-examined. Q. You are a coal-porter? A. Yes—I did not stop him, because I did not wish to trouble my head about it—when I went for my supper he was coming out at the back of the house.
HENRY COOK . I am a grave-digger of Stepney, and lodge at the World's-end. On the 30th of May I saw the prisoner enter the prosecutor's premises, after the men had done work—after that Symons told me something—I went to Mr. Porcas, and he sent his boy to Mr. Stedman's—I afterwards saw the prisoner on Mr. Stedman's premises—they are empty houses—I went in at a front entrance, with Mr. Porcas, and found the prisoner in one of the up-stairs rooms—I asked what business he had there—he said he was placed there by Mr. Searl, the master, to watch the premises—I asked if he was going to stop all night—he said he did not know that he should—I asked how the back gate came open—he said he did not know—he came down then directly, and we went out-he fastened the door-I went to the house again Mr. Stedman, and searched round the premises with a light, and there was no one there—the prisoner could not have got out of the back entrance—he must have come out at the front.
Cross-examined. Q. He gave some abusive language, did not he? A.
Yes—he asked what business I had there—he fastened us out with a padlock—I did not see any blinds there.
ABRAHAM EARY ( police-constable K 355.) On this Tuesday evening, about nine o'clock, I went with Stedman to the house opposite the World's-end—the blinds were wrenched from the parlour window—I apprehended the prisoner on the following Thursday—I said the party wanted to see him on account of some blinds at Mr. Stedman's premises—he said he knew nothing about them, and then afterwards said that Taylor led him into the hobble.
FREDERICK WILLIAM SERLE . I am a builder. I was making the alterstions in the house opposite the World's-end—the prisoner worked for me on the Friday, and was discharged on Saturday night—he was a daily workman, at 5 s. a day—he came on the Monday, and asked for a job—I said I did not want him—he had no business there on the Tuesday.
Cross-examined. Q. Was a man named Taylor at work with him? A. yes.
(The prisoner received a good character.)
GUILTY . Aged 35.— Confined Two Months.
[trialAccount t18430612-1957]
1957. FREDERICK LUTMAN BRIDGEWATER was indicted for stealing, on the 30th of May, 1 coat, value 1 l.; 1 ring, 2 s.; and 8 yards of velvet, 8 s.; the goods of John Price.
JOHN PRICE . I live in Great North-street, Lisson-grove, and am a grocer. The prisoner lived with me—on Tuesday, the 30th of May, I was out, and returned at three o'clock in the afternoon—I had not seen the prisoner when I went, nor when I came back—he is my son-in-law, and his mother, who was my wife, was then on her death-bed—on my return home she gave me some account, and on the Friday afterwards she died—after her death I looked in a drawer, and missed a coat—on the Saturday the prisoner came to my house—I said he had taken the coat—he said, "Oh, it is very easy to get that back again, that was pawned for 1 s. 6 d."—he gave me the duplicate, and said he had got two others—I said, "You have not taken anything else, have you?"—he said, "Yes, a piece of velvet and a ring"—I said, "You are a bad young man, I shall never forgive you"—he brought me the tickets on Sunday morning, and left them on the mantel-piece—I redeemed the coat—I have not redeemed the other things.
Cross-examined by MR. HORRY. Q. Was not his answer that he had not pawned the coat for much? A. He said so—I am not in business now, I am in a private lodging—I dined with the prisoner on the Monday, in Moorgate-street, with a friend—it was on Sunday he brought the duplicates—I told him I should prosecute him—about seven o'clock on the Tuesday morning he came drunk in a cab, and knocked at my door—the cab -man gave him in charge for not paying his fare—the prisoner has had some money left him lately—Mr. Shadwell, of Gray's Inn, is one of the executors, and he is the person to pay that money—I did not send a person to Mr. Shadwell to get 30 l., which was part of the money owing to the prisoner—I know 30 l. has been paid on account—I did not send a person to Mr. Shadwell with the prisoner's consent to get 30 l. of that money—I mean to swear I did not receive 30 l.—I never received a farthing—I had 10 l. from the prisoner's friend—I know nothing about its being a portion of the 30 l.—I thought he lent me the money—I believe I owe the man the 10 l.—the 10 l. came from the 30 l.—the prisoner had 5 l., that was all he had of the 30 l.—there is a balance due to him from Mr. Shadwell, I know—I have been to see the prisoner since he has been committed—I saw him on the Saturday—I did not want him to send me to Mr. Shadwell for the rest of the money—the prisoner wrote me a letter that he
wanted to see me—I do not know when my coat was pawned—I had to pay a halfpenny interest for it.
Q. Upon your oath, if you had received an assignment from the prisoner of the money due to him, should you have prosecuted this case? A. Yes, just the same—I told him I should prosecute him—I did not give him in charge before the Tuesday, because I was waiting till after the mother's funeral—she was not buried till the Sunday week; but it was on the Sunday after her death I had the conversation with the prisoner—I did not give him in custody on that Sunday, because I was in so much trouble—I will swear I had not been asking him to execute an assignment of that property—I dined with him and his friend in Moorgate-street on the Monday—his friend had received a cheque for 30 l. from Mr. Shad well—I do not know whether the dinner was paid for out of that money—the prisoner had 5 l. of it—it was an understanding with his friend that he would procure him a situation for 25 l.—I do not know that the prisoner preferred trusting that friend to me.
CHARLES BALL . I live with Mr. Trail, a pawnbroker. I produce a gold ring, pawned by the prisoner on the 30th of May, for 1 s., in the name of James Lawrence—I produce some cotton velvet which was not pawned by the prisoner—I gave this duplicate for it.
( Property produced and sworn to)
EDWARD HOWLAND ( police-constable S 136.) I was present when the inspector read the charge to the prisoner at the station—the prosecutor was present—the prisoner said, "I do not think he will appear against me."
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18430612-1958]
1958. JANE VINCENT was indicted for stealing, on the 16th of June, 1 jacket, value 5 s.; and 1 handkerchief, 1 s.; the goods of George Todd.
GEORGE TODD . I am the carpenter of the brig Britannia, lying in Bugby Hole. On the night of the 15th of June I met the prisoner in Ratcliff Highway, and went home with her between ten and eleven o'clock—I gave her 3 s. and went to bed—I put my jacket, and trowsers, and waistcoat on the floor—I had a pocket-handkerchief in my pocket—I awoke about two in the morning—I got up and said to the prisoner, "Where is my jacket?"—she said, "It is all right"—I said, "You had better give it me now"—she refused—I came out and gave her is charge—the officer searched another apartment and found my jacket—this is it—I have not found my handkerchief.
Prisoner. Q. Did you not leave the jacket with me for 5 s.? You had been fighting with two men, and asked me to look if your head was cut, and it was. Witness. It is not true that I had no money—I asked her for my jacket—she said she would split my head with a weight—I did not ask her to look at my head.
GEORGE HAILES ( police-constable K 54.) I was called by the prosecutor. I went to a house in Twine-court, where I found the prisoner on a bed on the floor-there was no other furniture in the room—she said the prosecutor had given her the jacket to sleep with her, and she would see him b----before she would give it him—I went to another house which I knew the prisoner had, and found the jacket there.
Prisoner's Defence. He said he would give me a pint of half-and-half; we went to the Albion, and he had not a farthing to pay for it; he said he would leave me his jacket till the morning; I said, "You have got no neck handkerchief on;" he said, "No, I have been fighting with two men;" I said, "Don't say I have robbed you;" he said, "No." He wanted me to take the waistcoat and leave the jacket, and I would not; he got in a passion,
and said he would put me in the watchhouse; I never had a farthing of his money.
GUILTY . * Aged 27.— Confined Six Months.
[trialAccount t18430612-1959]
1959. MURTY DUGGAN was indicted for stealing, on the 7th of June, 2 ounces of tobacco, value 8 d.; 2 shillings; 3 sixpences; and 1 groat; the property of George Wellman.
GEORGE WELLMAN . I keep an oil-shop in Coram-street, Bloomsbury. On the 7th of June, about four o'clock in the afternoon, I went over the way for three or four minutes—a boy called me back, and I saw the prisoner coming out of my shop—I asked him what he wanted—he said, "Half an ounce of tobacco"—I served him, and he paid for it—as he was going out I looked in the till, and found all the silver was gone but a sixpence—I had had 4 s. 4 d. in it before I left—I went and caught him just as he was going into his own house, which is two doors from mine—he walked back with me to the shop—he asked me how much silver he had taken from the till—I told him—he produced this tobacco first, and then the silver, and said that was all he had of mine—he hoped I should do nothing with him—I gave him into custody.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. You had let him go at first? A. Yes; I took the money and tobacco from him, and then took him in the room—I had seen him go past—he gave me two shillings, three sixpences, and a fourpenny-piece, and the tobacco—that was all that I am aware that I had lost.
JAMES COBBINS ( police-constable E 79.) I took the prisoner—he said he had given him back what he had taken, and he did not think he could hurt him then—he was the worse for liquor.
GUILTY . Aged 26.— Confined Three Months.
[trialAccount t18430612-1960]
1960. GEORGE CAMPION was indicted for stealing, on the 29th of May, 1 coat, value 10 s.; the goods of Thomas Duggan.
THOMAS DUGGAN . I am the master of a schooner. On the 29th of May I came to London on business—I saw the prisoner that afternoon near London-bridge, with a boat, and I engaged him as a waterman to take me on board my schooner—I got in his boat to look for the vessel—we could not find it, and he rowed me back—I asked what he would take me to Greenwich for—he said 3 s.—we then went to have a pint of beer, and I thought I would stop till my vessel passed—he said he would wait no longer, and I paid him the 3 s.—he then said I might as well give him the preference to put me on board my vessel when it came—the vessel did come, and he put me on it—I told him to come up, and I would settle with him—he would not, but he pulled off with my coat in his boat—on the Friday following I saw him in a public-house—I asked what he had done with my coat—he said he had pawned it—I said he had put himself to a great deal of trouble, when he might have had his money at first—I gave him in charge the next morning—this is my coat.
Cross-examined by MR. PRENDERGAST. Q. You went to the stairs where you knew him to be, on the Friday, did you not? A. Yes, to the same public-house—the boy said he told him he had my coat—I was not drunk—I had left the corn-market at half-past three o'clock, and I first saw the prisoner about half-past four or five in the afternoon, and it was about eleven o'clock when I got on board my vessel—I was at the public-house till the vesssel came up—when I got to the public-house on the Friday, some one went for the prisoner—he came to the house to me—I do not think he showed me the
duplicate of the coat—I wanted to get my coat from him—we had two or three pints of beer together.
JOSEPH JOHN LEWIS . I am an inspector of the Thames police. I went with the prosecutor, on the 3rd of June, to Limehouse, and apprehended the prisoner I asked what he had done with the coat—he said he had pawned it at Mr. Dicker's, in Three-colt-street, Limehouse—I went with him, and found it he said he was very sorry for what he had done—he hoped the prosecutor would look over it.
Cross-examined. Q. He made no secret of it? A. No—the coat is worth a great deal more than 1 s.
THOMAS DICKER . I am a pawnbroker. This coat was pawned for 1 s. on the 31st of May.
MR. PRENDERGAST called
STEPHEN WILLIAM KITCHERN . I live in Lamb's-buildings, Limehouse. I saw the prosecutor about eleven o'clock at night on the 29th of May, when he was about to take water—he and the prisoner staggered down the shore, and the captain tumbled over a boat—I was about to pick him up, but he got up, and they went off—I saw the prisoner come back, but I did not go to his boat—he is regularly at those stairs—on the following morning I heard from two or three, who were standing at the stairs, that the captain had left a coat in the prisoner's boat, and if I saw the captain I was to remind him of it—I saw the captain on the Friday—he did not appeal to me, but he sent a person I knew very well to find the prisoner.
RICHARD EDWARDS . I am pot-boy at this public-house at Limehouse-hole-stairs—the prosecutor and prisoner were drinking there on the Monday—they had six pints a-piece as near as I can tell—on the Tuesday morning the prisoner came and had a pint of beer—he said, "I put the captain on board, and he would not pay me; if you should see him, tell him I have got his coat safe enough"—on the Friday evening the captain came with another man, who went out, and brought in some waterman, and said, "Captain, is this the man who has got your coat?"—the captain said, "No, it was an older man"—I then said I saw Campion, and he said he had the coat—the man that was with the captain got up, and says, "He has, has he? do you know where he lives?"—I said no—a man named Sykes said, "I know where he lives," and the captain said, "I will give you 6 d. to fetch him," which he did—they stopped together till about eleven at night, and had some pots of beer.
STEPHEN WILLIAM KITCHERN re-examined. Q. When the captain sent for the prisoner, did you remain with them? A. I did—the prisoner said, "Well, captain, I suppose you want your coat?"—the captain said, "Yes, I do, have you got it?"—the prisoner said, "Yes, it is all safe, captain, but I have been obliged to pawn it, to get my children bread; I have six at home, and no money to buy bread, but if you give me 1 s., I will get it out, and bring it you to-morrow morning, if you will tell me where the ship is"—the captain said he had no small change, and the cabman who was with him said, "Shall I pay it?"—the captain said, "Yes," and the cabman gave him 1 s., took the ticket, and put it into his pocket.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18430612-1961]
1961. GEORGE DRANE was indicted for stealing, on the 20th of May, 3 trusses of straw, value 3 s. 6 d.; the goods of John Farrow, his master.
JOHN FARROW . I live in Red Lion-street, Spitalfields. I have a farm at Barking, in Essex—the prisoner has been in my employ nine or ten years—on Saturday morning, the 20th of May, I met him on the road, four or five
miles from London—he had a load and half of straw—my son, who lives on my farm, had ordered him to bring some straw to London—he should have had fifty-four trusses—it looked a very handsome bright load, but I thought it was bigger than it ought to be—I then went on to my barn, and thought there was more gone than there ought to be—on the morning of the 24th of May I saw some trusses of straw at the police-yard—it was mine—it was very handsome and bright, and not so long as some persons is—it was white wheat straw, and looked as if it had been bleached with brimstone—I looked for some of the ears of corn in it, and they exactly corresponded with what had been threshed out at my barn—I have no difficulty in swearing that it belonged to me—I have been used to the farming business—I had noticed every load on the road, and there was no other corresponded with that.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. When you were before the Magistrate you only swore to the best of your belief? A. To the best of my belief, it is mine, and I am positive of it—I do not say that because I think it will make the case stronger—my son is not here—I discharged a man named Lazarus, who used to carry things from my barn.
EDWARD MENCE . I am a cheesemonger, and live in Sidney-place, Commercial-road. On the 20th of May, between ten and eleven o'clock in the morning, the prisoner was on the road with a cart and two horses—I was up stairs, and was called down by my wife—I looked out at the window, and saw the prisoner in the act of unloading the straw, and there was another helping him—I afterwards came down, and the cart was gone—I found three trusses of straw left at my place—they were afterwards delivered to the police-constable—they had not been touched, or done anything to, in the mean time—I had purchased straw of the prisoner before.
GEORGE MURRAY . I am a policeman. On the morning of the 20th of May, I saw the prisoner drive a cart with two horses from opposite Mr. Mence's door—I watched him—he went as far as the George—there was another cart just before him—he and the driver of that cart went into the George—I took the names that were on the carts—I afterwards found some straw at Mr. Mence's, and took it to the station—I afterwards apprehended the prisoner—he said the straw he sold to the cheeesemonger he had bought on the road, and he could bring twenty people to prove it.
NOT GUILTY .
NEW COURT.— Tuesday, June 20 th, 1843.
Sixth Jury, before Mr. Common Sergeant.

1962. JANE BILLINGTON was indicted for stealing, on the 1st of April, 3 blankets, value 6 s.; 2 quilts, 10 s. 6 d.; 1 pillow, 2 s. 6 d.; and 1 milk-pot, 3 d.; the goods of Lewis Chapman:— also, on the 8th of-April, 1 table-cloth, value 1 s.; 2 blankets, 4 s.; 2 quilts, 8 s.; 2 sheets, 3 s.; 2 pillows, 4 s.; 1 bolster, 4 s.; 1 piece of carpet, 1 s. 6 d.; one wine-glass, 3 d.; and 1 sugar-basin, 6 d.; the goods of George Tomlin:— also, on the 31st of Jan., 1 pair of sheets, value 2 s.; 1 pair of pillow cases, 1 s.; 5 blankets, 10 s.; 1 bolster, 7 s.; 2 pillows, 4 s.; 2 quilts, 4 s. 6 d.; 1 carpet, 3 s.; 1 coffee-pot, 6 d.; 1 tea-pot, 9 d.; 6 plates, 1 s.; 1 dish, 6 d.; 2 cups, 2 d.; 2 saucers, 2 d.; 1 basin, 4 d.; 3 mugs, 1 1/2 d.; 1 coal-box, 1 s. 6 d.; 1 candlestick, 9 d.; 2 chimney ornaments, 6 d.; 1 looking-glass and frame, 1 d. 6 d.; 3 spoons, 3 d.; 1 salt-cellar, 4 d.; 1 pepper-box, 4 d.; and 1 mustard-pot, 4 d.; the goods of Thomas Ruck:— also, on the 22nd of April, 3 blankets, 15 s.; 2 sheets, value 4 s.; 2 pillows, 10 s.; 2 pillow cases, 1 s.; 1 counterpane, 5 s.; and 1 table-cover, 3 s.; 1963. DANIEL LANE was indicted for embezzling 7 l. 18 s. 8 d.; 29 l.; 4 l. 7 s.; 1 l. 4 s. 6 d.; 1 l. 3 s. 9 d.; and 1 l. 10 s. 6 d.; the monies of John Groves and others; to which he pleaded
GUILTY .— Confined Three Months.
[trialAccount t18430612-1964]
1964. JAMES LOVELL was indicted for feloniously receiving, on the 24th of April, of a certain evil-disposed person, 905 yards of serge, value 100 l., the goods of William Benson Stones, well knowing the same to have been stolen; against the Statute, &c.
MESSRS. CLARKSON and PAYNE conducted the Prosecution.
WILLIAM BENSON STONES . I am a Manchester-warehouseman, and live in Warwick-street, Golden-square. On Saturday night, the 22nd of April, I went into the warehouse, and chained up the dog—I left the warehouse and the property in it perfectly safe—next morning I went again into the warehouse, and heard a knocking on the wall—the property appeared safe—about half-past seven o'clock on Monday morning I went into the warehouse again, to the spot where I had heard the knocking the previous day, and discovered a hole in the wall, that led into a coach-maker's next door—I then examined my warehouse, and missed 300 yards of silk serge and laventines, worth about 424 l.
Cross-examined by MR. WILKINS. Q. Have you any partners? A. No—I heard the knocking between eleven and twelve o'clock—I am quite clear as to that—there is nothing to separate my premises from the coach-maker's but this wall, where I heard the knocking.
JOHN HIBBARD . I am a labourer in the employ of Mr. Kempster, a coach-maker, in Warwick-street. On Sunday, the 23rd of April, I was at my master's place from nine o'clock till a quarter to one—the premises were all safe then, and I left it double locked—at a quarter-past six next morning I went there, and found the factory door only once locked—I found a hole in Mr. Stone's wall, about three feet one way, and two the other, big enough for a man to go through.
Cross-examined. Q. Have you your hearing very well? A. No—I know I left at a quarter before one o'clock, because I looked at the public-house clock opposite, and the people at the Catholic chapel were leaving—the hole was in the down stairs room—if there had been any hole in the wall then I must have seen it—I was the whole of that time in the doorway, which leads immediately to the room where the hole was—I never saw the prisoner there.
NICHOLAS PEARCE . I am an inspector of the A division of police. On Wednesday afternoon, the 24th of May, I saw Dafton at the bottom of Holborn-hill—I followed him to the prisoner's house, in Goswell-street—he has a shop
for manufacturing fringe—there was a quantity of fringe in the shop, and tassels and other things in the window—Dafton went into the shop—he remained there twenty minutes, and came out with a roll of silk serge—I followed him into Bardwell and Co.'s shop, at the bottom of Holborn-hill—I went there too—I asked him what he had under his arm—he said, "Silk"—I told him who I was, and took the parcel from him—this is it—I left him in charge of a constable at Bardwell's—I then went to the prisoner's house—the prisoner was standing at the door—when I asked if his name was Lovell, he said "Yes"—I walked into the shop, and asked if he knew a man named Dafton—he said, "No"—I asked if he had sold any silk to a person of that name that day—he said, "No," he had not—I asked if he had sold any silk to any other person that day—he said, "No," he had not—I told him I was an officer, and I should search his house—he said, "You shall not do it without a warrant"—he said he did not know me, and he should send to the station for the inspector—I said, "I will send out for a constable"—I did so, and one presently came in—I was in plain clothes, and the constable did not know me—presently a second constable came in, mentioned my name, and told the prisoner who I was—I then began to search in a back room adjoining the shop—I found there four pieces of silk serge covered over with a piece of cloth—they were brought into the shop, and placed by the side of the counter—the prisoner said they belonged to another man—I said, "Perhaps you will tell me who that man is?"—he said, "I shall answer no questions, it is not your place to ask questions"—I made a further search in a box under the shop window, and found these forty-seven yards of brown silk—I found a coat in the back room, and in the pocket of it I found this pocket-book with the things which are now in it.
Cross-examined. Q. What did Dafton tell you he had given a yard for this silk? A. 1 s. 9 d.—I had not known the prisoner at all before—I perceived that he was a little hard of hearing—there was a counter in the shop, and the box was inside the counter—I did not see any shopwoman there.
JOHN HAYNES ( police-inspector, A division.) On the 25th of May I went to the prisoner's house—I found twenty-four patterns of silk serges behind a nest of drawers in the front shop, in a piece of very thin brown paper, among a quantity of rubbish, loose papers, and other things, crammed behind the drawers—on the day before when I was there with Pearce we found a large quantity of bitter aloes, nutmegs, isinglass, Prussian blue, silk drawers, cotton stockings, and boots, in different parts of the house—on the 25th I found twenty-four pairs of silk stockings among a quantity of rags under the counter.
Cross-examined. Q. When was the first time you gave this account about the nutmegs, and drawers, and stockings? A. To-day—I was a chemist and druggist before I was a policeman—I thought it a marvellous thing to find silk drawers and stockings in such a place.
MR. PAYNE. Q. Where did you find these silk drawers? A. Among a quantity of rags under the bed in the kitchen, and twenty-four pairs of cotton stockings were among some rubbish in a sort of basket under the counter—some of the silk drawers were in the different cupboards and corners about the parlour.
JOSEPH WADSWORTH . I am foreman to Mr. Stones. By his direction I made out a list of the stolen articles—I began it on the Monday morning, and finished it on the Tuesday, and on the Tuesday I sent the list down to the station in Vine-street.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you make it out from information from other parties? A. From deficiencies in the stock, but I went through the books myself, and the silks too—a man helped me to trace the different numbers—I
went through the whole of the silks—we had the stock-book, which is entered up by different parties, and I have no doubt it is correct—I act upon it—I made out the greater part of these entries, not all of them—all the parties in the warehouse are likely to enter them—they are not all here.
COURT. Q. Have you any mode of telling of your own knowledge the loss sustained that night? A. Yes—I know where they were placed—I went round the shop, and saw the places where property was missing—I did not know before that what property was there except from the stock-book—there were fifty-five ends and pieces missing—there are three persons in the employ besides me—I can say from my own knowledge without going through the book that we lost from forty to fifty pieces of silk serge, which were safe on Saturday night, and were gone on Monday morning—I have seen this piece of brown serge in custody of Pearce—it is Mr. Stone's—I saw this piece of drab serge in the possession of Pearce—it is Mr. Stone's—I have seen the other pieces produced by the policeman—we had pieces of silk of this description and pattern on Mr. Stone's premises—I believe all these pieces to be his.
Cross-examined. Q. How many pieces do you profess to identify? A. There are five pieces which I have gone through—the lengths correspond as nearly as possible with those we lost—I have no other mark on them—I only know them by the quantity and colour—this drab would be about 2 s. 3 d. a yard at a fair wholesale price—the retail price would be from half-a-crown to 2 s. 8 d.
SAMUEL DAFTON . ( A prisoner.) I was committed by the Magistrate upon this charge—I have known the prisoner about four years. About a month ago to-morrow, I met him in Aldersgate-street, near where he lives—after saying, "How do you do," he stopped, and said, "I think I have something that will suit you, as you are in the woollen line; I have got some silk serge that you might sell, they are used by the tailors and drapers; those who buy cloth will buy them; you might sell them when you are selling your cloth"—I said they were out of my line, I did not deal in them generally, but perhaps I might sell, as I was passing round, when I was out on my own business, if he would give me some patterns—he said would I call and look at them—I said, "Yes, when I came back from the City, or on the following morning"—I then left him—I called at his shop the following morning, and he showed me four or five pieces of silk serge in the parlour—he said these were the things that he had mentioned to me the day before—I asked the price, and he told me 2 s. 3 d.—I said, "If you like to cut me patterns I will take them with me, and see if I can sell them"—he cut me patterns from the end of each, and I took them with me—I asked him if they were cheap—he said, "Yes, very cheap"—I called at Messrs. Bardwells, and showed them the patterns—they would not give me the price I asked—I called on the prisoner as I came home in the evening—I told him I had shown them, but they were too high—then he said he would take 2 s. for them—he said they were very cheap—I said I did not know, but I thought by what they said, they were not cheap—I called at Messrs. Bardwells again, and they offered me 1 s. 11 1/2 d. a yard for them—I went back to the prisoner and told him I might sell them if he would take 1 s. 9 d.—that was for two pieces only—he said if I would get him the money he would take that for the two pieces—I said, "I can give you 1 s. 9 d. for these two," which were the two that Bard well had offered me 1 s. 11 1/2 d. for—one was a black, and the other a brown—he said he would take that price if I could sell him the lot he had there—I said I did not know whether I could or not—he showed me four or five pieces—I said, "If you will let me have these two at 1 s. 9 d. I will keep the patterns of the others which I have got, and endeavour to sell
them"—he said he would let me have the two at 1 s. 9 d., and I was to endeavour to sell the others—I gave him the money—I borrowed the money to pay for them, and then I took them to Bardwells and sold them to them—I called on the prisoner again, and gave him the patterns back of the other three, and said I could not sell them—he said, "Very likely if you look in in the morning I shall have two or three others of different colours"—I looked in on the following morning, and he had three or four pieces more—he said, "Here are some fresh ones, can you do anything with them?"—I said, "I don't know, but cut me patterns, I will try"—I said, "You have a good many of them"—he said, "No, they are not many, they are a few I have taken from a customer of mine, instead of money"—I called at Bardwells, and said, "Here are some fresh ones"—I sold them two more at 1 s. 10 1/2 d.—they said they thought they were not so good—I paid the prisoner 1 s. 9 d. for them—he wanted to know if I could not sell the others—I said, "I do not know, but if you let me keep the patterns, as I am about town, I will try and sell them"—he said, "I wish you could sell them, as I want the money for them, you will be down in the City to-morrow morning"—I said, "Very well"—on the following day but one I called and saw him—he then said he had two or three more—those I had the patterns of were gone—I said, "You have sold them?"—he said, "Yes, I believe they are gone"—I took out the patterns, and said, "They are of no use to me then"—he said he had another piece or two, and that was all—he said, "There is one here which is of a superior price to the others, I won't take less for this than 2 s. 3 d."—I said, "It does feel stouter, give me a pattern of it, and I will try to sell it"—he said, "I wish you would try to sell the whole of them"—I said, "Give me a pattern of that that seems to be better"—I took the patterns, and sold one to Mr. Lowe for 2 s. 1 d.—I had told Lovell before that the two last I had sold at 1 d. a yard less than the others, and I said, "Let me have that at the same price as the others"—he said, "Well, well, sell that, I don't think we shall be very nice about 1 d. a yard," and I paid him 1 s. 9 d.—there were two or three left—he said, "What do you say to these?"—I said, "I don't know, if I have the patterns, I will try to sell them"—I think they were different to what I had seen before—I had the patterns of one of them, and showed them to several persons—I could not sell them—I could not get above 1 s. 6 d. for them—I called on Bartram again, and sold them another for 1 s. 10 d.—I paid the prisoner 1 s. 9 d. for it—he said he had got four pieces more left—he said, "There are these left, they are all I have got"—I said, "I will see what I can do with the drab," and I took it directly to Bardwell, and showed it them—a man, who I did not know, came up to me, and said, "What have you got there?" and I told what it was.
Cross-examined. Q. Where do you live? A. In Brewer-street, St. John-street-road, opposite Sadler's-wells—I am a housekeeper—I have lived there six or seven years—I have no lodgers—I carry on the woollen business—I am a woollen agent—I have no warehouse, but I have a place in the City, at my sister's—if I have occasion to send goods, I send them there; and if I have goods from the country, I have them there—my sister keeps a boarding-house in Basinghall-street—it is in part of her house that I deposit my goods—in different rooms, generally on the landing, and latterly in a little room behind, which they generally call my warehouse—it is a room where I frequently put things—I have not deposited any goods there for some time—it may be a month, or longer—I have little occasion for a warehouse—I have been in prison all this time on account of this silk—my son makes a quantity of fancy writing-desks, and sends them there for me to sell, and I have hundreds
of pounds worth of goods sent there—if my son has any desks on hand, I endeavour to sell them—I deal in nothing else but woollens—I have sold Isces for Mr. Hall, of Montague-street, Spitalfields, during the last twelve months—I have exchanged cloth with him for laces to the amount of 200 l. or 300 l.—I deal with two or three persons in the country for cloth—I deal with Maltby, of Leeds—I have sold many goods for him—I think he lives in Basinghall-street, Leeds—I direct to "William Maltby, of Leeds"—I deal with Mr. Shamacher, a wool-merchant, in Thames-street—I did business with him within a month before I was taken—I never sold any silk serge before I met the prisoner—I have sold silk—nine or ten months ago I had two or three remnants of silk—I bought them at a sale—I have not sold 10 l. worth of silk for these twenty years—I do not suppose I have bought 20 l. or 40 l. worth of silk in my life, or had three dealings in silk—I do not recollect having any dealings in silk for seven or eight years to my knowledge—when I went for the piece of drab silk I saw the prisoner at his house—that is true—I did not come into the shop and inquire for Mr. Lovell, and Mrs. Lovell did not tell me he was not at home—I do not recollect seeing her that morning—I did not take any silk into that shop, only the patterns—I do not know a gentleman of the name of Anderson—I did not show Mrs. Lovell the silks, and she did not ask me to leave them to show to her husband—I did not take into Mrs. Lovell's shop, on the 24th of May, a parcel in a coarse wrapper, and say I had a nice cheap lot of serge I should like to sell her—she did not say her husband was not at home—I did not say, "Give me leave to show them to you, they are worth the money," nor put the parcel on the counter, and say they were silk serges I could sell at 2 s. 3 d. a-yard—she did not say she would have nothing to do with them, I might call again when her husband was at home.
Q. Did she not say to you, after whispering with a gentleman, that she would give 2 s. 2 d. for the black, but would not have the colours, as they were of no use? A. No—I did not at first refuse, and afterwards agree to let her have them at 2 s. 2 d., nor ask for a pair of scissors to cut the samples, nor ask her to let me leave them for two or three hours, and that I would then let her know—I did not pull out a small paper parcel, and tell her to throw it into the fire, and say they were patterns of goods which I had sold, and they were of no use—she did not in my presence give them to the children—I saw a girl in the shop, and I think Mrs. Lovell was in the shop once or twice during the whole of the times I called there—she was not there on the day I had the drab silk—when I took this piece of drab silk I saw a girl in the shop, and I saw Mr. Lovell in the parlour—I stood in the shop and pulled out the patterns, and I said, "There, Mr. Lovell, what about this? I can sell this"—that was the drab one—he said, "Come in here"—I walked into the parlour behind the shop, and then this conversation took place in the parlour about the drab—I told Mr. Bardwell they had been taken in exchange for goods, not that I had taken them—I said, at the time of the examination, that that was a mistake—I did not tell Mr. Lowe that I had taken them in exchange for goods—I said they had been taken in exchange for goods—I did not tell either Bardwell or Lowe that I had taken them in exchange for goods—I have been engaged in the cloth business forty-five years out of forty-eight—I was apprenticed in the trade—I worked in it when I was between three and four years old—I had a warehouse in Basinghall-street from 1818 till 1822—I gave it up because business was bad—I failed, and took the benefit of the Act in 1825, I think—I have done so twice, but it was for the same thing—there was something left out the first time I took it—there were some goods that were sent in, and were omitted to be put down, and thus, after six
months, I had to go through the course again—I was opposed by a party who had sent in some goods which I objected to take—there they lay, and when I had taken the benefit of the Act the man sued me for these goods—he would not take them, and I gave them up to the Court—the man made himself assignee, and took those very goods—I have not been very intimate with the prisoner—I never had a quarter of an hour or ten minutes' chat with him in my life before this—I have known him about four years—during all that time he has lived and carried on business in the same place.
MR. PAYNE. Q. When you went from Mr. Bardwell's to the prisoner, on the day the policeman saw you, did you carry any parcel in? A. No; I came out with one containing this drab silk—I have never been charged with any criminal offence, or ever been in custody in my life.
GEORGE NELSON . I am porter to Mr. Stones—I know this piece of brown silk serge—I saw it on my master's premises on the 19th of April—there are my initials on it—I saw this piece of drab silk serge safe on the 19th of April—here are my fellow-servant's initials on it—these were a part of the stock that were missing when I examined the stock-book—from my knowledge of the business, I believe these other pieces produced are Mr. Stone's—he had serge of the same pattern and quality, and appearance, on his premises just before the robbery.
Cross-examined. Q. By what do you identify the two pieces? A. This one by my own initials—my initials were placed on this on the 25th of Nov., when these pieces were sent out by me and entered in the approbation-book to a gentleman named Dewey—I can say I saw them on the 19th of April, from an examination of the pattern-book, which it is my duty to do—I checked them off as being in stock—seven or eight persons are on the premises, and we all serve occasionally.
CHARLES LONG . I am shopman to Mr. Stones—I know this piece of drab silk serge—I put my initials on it on the 19th of April, when it was sent out to Mr. Rennells—it came back on the 21st, but I did not observe it then.
JOSEPH BARTRAM . I am a partner in the firm of Bardwell and Co., Holborn-bridge—I remember Dafton coming to me on the 20th of May—he brought three patterns of levantine and serge, two of which I bought of him—forty-eight yards of black serge, and eighty-eight yards of brown levantine, at 1 s. 11 1/2 d. per yard—on the 23rd of May I purchased of him ninetythree yards and three-quarters of black levantine, and fifty-three yards and three-quarters of brown levantine, at 1 s. 10 d.—they came to 13 l. 10 s. 5 d.—I paid him 13 l. 10 s. 4 d.—on the 24th I bought a length of black serge of him, under the direction of Inspector Pearce—(I had communicated with Mr. Stones, and had an interview with Mr. Pearce the night before)—I asked Dafton if he had any drab serge—he went away, and returned in about an hour, and brought this drab serge, which I had bought of him, under his arm—Mr. Pearce followed him into the shop, and took him there after I had bought the black serge of him, and he went away to fetch the drab—he had no parcel with him at all—he brought me patterns.
Cross-examined. Q. How much did you pay him altogether? A. I think 31 l. odd—I bought four different lots—he told me he had taken them in barter—I am sure of that.
MR. PAYNE. Q. Can you be certain whether he said he had taken them in barter, or that they had been taken? A. I understood him to say that he had taken them in barter—I would not swear positively to it, but that is my impression—I took it for granted that they were his own, saying he took them in barter.
NICHOLAS PEARCE re-examined. I followed Dafton from Mr. Bardwell's to Lovell's—he took no parcel in with him, but brought this one out.
SAMUEL LOWE . I am a tailor, and live in Barbican. On Tuesday, the 23rd of May, I bought some serge of Dafton, at 2 s. 1 d. a yard—I paid him 10 s. 6 d. for it
Cross-examined. Q. Did he tell you he had taken it in exchange for other goods? A. Yes, I am sure of that—he asked me 2 s. 3 d.—I offered him 2 s.—he said it cost him 2 s.—I then said, "I will give you a penny a yard profit"—he then showed me a pattern of brown serge, and asked me to purchase that—I said it did not suit me.
DAVID MITCHELL . I live in Cloth-fair, and deal in woollens. On the 22nd of May I bought two pieces of serge of the prisoner, consisting of 200 yards together—I paid him 20 l. for them, at 2 s. a yard—I have known him three or four years, and am acquainted with his writing—I have a bill of his hand writing—I think I have never seen him write—he has sometimes made out bills at our warehouse, and sometimes sent them—I have acted with him in business on those bills which he has written at my warehouse—I settled with him on the faith of this writing—it is not the writing of any one in my employment—the prisoner's signature is put to this bill of parcels, and to all of them, and I have acted upon them.
Q. Look at this account, which was found in the pocket-book in the prisoner's room, and from the knowledge you have acquired by what you have told us, what is your opinion about it? A. I believe it is the prisoner's writing—the figures on this other paper I should not believe to be his—this other paper looks more like his writing—if this had come to me from him, I should have thought it was his writing.
Cross-examined. Q. Suppose you had seen this, if it had been lying on my desk, could you have expressed any belief about it? A. I then could not have said—if I had seen it at the other end of the town, I should not have expressed my opinion that it was the prisoner's writing—I know he can write—I saw him take the pen and write, but I did not stand over him to see him mark a letter—I thought him a respectable man—I was introduced to him by a person named Dafton, who had shown us some samples of woollen goods—I never dealt with Dafton for silks—the prisoner did not tell me the silks were selling for another person—I bought them in our warehouse—the prisoner brought samples first, and there was some dispute about the price—I am not quite certain whether he said that before he could part with them at any price he must consult another person—he said he did not think the price could be taken.
GEORGE THOMAS . I believe these pieces of silk, produced by Mr. Mitchell, are what I sold to Mr. Stones on the 7th of April—I sold him two lengths then, and they are the same quality and pattern, and every thing the same.
Cross-examined. Q. I believe you are clerk to your brother, who lives in Neville's-court, Milk-street? A. Yes—he is in a large way of business—there were but two pieces of this description in our warehouse—very likely there are some thousands of yards—I know this by the selvedge, and by the character of the piece—the manufacturer had about eighteen pieces of it—I know this piece so well, it is the fellow-piece to what Mr. Lowe has.
WILLIAM BENSON STONES re-examined. I have looked at all this property—I had property of this description, in pattern and quality, on my premises at the time of the robbery, and missed property of this description immediately after—I believe this is mine, and was on my premises at the time of the robbery.
HENRY FRY . I am in the employ of Mr. Stones. I am acquainted with
his stock generally—it strikes me that I have seen this one produced by Mr. Mitchell before—this piece of drab I can identify as his—I saw it on his premises on the 21st of April.
JAMES WADSWORTH . Those patterns that were found in the prisoner's house I believe to be patterns of part of the stock lost by Mr. Stones—I have compared them with the articles—they correspond.
MR. WILKINS called
THOMAS ANDERSON . I live in Victoria-row, Pimlico. I keep a shop and sell Berlin wool, and am in the silk, woollen, and trimming trade—I sell fancy goods in general—I have an agreement for a lease of my premises—I pay 116 l. a-year rent—I have known the prisoner between sixteen and seventeen years, and have been in the habit of dealing with him—on the 24th of May, between eleven and twelve in the forenoon, I went for an order which I had given to be done by twelve o'clock—some gimp fringe and tassels, and buttons, and sleeve-ties—it was not completed, and I waited in consequence of it—I saw a person come in to the prisoners with a bundle under his arm—I saw the bundle opened, and it contained silk serge—there were two colours and black—one of the colours was a kind of drab, but I did not look at the coloured ones at all—the others were black—Mrs. Lovell was present when I went into the shop, and the person who came asked her whether Mr. Lovell was at home—she said, "No, he is not"—he said he wanted to see him, as he had got a job of serges which he could sell cheap—he could sell them at 2 s. 3 d. a-yard, if she would take the whole—she said she did not want any—I saw the colours at a distance, but the black I looked at and handled, and I felt disposed to make a purchase of them—I nodded to Mrs. Lovell and went towards the parlour—she followed me—I told her if she would buy the black, I would not mind giving 2 s. 3 d. a-yard for them—I did not want the coloured, it was no use to me—the whole six pieces of serge were then left—he had asked 2 s. 3 d. a-yard for them—Mrs. Lovell offered him 2 s. 2 d. for the black, after what I had told her, and he said he would take it—he asked for scissors and cut some samples off, but whether he cut from them all I cannot say, but I know he cut some from both the colours, and from the black—after he had cut off these patterns, he said he did not know whether he could part them, but if she would allow him to leave them there, he would let her know in an hour or two whether he could sell the black without the colours—Dafton has very much the appearance of the man, but I could not say positively that it is him, my eye-sight is very bad—to the best of my belief he is the man—I used my glasses to look at the serge—after the patterns were cut off, the man gave Mrs. Lovell something wrapped up in a piece of brown paper—I do not know what it was—he said they were some patterns of goods that had been sold and done with—they were of no use to him, and Mrs. Lovell might put them behind the fire—Mrs. Lovell's little child, who was behind the counter, said, "Mother, give it me for my doll"—I did not see her give the bundle to the child—it was on the counter—the man then went away—the shopwoman was in the shop at the time—Mrs. Lovell took up these pieces of serge, and put them on one side at the further end of the counter—they were there when I left.
MR. PAYNE. Q. How long have you kept your shop? A. Six months—before that I kept the Black Horse, at the corner of Black Horse-yard, in the Dover-road—I kept that about two years—it is twelve months ago—I left it because I was burnt out—between that time, and the last six months, I was out of business entirely—I had been in the public line fourteen or fifteen years—I kept the Rum Puncheon, in Old-street—about eighteen
months—before that I lived in Southampton-street, Fitzroy-square, for five years with my son, who keeps an oil shop—I was then dealing in silks and trimmings of all kinds, and serges occasionally—I did not get acquainted with Lovell by selling serges, but by his doing work for me sixteen years ago—when I was at my son's, I had a warehouse on the first floor—I had been nine years at Chelsea, and went from there to Southampton-street—I kept the Pitt's Head, in the Grange-road, for eleven months, twenty years ago—I have never been anything else—I attend sales by auction—I never had anything to do with the law—it was between eleven and twelve o'clock in the morning that the man came to the prisoner's—I know it was not twelve, because I got the goods I was waiting for, and in coming across Clerkenwell-green, it struck twelve—Mrs. Lovell was behind the counter, and the man stood in front—the shopwoman was behind the counter, and backwards and forwards to a table—the man had a hat on—he did not take it off at all—I had my hat on—I believe I did not take it off—I was sitting on a leather seated chair just by the window—it was not an arm chair—Mrs. Lovell asked me to sit down as I was waiting for the work—the serges that the man brought were wrapped up in folds—I should say they were worth from 2 s. 6 d. to 2 s. 9 d. a-yard—there were six pieces, and I bid money for four—perhaps the six pieces might be worth 60 l.—I do not know—I did not buy them because they were offered to Mrs. Lovell—I did not want to take them from her—she told him he must call again when her husband came in—the prisoner is a fringe and trimming manufacturer—I have sold him silks and worsteds—I went before the Magistrate on the second time, and the solicitor told me he had no hearing, but was remanded for a third examination—I went up then, but did not offer myself, because the attorney spoke to the Magistrate—I did not tell him I could show where the prisoner got this, because the attorney said the Magistrate was determined to commit the prisoner—so I was merely like a bystander.
MR. WILKINS. Q. Was it in consequence of some information from the solicitor, or the prisoner that you went there? A. Yes—I was ready to give my evidence.
ELIZA THOMAS . I am the wife of James Thomas, and am shopwoman to the prisoner. I saw the last witness come to the prisoner's shop, on Wednesday the 24th of May—he was there about ten o'clock in the morning—while he was there Mr. Dafton came in, and asked if Mr. Lovell was at home—Mrs. Lovell said no—Dafton had a parcel in a coarse wrapper under his arm, and he said he had a nice cheap job lot of serges, that he should like to sell her—she said her husband was not at home, and she could say nothing to it—Dafton said, "Give me leave to show them to you"—he laid the parcel on the counter, and opened the silks—I could see that they were silks of the same sort as these—I did not hear him mention exactly the price—he said he would sell her them cheap—I saw Anderson whisper to Mrs. Lovell at the end of the counter, and then she told Dafton that if he liked to take 2 s. 2 d. for the black, she would buy them, but would not have the colours at any price—he did not say whether he would let her have them or not—he then asked for a pair of scissors, and they were handed over to him—he cut off some of the ends of the silks, then asked her to let him leave the silks there for two or three hours till he returned, and he would then let her know whether he could separate the black from the colours—he then produced a paper with some small pieces of silk in it—he said they were patterns of goods he had sold—they were of no use, and he asked her to burn them—she gave them to the child, and told her to burn them—Dafton then went away, and Mrs. Lovell put the silks on a shelf at the end of the counter—
after Anderson was gone, a little girl three years old, one of the children came in, and brought some pieces of silk, and in consequence of what she said I put the pieces of silk in a beaufet—Mr. Lovell came home about four o'clock—Dafton came back about four—he saw Mrs. Lovell, and told her he had sold the drab, and she might have the black at her price—she called Mr. Lovell—Dafton and him were together in conversation in the shop—Mr. Lovell said he must measure the silk—Dafton said he was going to take the drab, and he would return shortly—the drab was on the counter—he then wrapped it up, and took it away with him—Mr. Lovell put the blacks at the end of the counter, and Mrs. Lovell afterwards put them in the parlour as they were in the way—Dafton told me that he would leave the brown silk till he came back, and I put it into a box under the show board in the shop—I left between four and five o'clock, as I was ill, being near my confinement—on the solemn oath I have taken every thing I have said is true.
MR. PAYNE. Q. How often had you seen Dafton before? A. I have seen him several times in the shop talking with Mr. Lovell—he had been there a few days before—I do not know how often—I do not know whether I had seen him there several times within the course of four or five days before—I cannot say that he had not been there—I am there from nine o'clock till nine—I sometimes have my meals there, and at other times at home—my husband is a stone-mason—I do not know of any silk drawers being among some rags under a bed—I will not swear there were not some under a bed in the kitchen—I was not there when the officer searched the place—there are boxes with the children's clothes in under the bed in the kitchen—I could not swear there were not rags there—there were a few pairs of stockings there—I did not see twenty-four pairs—I will not swear they were not there—I never saw any aloes or nutmegs there.
MR. WILKINS. Q. You have been asked whether Dafton had been there several times in the course of a few days? A. I had not seen him many times—I have been engaged in this trade all my life—it is very common for a man to take hosiery in exchange for trimmings—I have known Mr. Lovell five or six years—during the whole time he has borne the character of a respectable shopkeeper.
COURT. Q. Where was Mrs. Lovell when Dafton came into the shop? A. Assisting me to get an order off—she was behind the counter, and so was I—Dafton was on the other side of the counter, and Anderson was on the other side of the counter, waiting for some work—he was sitting part of the time—I am not related to Lovell.
(Samuel Gogay, a silk and cotton bobbin seller; James Wingfield, a saddler, Long-lane; Thomas Crossley, warehouseman, Falcon-square; Edwin West, warehouseman, Fore-street; and Samuel Craig, of Surrey-road; gave the prisoner a good character.)
GUILTY . Aged 49.— Transported for Fourteen Years.
Second Jury, before Mr. Common Sergeant.
[trialAccount t18430612-1965]
1965. JAMES WEYMOUTH was indicted for feloniously receiving of an evil-disposed person, on the 2nd of May, 7 sovereigns, and 220 l. Bank-notes, the property of John Marson, which had been before stolen, well knowing them to have been stolen.
MR. WILKINS conducted the Prosecution.
JOHN MARSON . I am a carpenter, and live in Passmore-street, Pimlico. On the 1st of May I went to the London and Westminster Bank, Lothbury, with a cheque, and received two 20 l. Bank of England notes, and ten sovereigns—I put them into my purse—on the evening of the 2nd of May I went
to the Ben Jonson, Great Wild-street, Drury-lane, with Mr. Stepping, about ten o'clock, and staid about an hour—before leaving, I took out my purse to pay for two glasses of brandy and water—my notes and sovereigns were then safe—I changed a sovereign—I took my notes out, wrapped them round a bill of exchange, and put them into my purse—I had been drinking a little—Mr. Stepping fetched a cab for me—I got in, and fell asleep in the cab —the cab man awoke me—there was no other person near me—I had told the cab- man where to drive me, and mentioned the number of my house, but he stopped about a hundred yards from my house—I paid the cab man with some money which I had put into my left-hand trowsers pocket, that I might not touch my notes—I walked home, and got up the next morning between five and six o'clock—I then discovered that my notes and money were gone—I have a wife—a plasterer lives in my first floor—I do not know the prisoner—this is the cheque which I received the money for.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. Were you drunk, or sober? A. I had been drinking a little, but was not drunk—I was not sober—I saw the prisoner on the Saturday following—he was not in custody then—I went with him to several places to find Colonel Johnson—I went with him through Highgate on Saturday—I told him I must go back to the bank, and could not go on—I went with him on the Sunday to Colney-hatch—I did not find a Colonel Johnson there.
MR. WILKINS. Q. Why did you go to Colney-hatch? A. He told me he had received this 20 l. note of a Colonel Johnson, at Colney-hatch—he knew the road till we got near there, and then he had forgotten it—I knew what was going forwards on the night I lost my notes—they were safe in my right-hand trowsers pocket when I got into the cab.
JOSEPH STEPPING . I live in Fetter-lane, and am a tailor. I was with the prosecutor at the Ben Jonson in Great Wild-street—I fetched a cab for him about a quarter before eleven o'clock—before he left he took his purse out, and had two 20 l. notes, which I had seen before, and a bill of exchange, or something of that kind—he put the notes back into his purse, and the sovereigns at the top of them—he put his purse into his pocket, and buttoned it up—I told the cab-driver to drive him to No. 16, Passmore-street, Pimlico.
HENRY SHRAPNELL WHITHERS . I am chief cashier in the London and Westminster Bank, in Lothbury. On the 1st of May this cheque was presented—I paid it in two 20 l. notes, and 10 l. in gold—this is one of the notes—( looking at it)—I took the numbers and dates at the time—this note had been issued from the Bank of England that morning, and I paid it about half an hour afterwards to the prosecutor.
WILLIAM HIGMAN . I am a clerk in the Bank of England. 100 20 l. notes were issued on the morning of the 1st of May, to the London and Westminster Bank—this is one of them.
GEORGE THOMAS HUGGETT . I live in Shoreditch, and am a carriage-maker—I have known the prisoner fifteen or eighteen months—he is a coach-swith, and lives in Brill-yard, Somer's Town—on Wednesday afternoon, the 3rd of May, about two o'clock, the prisoner came to me to buy a phaeton body—I had not one, but I said if he would take me down in his pony-chaise to Deptford, I had got two there—I was driven down there in his pony-chaise by a man who represented himself to be a cabman —that man said he drove by night, and his son by day—when we got to Deptford the prisoner purchased a phaeton body, and he offered me in payment a 20 l. note—I had not change, and we were driven back to my place in Shoreditch—the prisoner produced the note again, and gave it to my daughter-in-law to get change—she took it to Mr. Webb, and brought back gold, and gave it to
the prisoner—the prisoner said he had sold a pony-chaise for 20 l. on the Saturday previous, and had received that note for it.
Cross-examined. Q. Did he mention the word "note" at all? A. Yes—I have had dealings with him before.
SUSAN RAMPLING . I am daughter-in-law of Mr. Huggett. On Wednesday evening, the 3rd of May, the prisoner gave me a 20 l. note to get change-I changed it at Mr. Webb's, the cheesemonger's, and took the change back to the prisoner.
GEORGE WEBB . I am a cheesemonger, and live in Shoreditch. On the 3rd of May, Rampling brought me a 20 l. note—I changed it, and wrote on it, "Hugget, 169, Shoreditch"—this is it—here are my initials.
ROBERT COLE ( police-constable G 193.) On the 10th of May I received information, and went to the prisoner's place in Somer's Town—I said I was a policeman, he must consider himself in custody for stealing two 20 l. notes and seven sovereigns—he said, "Do you mean to charge me with stealing?"—I said, "Yes"—he said, "When?"—I said, "On the 2nd of May"—he said, "That is right; I can prove I took the note on the 29th, from a gentleman to whom I sold a pony-chaise."
(The prisoner received a good character.)
GUILTY . Aged 34.— Confined One Year.
Fifth Jury, before Edward Bullock, Esq.
[trialAccount t18430612-1966]
1966. WILLIAM McNESS was indicted for stealing, on the 24th of April, 6 spoons, value 3 l., the goods of Thomas Quarterman.
GEORGE KILNER . I am waiter at the York hotel, Bridge-street, Black-friars, kept by Mr. Thomas Quarterman. The prisoner came there on the 24th of April, between three and five o'clock, and had a glass of wine and a biscuit—he took his seat at a corner table—the dinner table was laid, and these six large spoons were on it.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. What do you know these spoons by? A. By the mark of "N" on them, the initial of the late landlord of the hotel—I do not know what his name was—I do not know them by any other mark—they are the spoons that were used in the house.
CHARLES WILLIAM PARR . I am in the service of Mr. Brown, a pawnbroker, in Fetter-lane—these spoons were pawned there on the 24th of April, between four and six o'clock, by the prisoner—I am sure he is the person.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you say before the Magistrates, that he was the man who pawned them? A. Yes—( looking at his deposition)—it was not stated that the prisoner pawned them—I have not said since I have been here that if any body would give me 3 l. I would go away—I have mentioned about 3 l.—I did not mark the words I said.
JAMES TIDMARSH ( police-constable M 51.) I produce these six spoons marked with a cross on the back, and the letter "N" in front—I found them at Mr. Quarterman's hotels—four at one hotel and two at another.
GEORGE TOMKINS ( police-constable M 166.) I received charge of the prisoner, on the 26th of May, for stealing plate.
NOT GUILTY .
Jury half Foreigners.

1967. ADRIENNE BANDIER was indicted for stealing, on the 6th of April, 1 pair of boots, value 10 s.; 1 cloak, 5 s.; 1 handkerchief, 1 s.; and 4 shifts, 4 s.; the goods of Frances Sophia Hastings, her mistress.
( The evidence was explained to the prisoner by an interpreter.)
FRANCES SOPHIA HASTINGS . I am single, and live in the Quadrant, in Regent-street. The prisoner was in my service for ten or eleven weeks, up to about the 1st of June—I discharged her—about a fortnight before that I had missed a pair of boots and two shifts—I accused her of taking them, she denied it—on the 8th of June I missed a cloak, and a handkerchief—this cloak, handkerchief, and two shifts are mine, and what I missed—here are two other shifts which I did not miss, but I found them at the pawnbrokers.
ANDREW WYNESS ( police-constable D 43.) I took the prisoner on the 8th of June—I went with her to No. 3, Foley-street, I there saw her looking at some duplicates which were in her hand—she put them into her pocket—I asked her to give me them—she was very abusive, and marched about the room and would not hear what I had got to say—I told a lady in the room to tell the prisoner to give them to me, but she would not—I then took her hands and called in the landlady, who took the tickets from her pocket—these are them.
JOHN SIMKINS . I am shopman to Mr. Fleming, a pawnbroker. I produce a cloak and a handkerchief pawned by the prisoner on the 16th of May—I produce two shifts, pawned on the 1st of May by a man—the tickets produced by the officer were given for them.
JAMES LEWIS . I am shopman to a pawnbroker. I have two shifts pawned by the prisoner on the 6th of April.
Prisoner's Defence. What I have done was to support a man that left England to go to France, and who was in distress. I expected some money from France, and would have redeemed the things.
GUILTY. Aged 40.— Recommended to mercy. — Confined Three Months.
[trialAccount t18430612-1968]
1968. JOHN JOHNSON and MARIA ALLPORT were indicted for stealing, on the 29th of Nov., 1 blanket, value 1 s.; 1 counterpane, 6 s.; 40 pound weight of feathers, 4 s.; 1 tea-kettle, 4 s.; 1 bolster, 8 s.; 1 saucepan, 1 s.; 1 looking-glass and stand, 2 s.; 1 jug, 6 d.; 1 wine-glass, 6 d.; 1 basin, 1 s. 6 d.; 1 ewer, 1 s.; 1 dish, 3 d.; 2 plates, 2 d.; 2 knives, 3 d.; and 2 forks, 3 d.; the goods of Elizabeth Beard.—2nd COUNT, stating them to be the goods of Elizabeth Bailey.—3rd COUNT, stating them to be the goods of our Lady the Queen.
ELIZABETH BEARD . I am the wife of Francis Beard, who is under sentence of transportation for having a letter concealed on his person. He was in the Post-office twenty years, and was transported for life—I live at No. 45, Gifford-street, Hoxton—the prisoner Allport came to my house to act as servant to Mr. Johnson, the other prisoner—he lodged with me about seven months, up to the 9th of May—Allport came to look at the lodging and engaged it—Johnson came the same evening and took possession—Johnson remained at the lodging—Allport brought a carpet bag, and fetched him some beer the first night—she came afterwards to light his fire and attend upon him every day—the room was furnished—I missed some things out of it on the 9th of May—but I had before that missed out of my own apartments a silver spoon in May, and I missed a table-cloth about two months after Mr. Johnson came to lodge there—since they have been given into custody I have missed a muslin dress, and before the 15th of April I missed a girl's shift and a bundle handkerchief—when their room was searched I found it was nearly stripped—the prisoners had not left the house till we gave them in custody—Johnson was still occupying the lodgings up to the 9th of May—I missed a counterpane, a tea-kettle, and a bolster, a saucepan, a looking-glass, a basin, a ewer, two plates, two knives, and two forks—it was property which belonged to my husband, and was in my custody—my first husband's name was Bailey—these are the articles—they were all my first husband's—none of them were my
second husband's purchasing—they all belonged to my first husband, and to my second husband also—they were let with the lodging—on the 9th of May I accused Allport of stealing a silver spoon, she denied it—I said I had lost property before—she said it was very strange, but I did not accuse her till the 9th of May—I then gave her in charge—she was taken to the station—I returned to my house with the policeman and searched the room—Johnson then came home, and I told him Mrs. Allport was out, that she had taken a silver spoon out of my apartment and I had given her in custody, that I had found the duplicate between the bed and mattress—he said he knew nothing about it—I said the room was stripped—he seemed astonished—I said the feathers were all gone out of the bed—he said he knew nothing about the pledging at first, but he then said the feathers were all made into pillows—I gave him into custody—he said what could be done—I said I had given it into the officer's hands—he then said he had been to see a gentleman that day to make arrangements to get the things out, and that he had got them out again and again, but Mrs. Allport had put them away in his absence—he said he had got them out over and over.
Cross-examined by MR. ROBINSON. Q. I believe when Johnson came home the policeman Wood was there? A. Yes—when I told Johnson, he said, "Bless my soul! is that so?"—he is a writer, or an author—I knew nothing of his circumstances till he was given into custody—Mrs. Allport cooked for him, and she has said he was poorly—he complained of ill health when he took the lodging, and said Mrs. Allport had been his nurse—she did every thing for him—I washed the table-cloth and sheets—Mrs. Allport asked me when I would have them, and she gave them to me—they have been at all times forthcoming—I had the rent by instalments through the winter, but lately more regularly—when I have missed any trifling thing I have said to Allport or to Johnson, "As far as you are concerned, all I require is, that on the day before you leave, or on that day, you will permit me to see that all my things are there"—I had no wish to prosecute—I believe I mentioned to Johnson's father, that if the things had been all safe, I should have thought nothing about it—I wished to get them out of the house quietly.
JAMES WOOD ( police-constable N 277) deposed as in the former case. (See page 366.)
THOMAS MAYNARD . I am foreman to Mr. Edwards, a pawnbroker—I have two pillows, a bolster, a blanket, and a table-cloth, all pawned by Allport.
Cross-examined. Q. I believe they have constantly pawned and taken them out? A. Yes, all but one spoon, which was pawned on the 3rd of May—those things have been pawned and redeemed once a week—I think this sheet is worth 7 s.—4 s. was advanced on it, which was all that was asked—it is not worth more than 5 s. in trade.
JAMES WOOD re-examined. I searched the room, but Johnson was not there—I took him to the station, and found on him fifty-four duplicates, twenty-two of which relate to this property.
HENRY LIEBRICHT . I am assistant to Mr. Sowerby, a pawnbroker—I produce a copper tea-kettle, pawned by Allport.
Cross-examined. Q. I believe she has pawned articles at your shop almost every day? A. This very kettle was pawned in the morning, and redeemed in the evening, for about a fortnight previous to the 2nd of April. The record of the conviction of Francis Beard was put in and read.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18430612-1969]
1969. MARIA SCOTT was indicted for stealing, on the 20th of May, 8 yards of calico, value 5 s., and 4 yards of Orleans cloth, 4 s.; the goods of Arabella Burnell, her mistress.
ARABELLA BUBNELL . I am single, and keep a ready-made linen shop, in Guildford-place, Clerkenwell—the prisoner worked for me about two months—I told her repeatedly to leave, but she would not—she came every day, early in the morning, and opened the shop—I did not like to turn her away—she came in the morning, on Whit Tuesday, the 6th of June, pulled off her shawl, and was still in the shop—I told her I would not have her work for me any longer—about an hour after, while I was serving a customer, she made an excuse to go into the back kitchen, and I followed her—she came back into the shop, and I perceived something under her shawl—I found it was 2 1/4 yards of new calico, which I had seen before on the end of the counter—it is mine—she said it was her sister's—I asked which sister—the said the one in Whiskin-street, that she had it to make a petticoat, and the would go to her sister—I said she should not, and gave her in charge—I know this calico, by a dirty mark or fold that is on it, and can swear to it—there were two duplicates found in her pocket, which led me to three or four pieces of Orleans cloth, which are mine.
WILLIAM BURTON . I am a police-inspector. The prisoner was put into my custody.
MARY ANN REDMAN . I searched the prisoner at the station—I found on her three duplicates, two of them were for calico and Orleans cloth.
WILLIAM CLARK . I am in the service of Mr. Brown, a pawnbroker, in Griy's-inn-road. This Orleans cloth was pawned, I believe, by the prisoner—I gave this duplicate for it
CHARLES AUGUSTUS FRANCIS . I am in the service of Mr. Walker, a pawnbroker. I have three remnants of calico, pawned, I believe, by the prisoner—I gave this duplicate for it.
( Property produced and sworn to.)
GUILTY . Aged 15.— Confined Three Months.

1970. WILLIAM YOUNG was indicted for stealing, on the 10th of June, 10lbs. weight of bacon, value 5 s., the goods of John Hutley.
JOHN JAMES RESTIEAUX ( police-constable E 49.) I saw the prisoner in George-street, St. Giles, on Saturday night, the 10th of June, about half-past eight o'clock, with this bacon under his coat—I asked how he came by it—he threw it down, and said he bought it of a strange man in Holborn—I asked him how much it weighed—he said nine pounds—I took him to the station, and found it weighed ten pounds—he was not drunk.
BENJAMIN DUNT . I am in the service of John Hutley, a cheesemonger, in Broad-street, St. Giles. On Saturday night I saw the prisoner take hold of this piece of bacon—I asked him if he would buy it—he made no answer—I was busy with several people, and did not miss it till a quarter of an hour after, when the policeman came.
Prisoner's Defence. A man offered to sell it me cheap; he said there was nine pounds of it, and he wanted 4 d. a pound for it; he said he had got it from a chandler's-shop; I gave him 3 s. for it, and when the policeman came I dropped it accidentally—I was intoxicated.
GUILTY . Aged 37.— Confined One Month.
