1971. JAMES RALPH was indicted for stealing, on the 14th of April, 2 wheels value 4 s., the goods of Benjamin Newport.
BENJAMIN NEWPORT . I live at the corner of North-street, East India-road, and am a carpenter. I have a shed in my garden, and lost a pair of
small truck wheels from it—I saw them again, on the 9th of June, at Mr. Matthews's—I am sure they are mine.
WILLIAM MATTHEWS . I am a broker, and live in Salmon's-lane. On the 15th of April the prisoner brought me this pair of wheels—he said his truck was run over and broken, and he could not sell his articles—I bought the wheels of him for half-a-crown—I am sure he is the man.
JAMES EASTON . I am shop-boy to Mr. Matthews. I remember the prisoner bringing these wheels—I am sure he is the person—I went with the policeman and pointed him out.
HENRY WOOD ( police-constable K 44.) Easton pointed out the prisoner to me—I asked where he got those wheels—he said he never sold any wheels to him; but at the station he said, "I will pay the money sooner than go before the Magistrate."
Prisoner's Defence. From last August I was at work; I know nothing about the wheels; if I had wanted to sell them, I should not have gone close to my own home; there are plenty of men dressed like me.
GUILTY. Aged 23.— Recommended to mercy. — Confined One Month.
[trialAccount t18430612-1972]
1972. ELLEN REGAN was indicted for stealing, on the 1st of June, 1 coat, value 1 l.; 3 pairs of trowsers, 1 l. 10 s.; 1 jacket, 10 s.; 4 shirts, 10 s.; 4 sheets, 1 l.; and 1 pair of shoes, 5 s.; the goods of Edmond Pennington; and that she had been before convicted of felony.
EDMOND PENNINGTON . I live in King-street, Kensington, and am a tailor. On the 30th of May I missed the articles stated, from my room—the prisoner was in the habit of visiting me—when I leave my room I put my key into a knife-box which hangs outside the door—sometimes under the mat, and sometimes in another place—when I left it on that day, at six o'clock in the morning, it was in the knife-box—I came home at twelve—when I returned I had some suspicions, and went after the prisoner, and found her in John-street, St. Marylebone—two days after, I went into the prisoner's room—I told her a sister of hers had come from Liverpool, and if she would come to Kensington, she would see her at my room—the prisoner said she did not think her sister was there—I said she was—she said again she did not think she was, that she knew what brought me there, that she took the things, or stole them, I cannot say which, and burnt the duplicates—she took 31 s. 6 d. from her pocket, and said it was part of the produce of my property—most of my things had been in the drawers, which were not locked.
CHARLES REEVES . I am assistant to a pawnbroker. On the 30th of May the prisoner pawned this coat, three pairs of trowsers, three waistcoats, four shirts, three sheets, and a pair of shoes, for 2 l. 14 s.—I am sure it was her.
HENRY MOUNT ( police-constable T 126.) On the 1st of June the prisoner was given into my charge—the prosecutor brought this money, and stated how he got it.
Prisoner's Defence. He found me in Little Park-lane; I was not dressed; he told me to put my things on, and that my sister had come from Liverpool; he sent for drink, and then said, "I have been robbed, if you will tell me what you have done with the things, I will not hurt a hair of your head;" and he being my sister's husband, I thought he would not; I went and told him where the things were.
JOHN BARTON . I produce a certificate of the prisoner's former conviction, which I got from the Clerk of the Peace of Westminster—( read)—the prisoner is the person. Prisoner. Q. Can you take your oath of that? A. Yes, I have done it.
Prisoner. I know you do not mind taking a false oath, and you have taken one now.
GUILTY . Aged 35.— Transported for Seven Years.

1973. JOHN CLANCEY was indicted for stealing, on the 1st of June, I book of patterns, value 5 s., the goods of John Walker, from his person.
JOHN WALKER . I live at Norwood, and am a tailor. I was in the Two Brewers, in St. Giles, on the 1st of June—this pattern-book was in my coat pocket—I had been in front of the bar, and as I was coming out, I was spoken to by the policeman, and I then missed my book—(this is it)—I had seen the prisoner in the house.
Prisoner. Q. Was there other persons there? A. Yes, five or six persons—I was rather intoxicated.
JOHN SIMS HANCOCK ( police-sergeant E 12.) I was passing the Two Brewers, and hearing quarrelling, I looked in, and saw the prisoner take this book out of the prosecutor's pocket—he put it under his coat, and ran into No. 29—I followed, and brought him out—he threw this book down among some females.
Prisoner. Q. Where were you? A. Looking through the windows from outside—I saw all this plainly.
Prisoner. I saw the book on the floor and took it up; I brought it out in my hand.
GUILTY . Aged 21.— Confined Six Months.
NEW COURT.— Wednesday, June 21st, 1843.
Fifth Jury before Mr. Common Sergeant.

1974. HENRY HARRIS was indicted for stealing, on the 25th of May, I trowel, value 1 s.; 1 saw, 1 s.; 4 planes, 4 s.; 1 shovel, 1 s.; the goods of Francis Carter: and 1 plane, 1 s., the goods of George Presland.
CHARLES HAINES . I had a job of bricklaying, and borrowed a trowel of the prisoner—I gave it him back, and after he had pawned it he said he would sell me the ticket of it for 2 d.—I bought it and gave it to the officer.
GEORGE PRESLAND . I am a carpenter, and live in Derbyshire-street, Bethnal-green. On the 25th of May I left my basket, containing the articles stated—I lost them—this is my plane—I have a mark on it.
FRANCIS CARTER . I am building in Cooper's-gardens, Bethnal-green—I left my tools there safe on the 24th, and missed them on the 25th—this is my shovel, and this saw is mine, and this trowel.
JAMES WILKINSON . I am a pawnbroker. I took this saw in of the prisoner, the trowel was pawned by a female.
WILLIAM WAGG . I have a plane, pledged on this 26th of May—I cannot remember by whom.
ELIZABETH RIPP . I produce two small planes; the prisoner left them with me for a night's lodging.
FRANCIS CARTER re-examined. These planes left with Ripp are mine.
Prisoner's Defence. I bought the shovel and trowel of a man near Shoreditch church.
GUILTY . Aged 24.— Confined Four Months.
[trialAccount t18430612-1975]
1975. JOHN WARD was indicted for stealing, on the 14th of March, 1 shawl, value 15 s., the goods of Patrick Welch; and that he had been before convicted of felony.
CATHERINE WELCH . I am the wife of Patrick Welch, and live in York-court,
Marylebone. On the 14th of March my shawl was on a table just under my parlour window—I missed it about four o'clock in the afternoon—I saw the prisoner running from the window down through the court—a man from the window could reach it—this is it.
JOHN WELCH . I live in York-court. I was at my mother's door on the 14th of March—I saw the prisoner lift up the window and take the shawl out, and run down the court as fast as he could—I followed, and begged him not to take it—he turned back, used a bad expression, and said, if I said a word he would transport me, so help him God he would say I took the shawl; and my father being out of town, I got frightened—I had known him before.
Prisoner. Q. Did you not meet me at the corner of Lisson-grove that day? A. No, I did not meet any one in Crawford-street.
MARY WARNE . I am the wife of William Warne, a shoemaker, in the Harrow-road. The prisoner brought this shawl to my shop for sale, on the 14th of March—he asked 12 s. for it—I said I did not want such a thing—he said he had taken it in the country in exchange for goods—I pawned it for 8 s. and gave him the money—he asked me to purchase the ticket as it was of no use to him, and I did.
Prisoner. Q. Did you not see another person, and give him some beer out of a glass? A. No; you called two or three days after, and I gave you 6 d. for the ticket; John Welch came when I paid it—he was not with you when you brought the shawl.
WILLIAM LEONARD ( police-constable D 50.) I took the prisoner, and told him what it was for—he made no reply, but afterwards said Welch gave him the shawl at the corner of William-street.
Prisoner's Defence. I was at the corner of William-street; Welch came and said he had been in the Park, and found a shawl, if I would sell it he would give me 1 s.; he told me to go to Warne's; I went and said I wanted 5 s.; she said she had no money, but would see if she could pawn it; she came back, and said she had pawned it for 5 s., and had lost the ticket; I did not see the ticket; I took the 5 s. to Welch, and he gave me 1 s.
JOHN WELCH re-examined. I lent the prisoner 1 s., and asked him for it on Sunday—he said, if I would go a little way, he had 1 s. owing him, and he would pay it—he went to Warne's house—she could not give him 1 s.—she gave him sixpence, and he gave it to me—I never went to Warne's house to pawn the shawl, or see it pawned.
RICHARD RINGHAM . I am a jobbing man, and work for Mr. Byfeild. I live in Devonshire-place, Lisson-grove. I was standing at the corner of Homer-street, about the 14th of March—Welch came to me and said, "Have you seen anything of Ward?"—I said, "No"—he said, "I have just nailed a shawl worth 30 s., and he has run away, I am afraid he won't give it me"—I do not know what day of the week the 14th of March was—I never took notice—I never heard anything about it till three weeks ago, when Welch came and asked me after Ward—I thought it was the 14th of March—it is a good while ago—I had seen Welch about three times with Ward—I have only known Ward ten months—I have not associated much with him—I have no reason for saying it was that day—I only know it from what I was told by Ward's father—no one else was present.
MARTHA KELLY . I go out washing. I was coming out of York-court, between twelve and one o'clock, and saw Welch half way down the court with the shawl under his arm—I did not tell Mrs. Welch what her son had done—I happened to lose the ticket of a shawl, and went for an affidavit—I saw the prisoner in the Court-house, and asked what it was for—I was told
it was for a shawl that John Welch gave him to pawn, or to sell—I do not know which—there was a young person with me—I will take my oath to the shawl Welch had under his jacket—I could not tell whether it was on his right or left side—I had not known him before—I saw him coming out of the parlour window, and go towards East-street—I do not know whether he that the window, nor what he was dressed in—I believe he had a fustian jacket on I could almost take my oath it is the same he has on now—it is the same-he walked away—he did not say anything to any one—I never saw the other young woman—I do not know her name—I did not see Ward—I do not know him if I saw him—I do not know his father and mother any more than from seeing them outside—I came here to-day of my own accord by myself—his step-father lives in York-court, the same court where this took place—I do not know him, and was never in his house, nor in bis mother's house—I never exchanged three words with either of them in my life.
MATILDA WATTS . I saw John Welch come out of his mother's window, with a bundle under his jacket, on a Tuesday, in March—I cannot say what day of the month it was—I saw the black, and I saw the fringe—it looked to me like a shawl—I live in the court—I was coming down the court, from Davis-street, with my little girl in my hand—it was between eleven and twelve o'clock in the day—I knew Mrs. Welch—I did not tell her of the robbery, because it is no use telling her, she will always hold with her own child—I went out to my work—I had a glimpse of Kelly-there, but I did not speak to her till to-day or yesterday—I came here because I thought it a shame for one to suffer for another—I came up of my own accord—I know the prisoner's father-in-law—I first heard of the prisoner being taken up two or three days ago, and I thought if he had no imprisonment I would not interfere about it—I go out washing early in the morning, and return late at night—I live with my father—he is old—he was a sailor, and has got a pension—I have heard within three weeks that Ward was taken up—I heard he was at Marylebone office—I told a neighbour that I saw Welch go out with a bundle—I did not go to Marylebone office—Welsh was dressed in a fustian jacket and trowsers—I did not notice whether he shut the window—I spoke to nobody about it.
THOMAS MANNING . I am the prisoner's step-father, and live in York-court. Last Wednesday Mrs. Welch sent for me, and said she wanted nothing but her own—she asked me 1 l. 15 s. for the shawl, and said she did not wish to take the bill out, because her own boy was guilty; and then her daughter said she would take a guinea, and the daughter took a guinea of me—I met Welch and my boy together, at three o'clock, in the Bayswater-road, and they gave a pint of beer to a woman.
CATHERINE WELCH re-examined. I did not take the guinea—my daughter was on a sick-bed—he put it down, and my daughter took a buss, and came and brought it down here after him, and gave it to the officer—it was only to say it was not my property—Kelly lives in my brother's house—my boy was with me on the 14th of March, and came back with me—he came in two minutes before me—I had the key of my door in my hand—when I saw the prisoner run from my house, I stood thirty or forty yards from the boy, and he stood at my door waiting for me—my boy ran down after the prisoner, and returned back to me in about a minute, and said, "John Welch has taken your shawl, and he says if you will persevere he will transport me"—my husband was at work at that time for his employer in the country—he has been at home now about a fortnight—as soon as he came home my boy told him—I then went to where the prisoner live, and he was taken—Kelly cohabits with the Wards—she is there daily—I can swear that my boy was not
out of my sight from the time I put my shawl off my back on to the table, till I missed it.
JOHN WELCH re-examined. I was not with the prisoner about three o'clock the same day in the Bayswater road—I did not see him, or go with him that day, after he had run away with the shawl—I was with my mother all that day—I did not leave home all day—my mother went out, but I stopped in the room—I was in the yard when she came with the keys—I had been to the water-closet.
ELIZABETH SEBLEY . I live in Ward's-buildings, Great Woodstock-street—I saw the guinea paid to Mrs. Welch's daughter—I know the prisoner and his friends, but I am not living with him—I never stopped away from my father's an hour in my life.
JOHN SHOVELLER ( police-constable D 155.) I produce a certificate of the prisoner's former conviction, which I got from Mr. Clark's office—(read)—the prisoner is the person.
GUILTY . * Aged 19.— Transported for Ten Years.
[trialAccount t18430612-1976]
1976. GEORGE GREVILLE was indicted for stealing, on the 1st of July, 16 handkerchiefs, value 2 l.; 38 shirts, 5 l.; 2 pairs of socks, 5 s.; one shawl, 4 s.; two stocks, 3 s.; 10 pairs of stockings, 17 s.; 5 yards of calico, 8 s.; 48 shirt collars, 12 s.; and one scarf, 5 s.; the goods of John Mills, his master: and SARAH ANN DOWSON , for feloniously receiving the same, well knowing them to have been stolen; against the Statute, &c.
MR. BODKIN conducted the Prosecution.
JOHN MILLS . I am an outfitter, and live in Oxford-street. Greville was my managing foreman—he came about the 2nd or 3rd of January, 1842, and continued till he was apprehended—I knew him by the name of Greville—he told me he was a single man—he had 1 l. a-week, and his board—I trusted him a good deal—I took stock at Christmas and found a deficiency of goods.
GEORGE KEMP ( police-constable N 82.) On the 27th of April, in consequence of information, I went in private clothes with Ball, to Somerset-place, Hoxton—between five and six in the morning I saw Greville come out of No. 38, with a man called Long Tom —they went down several streets till they came to Clerkenwell, and went into a public-house by the Sessions' House—(I had not followed them twenty yards before they saw me watching them, and they separated)—when they came out I followed the prisoner—he went up Mutton-hill—I saw a broker and borrowed a white apron, and still followed him on to Tottenham Court-road—he stood and looked at me—he then went on—I followed him to Oxford-street, and then he went down a turning to Oxford-market—I saw a policeman and told him to watch him—he then ran off as hard as he could—I went round Marylebone-lane, and met him coming out that way—I followed him back up to Regent-street—I then went and said, "Who are you? Where do you live?"—he said, "I shall give you no information"—he said I had no authority—I showed him my staff, and told him I was an officer, and took him to the station—he gave the name of George Greville, 38, Somerset-place—he said he was out of employ—I then took him to the Hoxton station, in the neighbourhood of Somerset-place, and said "I charge you on suspicion of stealing a quantity of goods, shirts, and other things, the property of a person unknown"—he said he knew nothing about them—I found a bunch of keys on him—I and the other officer went to the house in Somerset-place, leaving the prisoner at the station—I saw Dowson at the house, and asked who she was—she said her name was Mrs. Greville—I said she must consider herself in custody, on suspicion of receiving a quantity of stolen shirts and other things, and she said she knew nothing about
them—I opened a drawer with one of the keys which I took from Greville—I found in it a pocket-book, containing duplicates—I had before asked if she had got any duplicates—she said she had got none—in a hole in the scrutoire in the drawer, I found some stockings—I got the key from Dowson—on the table I found this sheeting, which Downson was at work on—I asked where that came from—she said, "Oh, that is all right; that was bought at the tally-shop; I bought fourteen yards of it"—I found some shirt collars and handkerchiefs—they are all here—Dowson said they were her own.
Cross-examined by MR. DOANE. Q. The sheeting was some calico on the table? A. Yes, it was making up into shirts—some of it was hemmed—I saw a great many dirty shirt collars—I found no shirts there.
WILLIAM EDWARD BALL ( police-constable N 365.) I was with Kemp, and saw Greville and Long Tom separate—I followed Long Tom —he went to the prisoner's house about half-past seven o'clock, and came out again, after staying about half an hour—I followed him to Hoxton, and then lost all sight of him—when he came out the second time he had a paper parcel in his hand—he had it when he gave me the slip—I have seen him since go into his own house, but I had no ground for taking him—I arranged to take some message to Dowson—I went, and inquired for Mrs. Greville—she said, "I am Mrs. Greville"—she asked me, and I went in, I told her Mr. Greville was in a little trouble, and wanted his master to bail him—she got the pen and ink, and gave the address of Mr. Mills, the prosecutor—Kemp then joined me, and we searched the place—Kemp asked if she had any tickets—she said "No"—I asked if she had any duplicates of shirts pawned at Mr. smith's Pitfield-street—she said, "Yes, I went up stairs, and found some duplicates—the prisoners found this calico sheeting—she said it was her own calico, and she bought it at the tally-shop—I went up stairs, and found some duplicates—the prisoners kept the whole of the house—I found one pair of stockings, some collars, and other things, a quantity of handkerchiefs, and a piece of satin—I found this scarf in a box in the prisoner's bed-room—Dowson said that was a present made to Greville by his master, and the other things were all his—I found some more duplicates in a box—Dowson's things were kept in the same room.
Cross-examined. Q. You asked how she knew that the scarf was given him by his master? A. No, I did not, I swear positively—she said he had told her so, but I did not ask her—she showed me a bill of the calico.
GEORGE HANSON . I am assistant to Mr. Smith, pawnbroker, Pitfield-street. I produce five handkerchiefs pawned on the 20th of June, and two handkerchiefs pawned on the 25th of June, six shirts pawned on the 4th of July, two pairs of silk stockings on the 12th of Aug., five shirts on the 19th of Aug., three pairs of stockings on the 26th of Aug., two shirts on the 30th of Dec., some silk stockings and two handkerchiefs on the 24th of June, two handkerchiefs on the 25th of June, six shirts on the 4th of July, and three shirts on the 12th of July, all pawned by Dowso—I never remember Greville pawning anything—here is one of our duplicates found at her room relating to a shawl and three pairs of hose, pawned by Dowson on the 19th of August.
Cross-examined. Q. How can you tell us that Dawson pledged these things? A. From memory, and likewise from my own handwriting on the duplicates—I cannot tell how she was dressed—I quite remember seeing her—perhaps 200 persons come in to pawn in the course of the day—she came alone on the 23rd—I will not swear that her servant was not with her on the 12th of Aug.—her servant did not pledge the goods on the 23rd—her servant has pawned several times—I sometimes put down the name of Greville, and
sometimes the servant's name—a great many duplicates have been returned—none of them were for things pawned at our shop.
THOMAS WALDUCK . I live in the Hackney-road—I was in the service of Pickford and Roberts, pawnbrokers, in the Old-street-road—on the 11th of Nov. a handkerchief and sheet were pawned in the name of Ann Greville—this is the duplicate I gave—I do not remember the person.
SAMUEL ROSE . I am assistant to Mr. Shepherd, a pawnbroker—I have two stocks pawned at our shop by Dowson, in the name of Ann Williams—this duplicate produced by the officer corresponds with it—I produce a handkerchief pawned on the 15th of April—I do not know who by—this is the duplicate I gave the person.
RICHARD CRESSEY . I am shopman to Mr. Smith, a pawnbroker, in Bath-street. On the 26th of July I took in three handkerchiefs and two pair of silk stockings of Dowson—I gave her these duplicates—I produce a handkerchief pawned on the 6th of April, in the name of Mary Williams—I do not know by whom—this is the duplicate I gave—I produce six sheets pawned on the 8th of April, in the name of John Williams, six sheets on the 13th of April, by Dowson, six shirts on the 22nd of April, in the name of John Williams—I find the duplicates of all these among those produced by the officer.
Cross-examined. Q. Do you speak positively to those pawned in July? A. Yes—I have known Dowson some time—I remember taking them—I made out the duplicates.
JOHN MILLS re-examined. I do not know these things by any mark, but I had in my stock articles corresponding with these—I have missed them from my stock—the shirts were made by persons for me.
HENRY ROGERS . I am a shirt-maker in Addle-street, and supply the prosecutor—all the shirts produced by Cressey are my make, and two others also.
Cross-examined. Q. Do you make anything but shirts? A. No—I supply many persons besides the prosecutor—the shirts I make vary according to the parties we sell them to—we put a distinct mark for each customer—Mr. Mills has not a particular mark—I cannot tell when I sold these, except from the book—all the shirts had a particular mark, and all of them were sold to Mr. Mills—these have not got that mark—I could not swear to whom they were sold.
MR. BODKIN. Q. These six shirts produced by Cressey were part of twelve dozen that bore a particular mark, and were all sold to the prosecutor, and these have this particular mark? A. Yes.
MR. DOANE. Q. When was it you sold them? A. Between three and four months ago, while the prisoner was in the prosecutor's employ—this is the mark, "Q Q"—there is no other mark—the other two shirts have only the stock mark.
GEORGE BRITTAIN . I am a shirt-maker—these five shirts produced by Hanson, and pawned on the 23rd of August, are my make—I made them to order for Mr. Mills about twelve months ago—here is my mark on them.
JOHN LITTLEWOOD . I am assistant to my uncle, a hosier in New Bond-street—Greville was in my employ from Jan., 1839, till July 3rd, 1841—it was his business to give out work made by women, but principally to Dawson—he introduced her to us as a person who could do needle-work—I knew her as Mrs. Greville—he was known as George Greville.
GREVILLE — GUILTY . Aged 31.— Transported for Seven Years.
DOWSON — GUILTY . Aged 34.— Confined Six Months.
(There was another indictment against Greville.)

1977. WILLIAM ROOKE was indicted for stealing, on the 2nd of June, 2 petticoats, value 4 s.; the goods of Elizabeth Holbrook.
ELIZABETH HOLBROOK . I live in Portpool-lane. On the 2nd of June I had some petticoats drying—I took them out of the tub, and hung them across the chair—the prisoner came in with a board—he took it in—I went in, and they were safe—he came in from where they were three times, and the third time he came with the petticoats in a bundle, under his arm, running with water—they have not been found.
Prisoner's Defence. I never saw any petticoats, or touched them.

JOHN READ ( police-constable N 171.) I live in High-street, Homerton. I was on duty in Church-street, Hackney, about half-past three o'clock on the 10th of June—I saw the prisoner drop this coat and jacket, and run off—I took him without losing sight of him.

1979. ELIZABETH FAGAN and ALICE FAGAN were indicted for stealing, on the 8th of June, 3 bottles, value 3 d.; 1 1/2 pint of ginger-beer, 4 d.; 1 dead lobster, 1 s.; 3/4lb. weight of pickled salmon, 1 s. 6 d.; 1lb. weight of mutton, 6 d.; 1/2lb. weight of cheese, 4 d.; and 1lb. weight of bread, 1 d.; the goods of Richard Pryce, the master of Elisabeth Fagan.
RICHARD PRYCE . I live in the Strand; the prisoner Elizabeth was my maid of all work. About seven o'clock, on the 8th of June, I was called up by the police, who produced these things—I believe the ginger-beer to be mine—I asked Elizabeth, who is Alice's sister, to put on her bonnet and come to Bow-street—she agreed, and said her sister had drawn her to take these things—I made no promise to induce her to tell me—these things, are worth, altogether, 3 s. 6 d.
CHARLES HENRY BAGNALL ( police-constable F 31.) About half-past six o'clock this morning I stopped the prisoner Alice in Pickett-street, about half a mile from the prosecutor's—she had a basket—I asked what she had them—she said some empty bottles and other things—I asked where she got it—she said from 430, Strand—I knocked at the door—it was opened by Elizabeth, who said, "Good God, what is the matter?"—Alice said, "The policeman stopped me"—Elizabeth said, "I did give her these things, and I will give you 2 l. if you will say nothing about it," and then she said, "I will make it up 5 l."
Elizabeth Fagan. I never said so.
ELIZABETH FAGAN — GUILTY . Aged 28.— Confined Nine Days.
ALICE FAGAN — NOT GUILTY .
Sixth Jury, before Edward Bullock, Esq.
[trialAccount t18430612-1980]
1980. STEWART MAXWELL was indicted for embezzlement.
MR. PRENDERGAST conducted the Prosecution.
WILLIAM DEFOY . I an a Custom-house agent, and carry on business in London and in Paris; I was in England at the beginning of March. It was port of the prisoner's business to receive money on my account—Mr. Hall is
my clerk, and came into my employ a little while before the prisoner—I took the prisoner on his recommendation—I have not received either of the sums in question of the prisoner—it was Mr. Hall's duty to receive from the prisoner any monies he had received, and the prisoner ought to account to him immediately after receiving it.
JAMES HODSON . I am clerk to Mr. Prater, an army-clothier, at charing-cross; he occasionally acts as agent for some of his customers in the army. I paid the prisoner, on the 11th of March, 5 l. 2 s. 6 d. on account of Colonel William Ross, for Mr. Defoy.
Cross-examined by MR. HORRY. Q. Had you ever paid part of a bill to Hall, and part to the prisoner? A. Yes, and received the prisoner's receipt for the whole—that was the case on the present occasion—I had paid Mr. Hall 5 l. 2 s. 5 d., two or three days before—this is a receipt for 10 l. 4 s. 11 d., the full amount of the two sums.
ALFRED JOSEPH ALDERSON . I am clerk to Mr. Adland. On the 2nd of April I paid the prisoner 2 l. 15 s. 6 1/2 d. on account of Mr. Defoy—this is the receipt—the prisoner wrote it in my presence.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you give him the whole of the money? A. Yes.
JAMES ANTHONY VOLTEUR . On the 4th of April I paid the prisoner 3 l. 13 s. on account of Mr. Defoy—this is the receipt he gave—it was written in my presence.
Cross-examined. Q. Was that sum paid in the whole? A. Yes.
HERBERT WILLIAM HALL . I am the prosecutor's clerk. The prisoner was authorized to receive a sum on account of Colonel Ross, and various sums on account of his master—he has never paid me any sum of money as received on account of Colonel Ross—he has not accounted for a sum received of Adland, and for only 3 l. received from Volteur on the 4th of April—he paid me no sum of money at all on the 2nd April, nor for any received on the 11th of March—he did not mention at the time he paid the 3 l. that he had received any other sum from Volteur—he said Mr. Volteur would not pay any more, our charges were too high.
Cross-examined. Q. How many times were you before the Lord Mayor? A. Perhaps six—it may have been ten—the Lord Mayor said the prisoner might go out on bail—my depositions were not taken at the office—I signed none—it was not at my suggestion that I went before the Grand Jury without going before the Lord Mayor—our solicitor brought me here—I do not recollect being told to come before the Lord Mayer again—I was not aware that I had to go again—I will swear that the Lord Mayor did not say my accounts were kept in a very suspicious and unsatisfactory manner—he said it would have been better if the prisoner had kept the books himself—he did not say "and not trusted so much to me"—the prisoner did not keep any books himself, he depended entirely on me—on one occasion he gave a receipt in full where I have only received a portion of the money—he was in the habit of paying money over to me and taking no voucher—I was to pay him 10 s. a week—I have the cash-book here, which will show that I paid him regularly—we were in the habit of going out in company together every evening—we have played twice at skittles—I borrowed 2 l. of him—I do not remember borrowing 15 s.—I paid him—it was two or three months ago—I do not owe him anything—my salary is 70 l. a year—I cannot tell when I returned the 2 l.—I did not talk about paying the night before his apprehension—I do not think anything passed with any one about this 2 l.—it may have happened, and I forgot it—he was generally the collecting-clerk—I have oftener sent him out than gone myself—I do not think he has paid me money above a dozen times since
Feb.—it may have been a dozen, but not more—nothing passed between me and him about his wages having run in arrears for two or three weeks—I wrote to his father to say that he had misconducted himself, and to send the money up—I wrote a second time, but got no money—I told Mr. Chittle of the prisoner's deficiency, and wanted him to give some money, and it was directly after they refused to give me the money that I took him into custody—that was on the 9th of May—Mr. Prater's was the only one I had found out—this amounted to 5 l. 2 s. 6 d., and I sent to his father for 6 l. 4 s. 10 d.—I found out the other sums the next morning—the witnesses were all there on the second occasion, and examined—our solicitor applied to have him reminded—I do not know whether he did on the third occasion.
MR. PRENDERGAST. Q. Did you know from the prisoner that he had other money besides the 10 s. a. week? A. Yes, he said he had 15 s. a-week from his father—I wrote for monies he was deficient in on my own account—I did not communicate the deficiency to Mr. Defoy directly I discovered it—I did to the prisoner—he said he would write to his father to send it, and I waited for the result before I told Mr. Defoy—this was at the prisoner's request.
CHARLES HAWKES ( police-sergeant D 9.) I apprehended the prisoner, and found a quantity of papers on his person, and this small paper I found in a glove in his pocket.
HERBERT WILLIAM HALL re-examined by MR. HORRY. Q. Is this your signature to this paper? A. No, it is not—this is all my writing, and this too, and this—I did not say, before inspector Black, and other persons, that this was my handwriting.
MR. PRENDERGAST. Q. Is this which you say is not yours, like yours? A. Yet, partly—the body of it is in the prisoner's handwriting.
WILLIAM DEFOY re-examined. I have seen the signature to this paper—I do not believe it to be Hall's—the "L's" appear to have been placed over the letters again by a pen—the others do not—Hall has not hesitated from the first to say that this is a forgery.
Cross-examined. Q. Is this other Maxwell's signature? A. I believe it is—I first saw it at the Mansion House.
(The prisoner received a good character.)
GUILTY. Aged 19.— Recommended to mercy. — Confined Twelve Months.
[trialAccount t18430612-1981]
1981. JAMES REEVES was indicted for stealing, on the 2nd of June, 1 chest, value 2 s.; and 62lbs. weight of tea, 14 l.; the goods of Charles Rayner and another.
MR. DOANE conducted the Prosecution.
SAMUEL RANDALL . I am in partnership with Charles Rayner, of Aylesbury-street, Clerkenwell. On the 2nd of June, I sent Macklin with a truck to Mr. Hewitt, to order two chests of tea—he came back with only one. JAMES MACKLIN. I am in the prosecutor's service—I went to Mr. Hewitt on the 2nd of June, for an order—I got the order, and went to the London Docks—I put a chest of tea into a covered truck—I saw the prisoner at the Docks, with a low cart and a grey horse—he bad some chests of tea in the cart—he took three from the Docks—he said. "Where are you going?"—I said, "Near Nicholson's wharf"—I said, "Will you give me a pull behind your cart?"—he said, "No, I go too fast for you, if I see you at Nicholson's wharf, I will give you a pull up Fish-street-hill"—I went to Nicholson's wharf, and met the prisoner there with the cart—I asked him where I should go for my tea—he pointed out where I was to go, and said, "Do not leave your truck in the middle of the road, or the police will take it away, but if you back it against my cart, I will take great care of it"—I believed he was
telling the truth, and did so—I left the tea-chest in the truck—I went to Nicholson's wharf, and came back in about ten minutes—the prisoner, his horse and cart, and one of my chests were gone—I had noticed the name on the cart, "Henry Cone, No. 2, Ann's-court, Upper Thames-street"—I told the policeman, and at last I found the prisoner unloading his cart, and gave him into custody—I asked if he had seen anything of my tea—he said, "No"—I said, "I left my truck in your charge, you ought to have taken care of it"—he said, "I have not seen any one touch your truck"—I came back and told the policeman—he came up, and said he must go with him to the station—he said, "Let me take my cart and horse home first," he would not, and he was taken.
Cross-examined by MR. HORRY. Q. Where was it you told him he ought to have taken better care of your truck? A. In Martin's-lane, Cannon-street—he was unloading his cart at Finchinson's warehouse—it is not a very long way off Nicholson's wharf—it was about ten minutes from my missing the tea and finding the prisoner again—the address was not on the truck—I had not told him where I came from.
HENRY SMITH . I am delivery-foreman at the London Docks. I remember a chest of tea being delivered to Macklin.
THOMAS HOPKINS . I am in the service of Lynn and Gibson, fishmongers, near Nicholson's wharf. On the 2nd of June I was in the shop, and heard a something coming on the pavement"—I looked out, and law the prisoner lift up a square green chest of tea, and put it on the copse of his cart by itself—not among any other chests—soon after I heard of the loss of this chest—I saw Macklin crying—I afterwards saw the prisoner in the custody of king—I went to the station, and the prisoner denied all knowledge of Macklin—I turned round to him, and said, "Did you not pick up a box of tea that fell to the ground?"—"Yes," said he, "I did, it fell from my cart"—I said I did not see any vacancy where a chest of tea could fall from the body of his cart—that is the fact, the cart was not filled high up.
Cross-examined. Q. It was the fall that attracted your attention? A. Yes—the truck was behind the cart—when I saw the chest lifting up again it was beside the cart—whether it had fallen off any copse I could not tell—the prisoner's face was towards me—I did not see him drive away—his horse's head was turned in the direction of Martin's-lane.
COURT. Q. Did you see the truck behind the cart? A. I did not—I saw the truck after the cart was gone, just where the cart bad been standing—it had nothing on it then.
DAVID KING ( City police-constable, No. 548.) I saw Macklin complaining of the loss of his tea—I received an account from Hopkins—I turned from him in the direction of the horse's head, that led me to search in that direction for the prisoner—as I was returning I met Macklin—he took me to Martin's-lane, Cannon-street—I found the prisoner there unloading his cart—I asked if he knew anything of the boy's tea—he said, no, he knew nothing at all about the boy, and had not seen him—I took him into custody.
HENRY CONE . I am a carman. I employed the prisoner to take some tea in one of my carts—I saw the tea after it was loaded in the cart—in my opinion none of the chests could fall out after I left it.
JURY. Q. Had your cart any business to stop at Nicholson's Wharf? A. Yes, we had three chests there—he had to stop opposite the fishmongers'—I brought the chests of tea out by another cart, and deposited them there in my cart—there were twelve chests—I could not swear none of them were put on the copse of the cart—I did not see him leave the wharf.
Prisoner. I and the carman went down Darkhouse-lane, and had a pint of
beer. Witness. He had no right to go from my cart, and go 150 yards down mother lane.
GUILTY . Aged 30.— Confined Six Months.
NEW COURT, Thursday, June 22nd, 1843.
Sixth Jury, before Mr. Common Sergeant.

[trialAccount t18430612-1992]
1992. JAMES WARE was indicted for stealing, on the 3rd of Jan., 1 cap, value 3 s.; 1 cap band, 3 s.; the goods of John Lawes.
JOHN LAWES . I am a sailor. In Oct. last, I had occasion to go to the London Hospital—I left my chest with Jane Sonnex—there was a cap and gold band in it—this is the cap—the band is not found.
Cross-examined by MR. WILKINS. Q. How long have you had it? A. About fifteen months—I bought it in India—I do not know the prisoner—Mrs. Sonnex is a widow, and I lodged with her—the prisoner was not there when I was—I knew nothing about the cap's being lost till the officer came to know if I knew anything about it.
COURT. Q. Had you seen Mrs. Sonnex, between last Oct. and last Tuesday week? A. Not till about a fortnight ago—she visited me about once a week at the Hospital, to bring me clean linen—the last time she visited me was last Tuesday week.
JANE SONNEX . The prosecutor lodged with me, and left me his clothes—among the rest there was a cap and a band—the prisoner lodged with me from the latter end of December till the 4th of January—the cap hung in my front parlour, and when he went away I missed it—his ship did not sail till the 5th of January—I went down to Blackwall—the ship was hauling out of the basin—I could not see the prisoner—I saw no more of him till Tuesday week last, when he was at the Spread Eagle, at Limehouse—I asked him to deliver me the cap and band up that be took out of my house—he called me a b----w—, and a bitch, and ran into the Windmill—I followed—I was going in, and the landlord put me out twice—I gave the prisoner in charge—I had seen him with a cap on, but I could not tell whether it was this one.
Cross-examined. Q. How many times did you see him with the cap? A. I cannot tell—I saw him with a cap and a band, but I could not tell whether it was gold or not—it was such a made cap as this—I never gave information that the cap was lost till the prisoner was in custody—I went to see the prosecutor every week—I did not ask the prisoner why he did not come back to lodge at my house, nor say, "You b----, I will do for you"—I go by my own name—I am not called Mrs. Murray—I might be, but I never answer to that name—some persons might call me so—I do not know it—the prisoner gave me five sovereigns the day he was paid, for getting his clothes out of pledge—he had a cap on his head at the time he paid me—he did not owe me 4 l. 10 s., and say he would give me 10 s. more, and that would pay for the cap, and perhaps he might stay a day or two longer.
JOHN DAVIS ( police-constable K 94.) I took the prisoner—he said he had got the cap in bis chest, but it was given to him by Mrs. Sonnex, and he bought the band—I took him to the station—the sergeant sent me back to hit lodging, and I found this cap in his chest—he said he had given the band away, and he had promised to give 7 s. to Sonnex.
Cross-examined. Q. Did he not say Mrs. Sonnex had first lent him the cap, and he had paid her for the band? A. I do not recollect his saying that.
NOT GUILTY .

[trialAccount t18430612-1994]
1994. ELEANOR BOYLAN was indicted for stealing, on the 4th of June, 2 shillings, 3 sixpences, and 5 groats; the monies of James Hayes, from his person, and that she had been before convicted of felony.
JAMES HAYES . I live in White Lion-street, Chelsea. The prisoner was my housekeeper—on Sunday, the 4th of June, I came home, and went to bed—she assisted to undress me, and took the money out of my trowsers pocket—there were two shillings, three sixpences, and five 4 d. pieces—I attempted to put my trowsers under my pillow, but she took the money out before I missed it—I sent her out for a candle, but she stopped out all night—next morning I went in search of her—I heard her voice in another house between five and six o'clock—I brought the police down, and there she was—I counted my money before I went to bed—I was in the bed about two hours before I missed it—I am sure no one could have taken it but her—she has not been with me above six weeks, and never missed sleeping at home before.
Prisoner. Q. Did you not give me your money? A. No—they are not thieves and w----s who lodge in my house, they are married persons and single men—I did not transport my first wife, I buried her at Wolverhampton—I did not come into your bed, you forced yourself into mine—I never wanted to marry you—your own husband is in the workhouse.
BENJAMIN BYRNE ( police-constable B 16.) At half-past five o'clock on Monday morning I went to Lawrence-yard, Royal Hospital-row—the prisoner was in bed with another woman, but the prisoner had her clothes on—the prosecutor said he wanted his 7 s. back—she desired a shoemaker, who was living with the other woman, to send him away—he went away immediately—the other woman said to the prisoner, "He is gone for a policeman now, and he will come and find the money on you"—the prisoner said, "The policeman is here," and I saw her making a motion towards her left stocking—I took her hand, and took two shillings, five sixpences, and three 4 d. pieces in the stocking of her left leg, and two half-crowns in her pocket at the station—she said he gave it her—she said she got the money from her daughter—I heard her make a statement to the Magistrate—I saw the Magistrate sign it—this is it—( read)—"The prisoner says—He put the money into my apron, and wanted to put it into his pocket; I said I would rather put it into mine than yours; he said, 'What is mine is yours, and what is yours is mine;' I spent 1 s. or 2 s.; I did not mean to spend it."
Prisoner's Defence. It is nothing but jealousy; he brought the 7 s., and said, " Nelly, come here;" he said "I can keep you better than a young man;" I said "I do not wish to live with you;" I slept with him and acted as wife.
BENJAMIN BYRNE . I produce a certificate of the prisoner's former conviction, which I got from Mr. Clark's office—( read)—the prisoner is the person.
GUILTY . Aged 43.— Transported for Seven Years.

1996. WILLIAM STRONG was indicted for stealing, on the 23d of May, 1 microscope, value 1 l. 10 s., the goods of Thomas Bassett Matthews.
THOMAS BASSETT MATTHEWS . I live in Athol-place, Pentonville. The prisoner worked for Mr. Weedon—he came to me on the 23rd of May, and said Mr. Weedon would be much obliged to me if I would lend him a microscope, one that he had made for me before, to look at it—I sent it by the prisoner.
MICHAEL WEEDON . I am an optician, and live in Chapel-row. The prisoner formerly lived with me—I did not send him for this microscope on the 23rd of May—he did not live with me then—he has not brought it to me.
HORATIO ANDERSON ( police-constable G 163.) The prisoner came to me on the 13th of June, and said he stole a microscope, and wished to give himself up, as he thought the police were on the alert after him, and he thought he should not be concealed long.
Prisoner. I never went to Mr. Matthews for it.
THOMAS BASSETT MATTHEWS re-examined. I had seen him at work at his master's—I am certain he came for it—I let him have it to look at.
GUILTY . * Aged 24.— Transported for Seven Years.
There was another indictment against the prisoner.

JOHN EDWARD GINGELL . I am a broker, and live in Parish-court, Marylebone. On the 16th of June the prisoner came out of my shop and asked if I wanted a bottle of brandy—I saw something under his coat—I took him back, and he put the time-piece down on the table which he took it from.

1998. JAMES WILLIAMS was indicted for stealing, on the 6th of June, 1 pair of trowsers, value 10 s.; 1 pair of stockings, 2 s.; 2 handkerchiefs, 7 s.; 1 shirt, 2 s.; 1 cravat, 6 d.; 1 ring, 6 d.; 9 sovereigns, 2 half-sovereigns, 7 half-crowns, and 1 sixpence; the property of Thomas White, in a certain boat, upon the Grand Junction Canal.
THOMAS WHITE . About ten o'clock, on the night of the 6th of June, I went to bed in a barge on the canal at West Drayton, Hillingdon—my things were safe in my cupboard a quarter of an hour before I went to bed—I awoke between three and four and missed them—the key of the cupboard was in my trowsers pocket at the back of my head—I have seen the prisoner working on our canal—these are my trowsers—I had a ring like this in my pocket—I believe this to be mine.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. Have you any doubt about them.
A. Not any—I worked hard for the sovereigns—Soldier is a boatman who works about.
ALFRED HUGHES ( police-constable D 174.) I found the prisoner outside the Red Lion, in the Edgeware-road, on the night of the 8th June—I said I should take him on suspicion of stealing 11 l. and a quantity of clothes of Thomas White—he said, "Where?"—I said at West Drayton—he said, well, we could have a drop of beer and settle it—another boatman came out, and the prisoner said, " Soldier, give him a shilling, and say you have not been able to see me"—I said he must go to the station—he said he would not—he sat down—another officer was opposite—I called him, and we took him—I found on him this ring, and part of the property—I then went to No. 7, Union-place, and asked for Mrs. Buck—I said I had come for a bundle that Williams had left her—she produced this bundle in the room on the first floor.
Cross-examined. Q. Did not the prisoner address Soldier particularly about this matter? A. Not in my hearing— Soldier is the nick-name of the boatman.
MARY BUCK . I live with my mother, in Union-place. The prisoner brought this bundle on Wednesdy morning, the 7th of June.
Cross-examined. Q. Do you know this boatman they call Soldier? A. Yes, I have known him for years—he worked where my father does.
GUILTY. Aged 18.— Recommended to mercy.— Judgment Respited.

1999. GEORGE GREEN was indicted for stealing, on the 3rd of June, 1 half-sovereign and 3 sixpences, the monies of John Connor, from his person.
JOHN CONNOR . I am a pensioner of the East India-house, and live in London-street, Ratcliffe. On the 3rd of June I had been to get my pension—I received a half-sovereign and three sixpences—I got tipsy—when I cane to my recollection I missed my money.
GEORGE REEVES . I am apprentice to William Williams, of Cannon-street. On the 3rd of June I saw the prisoner go across Tower-hill, and take the prosecutor, who was drunk, into the Old George—he put his hand under the prosecutor's smock, took something out, and put into his own pocket—I told the policeman, who took him.
WILLIAM DONOVAN . I live in Rosemary-lane. I was on Tower-hill—I saw the prosecutor drunk—the prisoner and five or six more knocked him down, and felt his pockets—they said it was not in that pocket, it was in his other—they then took him to the Old George, and the prisoner took something out of his pocket, and put it into his own.
JOHN DOWDEN ( police-constable H 27.) I found the prosecutor lying on the pavement, opposite the Old George—I asked him if he had lost anything—he said he had, a half-sovereign and two sixpences—I went after the prisoner, and found a half-sovereign and two sixpences in his pocket.
(The prisoner received a good character.)
GUILTY . Aged 25.— Confined Nine Months.

2000. MARY ELLIS and ELIZA JONES were indicted for stealing, on the 16th of June, 1 watch, value 3 l.; 1 hat, 6 s.; I neckerchief, 5 s.; 2 account-books, 3 s.; and 1 half-sovereign; the property of Edward Thomas, from his person.
EDWARD THOMAS . I am a draper, and live in High-street, Whitechapel. I came home in a cab, about two o'clock, in the night of the 16th of June—I got out in Whitechapel, at the Horse and Leaping Bar, opposite my house—I had a glass of brandy and water—when I came out I was accosted by the two prisoners and others—they took me to a house in Duke-street—I was in liquor—I had a watch, a hat, a neck handkerchief, and two account-books—when the policeman came he found me on the floor, and my property was all gone—I have not seen the hat and handkerchief since—these are the other things.
MATILDA BALDWIN . Mrs. Morris, who keeps a lodging-house, came to me for a candle to light her to bed—she said, "I wish you would go and see if my place is all right"—I went, and saw the prosecutor lying across the floor, without a coat, hat, or cravat—two girls, I believe to be the prisoners, were with him—Ellis said they had got a bloak, and he had got no money, that he had been knocked down in Whitechapel.
MICHAEL COLEMAN ( police-constable H 177.) I was called by Baldwin—the prosecutor's coat, hat, and cravat were all gone—the prisoners were with him—Ellis was just rising up from him as he laid on the floor—she took off the floor these books, and gave me—I found the watch in her shawl, which I found between the bed and the wall, after I had taken them to the station.
Ellis's Defence. He was lying down by a court; I spoke to him; he went down the court, and agreed to give me 3 s. for the whole night; he had got no money; his two books and collar of his shirt were lying down by his side; I picked them up and gave them to him; I endeavoured to pick him up, but was not able. When Baldwin came in, she said, "What is the matter?" I said, "I have a tipsy man, and he has no money;" they called in the policeman; he came, and could find nothing; then they took me.
Jones's Defence. We saw him lying down; we went up; he got up, and agreed to go with Ellis. He had got no money. The landlady came in, and said, "Oh, what are you doing with the man?" and she called the police.
ELLIS — GUILTY . * Aged 19.
JONES — GUILTY . Aged 17.
Confined Six Months.

2001. MARY SHEA was indicted for stealing, on the 26th of May, 1 crown, 2 half-crowns, 4 shillings, and 1 sixpence, the monies of John Bartlett.
JOHN BARTLETT being called did not appear.
EMMA HEATH . I slept with John Bartlett on the 25th of May—I saw him with one crown, two half-crowns, and a sixpence, and in the morning it was all gone but three penny pieces—I did not see any one in the room, and the landlady said she had let no one in but the prisoner—I went after the prisoner, and found her in a public-house, and there was a half-crown found in her bosom, and a shilling at the station.
ELIZA MASSERSON . I am landlady of this house, which is in Red Lion-court, Poplar. I had no other room vacant, so I let the prisoner into the room where the prosecutor and Heath were—I bad seen a five-shilling piece, two half-crowns, and some shillings with the prosecutor, when he came home from the play with Heath—I slammed the door to, and no one could have gone in but the prisoner.
WILLIAM PEASLING ( police-constable K 264.) I felt in the prisoner's
bosom, and found half-a-crown—I said I wanted her for robbing a sailor—she said she had got no money at all, and had not robbed him—she said she got the half-crown from a man she had taken home to Mother Monegan's, in Black Lion-lane the night before.
CATHARINE BEALE . I am searcher at the police-station. The prisoner said she had no more money—I found a shilling on her.
Prisoner's Defence. There was a shipmate and another lad in the room; there were three beds. Masserson told me to lie down alongside of them. I came out, and the landlady was in the next house. I went down to this place, and the next morning Bartlett said Heath took the money from him. Masserson came, and said I had been there. I said I had no money. I took out the half-crown, and showed it to him. Heath came in, and said there was no danger; she could not swear I took the money, and that the prosecutor said she took it.
EMMA HEATH re-examined. There were not two other persons in the room—the prosecutor never charged me with taking his money—I never said he did—I went to see the prisoner in prison, because I thought she had no one to notice her.
GUILTY . Aged 33.— Confined Three Months.
[trialAccount t18430612-2002]
2002. DANIEL BARRY and JOHN LEONARD were indicted for stealing, on the 12th of June, 1 handkerchief, value 2 s.; the goods of William Scott; from his person.—2nd COUNT, of a man whose name is unknown.
WILLIAM WEST ( police-constable T 106.) On the 12th of June, about ten o'clock at night, I was on duty, in plain clothes, round Drury-lane Theatre, in consequence of the Queen's going there—I saw the prisoners in the crowd—Barry attempted several pockets—Leonard followed close behind him—I watched them to Drury-lane, and then saw Leonard attempt several pockets—they went on the pavement—a gentleman and lady were coming down—they turned, and followed them—Barry went behind, took something from the gentleman's pocket, and put it into his own trowsers—he waited on the other side, and made a sign for Leonard to come over to him—I then went and spoke to the gentleman—he went to the station, and wrote his name William Scott—I heard him say his name was William Scott, in the prisoner's presence—Barry was taken by Morgan, and I saw this handkerchief fall from him.
Cross-examined by MR. PRENDERGAST. Q. Did the inspector write down the gentleman's name? A. I believe he did—I did not go to his address—the Magistrate committed the prisoner on our evidence—I forget what the gentleman's address was.
JOHN MORGAN . I was requested by West to watch—I saw Barry in Brydges-street attempt several pockets—Leonard was close behind him—I watched them into Drury-lane, and saw them both attempt several gentlemen's pockets—a lady and gentleman were coming down—Barry went immediately behind him, put his hand to the back of his pocket, and then put his hand into his pocket, and took this handkerchief out—I did not hear the gentleman's name—I saw him sign it—I seized Barry, and put my hand to his flap, to get the handkerchief—he drew on the other side, and threw it down on the ground.
Cross-examined. Q. You went to the station-house? A. Yes, I heard all that took place there—I do not know whether the gentleman mentioned his name aloud, or only wrote it down—I went into the yard for a short time, as soon as I put the prisoners into the dock—I am conducting the business for my mother, as a green-grocer, in Covent-garden-market
—I knew West perfectly—I did not know him before he was in the police—I have not been a witness here before—I have at Bow-street, in cases of felony out of the market—it may be four or five times—West has not been the officer in either of the cases.
NOT GUILTY .

2003. CHARLES BRACKELLS was indicted for a misdemeanor.
EDWARD REEVES , (Jun.) I live with my father, Edward Reeves, a bed-sacking maker, in George-street-buildings. On the 4th of May, a lad named Cushion came and said he wanted 4ft. 9in. of bed-sacking, for Mr. Webster, who I dealt with—in consequence of that I gave Cushion the sacking, and some cord for Mr. Webster—I had no knowledge of Brackells—if I had not believed he had been sent for Mr. Webster, I should not have given it him.
JOHN CUSHION . I met the prisoner as I was going up Old-street—he asked me to go to Mr. Reeves, and ask for the bed-sacking—I went, and said, "Please I want 4ft. 9in. of bed-sacking for Mr. Webster," and they give it me—I gave it to the prisoner—I am sure he is the person.
CHARLES WEBSTER . I did not send the prisoner or Cushion for this bed-sacking or cord, nor receive either—the prisoner lived with me for two years—he was a very honest boy during that time—I think it was through distress.
GUILTY . Aged 18.— Confined Two Months.

[trialAccount t18430612-2006]
2006. RICHARD ANDERSON was indicted for embezzlement.
MR. BALLANTINE conducted the Prosecution.
FREDERICK HENRY WEST . The prisoner was in the employ of the Eastern Counties Railway—it was his duty to receive money, and issue tickets, at the Stratford station—he cut them from a book—there was a duplicate of each ticket left in the book—it was his duty to stamp the duplicate, and put his
initials on it—it was his duty to account, on Monday morning, the 29th, for the receipts of Saturday and Sunday, the 27th and 28th of May—he ought to have forwarded it by the guard of the first train, or the second at the latest—I received this letter, bearing the Birmingham post-mark—on the 30th of May I went down to Birmingham—the prisoner was in custody, and was handed over to me—in travelling to the station at Shoreditch, I said, "I am afraid this will be a bad job"—he said, "I am very sorry, I can't help it; I lost the money"—I asked how—he said he left the station on Sunday evening, and put the bulk of the money in silver into his pocket, and two sovereigns in another pocket—he was going to the westend of the town, to see his brother, and got on an omnibus, that after he left it he found the bag and silver was gone—some half-crowns and other money was produced to me at Birmingham—he had a right to take the money from the station for security—he would be answerable for it.
BENJAMIN RICHARDSON . I am police-inspector of the railway station at Stratford. On Sunday, the 28th of May, I saw the prisoner in private clothes, about a quarter to nine o'clock—he was allowed to be in private clothes after duty, which was over—next morning he did not come—I went into the office, and found a bunch of keys on the table—I took them up, and opened the till, and the money was not there—it was not kept in the till at night—this letter is in the prisoner's hand-writing—( read)—"To Mr. Superintendant West, Eastern Counties Railway.—Birmingham, May 29th.—Sir, I am sorry to inform you I lost about 6 l., the principal part of the money taken for tickets at the Stratford station from my pocket, on Tuesday evening, where I cannot say. I went into the country, to endeavour to make the money good, but have not been able to do so. I beg to state, sir, that I shall be in town to-morrow, and will call to give you the best account in my power. I am, sir, &c. ROBERT ANDERSON."
ROBERT COOPER . I am guard of the Eastern Counties Railway. It was my duty on Monday morning, the 29th of May, to bring the bags from the different stations—I called at Stratford, and found no cash there—the prisoner was not there—it was his duty to be there early to account to me.
Prisoner's Defence. The money was stolen from me. I went to Birmingham for the purpose of making it good, and wrote to the superintendent to inform him.
GUILTY. Aged 30.— Recommended to mercy. — Confined Three Months.
Before Edward Bullock, Esq.

[trialAccount t18430612-2011]
2011. FRANCES ANDERSON was indicted for stealing, on the 19th of May, 9 sovereigns, and 7 half-sovereigns; the monies of Thomas Bland, from his person.
MR. CLARKSON conducted the Prosecution.
THOMAS BLAND . I am a cheesemonger, and live at Woolwich. On the 19th of May, about one o'clock in the morning, I was returning from Greenwich to Woolwich; I had just got beyond the-dock, and near Air-street, where I live, the prisoner ran right up to me, meeting me—she caught hold of me, said, "Where are you going?" putting her arms round me—I said, "I am going home"—she said, "Come along with me"—in the mean time the got her hand into my pocket, and my purse was taken out in a few minutes—it contained 33 l. in sovereigns and half-sovereigns—she kept the purse on the side of her—I saw it in her hand, and said, "You have got my money"—she said, "Yes, come along, it is all right"—I said, "Give me my parse"—she said, "Come home with me and I will, it will be all right," and dragged me along the street, saying, "D—n the policemen, come home with me, it will be all right"—I had been drinking myself, and cannot say whether she had or not—I asked her all the way for the purse—she would not give it up—she had an opportunity of taking money out on the road, as she kept shuffling it about, and before I got to the door I heard the money jink—I caught hold of the purse, as she knocked at the door, and took it out of her hand—Parry, who kept the house, got up to get a light—she wanted me to go up stain—I said, "Stop a bit, let me see that my money is right"—I counted the money on the table, and found 12 l. 10 s. short—she still said, "Come up, it is all right"—I took hold of her—Parry and his wife were a long time arguing with me, saying, "Look at the disgracefulness of the thing, Mr. Bland, what will your wife say?"—I held her till a policeman came.
JACOB DAVIS ( policeman.) In consequence of information, I went to Parry's house, and found the prosecutor holding the prisoner—I took her to the station, and there she said to him, "I am aware I took your money from your pocket, but I returned it to you again."
NOT GUILTY .

2017. LEWIS SIMMONDS and WILLIAM KITCHEN were indicted for stealing, on the 7th of June, 1 handkerchief, value 2 s. 6 d., the goods of a man whose name was unknown, from his person.
JOHN CARPENTER ( police-constable R 84.) On Wednesday evening, the 7th of June, between seven and eight o'clock, I was in King-street, Greenwich, and saw the two prisoners following two gentlemen, who were arm-in-arm—Simmonds went up behind one of the gentlemen, followed by Kitchen, who spread open his round smock-frock close behind Simmonds, covering him, to hide what he was doing—I looked over Simmonds's shoulder, and saw his left hand go into the gentleman's left pocket—it was a Chesterfield wrapper—he took out a handkerchief and gave it to Kitchen, who put it inside his round frock—I afterwards found it in his breeches pocket—I found this other handkerchief in the back of Kitchen's breeches, which had a hole in them, and this other handkerchief I found round his neck—I spoke to the gentleman at the time—he said he had lost his handkerchief, and would follow me to the station, but he did not—I have not been able to find him.
Kitchen. I gave half-a-crown for that handkerchief in Rosemary-lane on Wednesday morning—I never saw any thing of Simmonds till I got to the fair. Witness. The one I found in his breeches he said he bought in Petticoat-lane on Sunday morning, and that he gave half-a-crown for the other on Wednesday morning—I asked him if there was any mark on this handkerchief—he said there was not, and there is a very large "T K" on it—I had followed them for about a quarter of an hour or twenty minutes, and saw Simmonds on one occasion lift up a gentleman's pocket to feel if there was any thing in it.
Simmonds's Defence. He did not say any thing about the handkerchief being marked till he was at the police-court next morning, and then he said he spoke to the gentleman, and he had said it was marked.
SIMMONDS — GUILTY . Aged 19.
KITCHEN — GUILTY . Aged 22.
Confined Three Months.

2018. CHARLES SIMMONDS was indicted for stealing, on the 6th of June, 1 handkerchief, value 2 s., the goods of Henry Edward Campbell, from his person.
HENRY EDWARD CAMPBELL . I am a painter, and live in Dartmouth-street. I was at Greenwich fair on Tuesday, the 6th of June—I had two handkerchiefs—I missed one when the officer informed me—this is it—it is soiled with paint—I did not see the prisoner till he was in custody.
JOHN CARPENTER . I am a policeman. About half-past nine o'clock on Tuesday night I was on duty in the fair, and saw the prisoner go behind two gentlemen, who were looking at Richardson's show—the gentlemen moved
on about twenty yards, and I saw the handkerchief hanging out of the prosecutor's pocket—the prisoner then went up, took the handkerchief out, and was walking away, when I took him with it, and told the prosecutor—I found this other handkerchief round the prisoner's neck, and these white gloves, a key, and kaleidescope on him.
Prisoner. It was not me that took the handkerchief, it was a young chap who stood before me; he ran through the mob, and the policeman took me; they picked the handkerchief off the ground. Witness. He said nothing of the sort till his second examination—there was no second person near him—I have made inquiries, and find he bore a good character up to three weeks ago.
GUILTY. Aged 17.— Recommended to mercy. — Confined One Month, the first and last week Solitary.

2019. SARAH EMERY and ELIZA DUNCAN were indicted for stealing, on the 5th of June, 1 watch, value 30 s.; 1 key, 5 s.; and 1 ribbon, 6 d.; the goods of David Darling.
ANN DARLING . I am the wife of David Darling, and occupy part of a house in Short's-alley, Woolwich. Mr. Grove, the landlord, is a relation of Emery's, and lodges in the same house—on the 5th of June, between four and five o'clock, Emery came and inquired for her brother's wife—she came again soon after, and came into the yard where I was—she would pass her brother's door and mine—I have the back room, and her brother the front—her brother's family were out—I had my husband's watch in my room—I had seen it safe about a quarter of an hour before—I missed it directly Emery was gone—this is it—I saw no one but Emery there—I left my door a little way open when I went into the yard.
Cross-examined by MR. HORRY. Q. While you were talking with her in the yard, any one else standing in the passage might have gone into your room? A. She was only momentarily speaking to me—her brother's room was fastened—I do not know whether she went to his door—she asked if his wife was at home—I said she was not—she had to pass my door, both in coming in and going out—she frequently came there—I did not follow her for two or three minutes after she left the yard.
THOMAS MYDDLETON . I am a butcher, and live at Woolwich. Between four and five o'clock on Monday afternoon, I saw the two prisoners talking together close by a public-house, not five yards from the prosecutor's house-Duncan had a basket—I was afterwards at the station, and heard the watch drop from Duncan's person—I saw the prisoners come direct from the house.
WILLIAM GLADWIN . I am a policeman. I took the prisoners—I told them they were charged on suspicion of stealing a watch—they both denied it—Emery said, "Good God! Mrs. Duncan, you have got me into a nice thing"—Duncan said, "I know nothing about it"—I took them to the station—as a female was about to search them, I heard the watch drop from Duncan—she said, "Mrs. Emery gave me the watch before we entered the station door"—Emery said, "For God's sake, Mrs. Duncan, ease me with it, (or words to that effect,) and I will do all I can for you."
Cross-examined. Q. Did she not say something about forgiving her for making such a charge? A. She did—there was a good deal of recrimination—Emery had a basket—I know Mr. Jeremy's hand writing—this is his signature—this was read over to the prisoners as what they stated—( The prisoners' examination being read, Emery stated that she accompanied Duncan to purchase a pair of shoes for her child, that she went to the prosecutor's house to see her sister; and went into the yard, leaving Duncan in the passage, and when she returned, Duncan was gone; that she overtook her, and knew nothing of the watch till Duncan dropped it at the station. Duncan stated, that when Emery went to the prosecutor's house, she remained ten or twelve yards from the door, and was never in the house; that when Emery came out, she gave her the watch, and asked her to pawn it.)
NOT GUILTY .
