2023. JOHN JONES was indicted for stealing, on the 29th of May, at Lewisham, 1 coat, value 2 l. 10 s.; and 2 pairs of shoes, 5 s.; the goods of Frederick Kidman Parkinson: 2 shirts, 10 s., the goods of William Newman: 1 pair of trowsers, 20 s., the goods of James Parkinson: 1 scarf, 10 s.; 2 pairs of gloves, 1 s.; 1 box, 1 s.; 1 watch, 15 l.; 1 seal, 2 l. 10 s.; 1 watch-chain, 20 s.; 1 key, 10 s.; 1 guard-chain, 8 l.; 1 pin, 5 l.; and 1 coat, 2 l. 10 s.; the goods of George Thomas Parkinson: and 2 forks, 5 s., the goods of George Heath Parkinson, in his dwelling-house.
GEORGE THOMAS PARKINSON . I live at Evergreen-lodge, Lewisham, with my father, George Heath Parkinson. On Sunday, the 28th of May, I went to bed about half-past eleven o'clock, leaving an enamelled watch, a gold chain, and a gold pin, on my drawers—I got out of bed at two o'olock in the morning, to see what time it was, and missed the watch and chain—I did not miss the pin till I dressed myself in the morning—I was not disturbed.
JAMES PARKINSON . I am the witness's brother, and live with my father. I was at home on the night of the 29th of May—I am not aware that anything disturbed me, but I awoke about a quarter to two o'clock, got out of bed, and looked at my watch, which was safe then—about half an hour afterwards I was in bed, and had not gone to sleep again—I fancied I heard a noise of somebody walking up the back staircase—I listened about two minutes, and was then almost satisfied that I heard somebody go into the billiard-room—I got out of bed, and went into the billiard-room with a light—not finding anybody, I went down stairs, searched the house—I found nobody, and returned to my room—I got up, about half-past three, as I was going out—it was quite light then—I looked out of my window, and saw the prisoner in our meadow, adjoining our garden—I watched him—he was standing under a tree—I saw him pass over a stile in the rick-yard, and walk towards the gate of our stable yard—I went down, and met him at the gate—he had a bundle—I asked what business he had there at that time in the morning—he said he had lost his way across the fields coming from Bexley—I said he ought to be ashamed of himself for walking across people's grass in that way, and ordered him off the premises—he took the bundle with him—about four o'clock I was going to Farningham, and missed a great coat which had hung in the hall—I then suspected he had stolen it—I immediately went round and gave information to all the policemen I could find, and described him—I then went to Farningham—I returned in the evening, and found him in custody at New-cross, and the same bundle was in the custody of the
inspector—I afterwards examined my father's premises, and found under the billiard-room window the marks of a ladder, which corresponded with the feet of a ladder on the premises—the billiard-room window, I believe, had not been fastened at night—I did not go inside the billiard-room when I got up—I merely opened the door, and looked under the table—I examined the bundle which the inspector had, and found property in it which I recognise—here is the bundle—I know all the property in it—here is the watch belonging to my mother—it has been in my brother's possession some time—this is the coat I missed, and here are my trowsers, which I know were in the house the night before—here is another coat belonging to my brother—the prisoner did not appear at all intoxicated—it was Monday morning.
JOHN WHITE ( policeman.) On the morning of the 29th of May, about five o'clock, I was on duty at New-cross, and saw the prisoner coming along with a bundle under his arm—I asked him what it contained—he said a coat—I asked where he brought it from—he said from Dartford—I asked where from there—he said his master—I asked where he was going to take it—he said to London, to his master's house—I said, "The coat on your back does not belong to you"—he said, "No, it belongs to my master"—I said, "Does he allow you to wear his clothes?"—he said, "Being a cold morning, he said I might put it on"—I asked if he had anything else in the bundle—he said, "Two pairs of shoes"—I took him to the station with the bundle, and found all these things in it—I said I would go to town with him to his master, to know whether he had authorised him to bring the things to London or not—I tied up the bundle—he said, "It is no use to go there, I picked it up in a ditch, on Bexley-heath"—I then searched him, and found this gold watch in his pocket, this coat on his back, and the rest of the things tied up in the handkerchief—next morning I showed the things to young Mr. Parkinson.
BENJAMIN LOVELL ( police-sergeant.) I was at the station at Greenwich—after the prisoner had been locked up there three or four hours, I examined him, and found this pin stuck inside his jacket, and these two forks in his righthand trowsers-pocket.
GEORGE THOMAS PARKINSON re-examined. This gold watch was the one I missed that night—it was my mother's—this gold pin and watch-chain is mine, and this coat—it was in the house the night before—this blue handkerchief belongs to me, and this coat belongs to my brother Frederick.
Prisoner's Defence. As I was coming to town this morning, I saw the bundle lying on Bexley-heath by the side of the road. I came across the fields on my road to London, and got out of the path, where the gentleman saw me.
GUILTY . Aged 30.— Transported for Ten Years.
Before Mr. Baron Gurney.
[trialAccount t18430612-2024]
2024. JOHN MINCH was charged on the Coroner's Inquisition only with feloniously killing James Brown.
JAMES RACE . On the 20th of May, between nine and ten o'clock in the day, I was on board the Lord Cook schooner, steering—I am the captain—the wind was south-east-east, a fresh breeze—I was making from the north shore to the south shore, to the upper part of Half-way reach, which is beyond Woolwich, going down the river—I saw a boy in a Peter-boat—I had towed him from Woolwich—he let go of his own accord, and appeared to be pulling down the river—I saw a ballast-lighter, belonging to the Trinity-house, coming down the river, astern of me—I did not notice how many men were on board—I saw a man at the helm—I did not notice whether there were more than one—she was going from the north shore to the south—there were
several vessels tacking about, but none to interfere with its motion, until the accident occurred—I did not hear an alarm, but saw the ballast-lighter in contact with the Peter-boat—the boat went down, and the boy in it—I saw the wreck of the boat afterwards, but never saw the boy again—a smack sent off a boat towards the wreck—the lighter did not stop or put about, but proceeded on its course—I cannot say whether the people on board knew what had taken place, but some time after I saw a boat proceed from the ballast-lighter—the boy was rowing against a head-wind—it was a smart breeze—the vessel was under command—I saw some people on deck immediately after—I was not noticing it till the accident occurred—if there had been a look-out kept I should think they must have seen the boat—the boy was master of his boat—he was going down with the tide—I should suppose be could get out of the way of danger, if he had either stopped hit boat, or pulled along—the vessel was sailing across the river—I do not know who was navigating her.
Cross-examined by MR. PRENDERGAST. Q. Was not the main-sail of the lighter towards the boat? A. Yes, between the boat and the person steering the lighter—if she had put her helm round she would have got the boat out in a shorter space—I heard no cry out—I was some distance off—there were several vessels tacking about—it required management to steer—the Peter-boat was still going down the river—I do not know whether the boy tried to get a tow of the ballast-lighter—if the boy had stopped his boat in time he might have got out of the way.
LABIN TILBROOK . I am a fisherman, and live at Greenwich. I belong to a fishing-smack, in which I was sailing down the river with eight others—I saw the boy in the boat rowing down the river—we stood to the south—we asked him how far he was going—he said, "To Rainham"—my brother, who is master of the smack, said he would give him a tow if he pulled alongside, but he could not catch us—we saw no more till a boy in our boat said, "Oh, master, he is run over"—I and another man hauled out our boat directly, and when I got within two strokes of him he went down—I put the oar down into the water, but could not feel him—at that time the lighter's boat came to the spot, within a minute or two—I think if they had heaved the vessel round directly, and jumped into the boat they might have saved the lad, but they kept right on till they got right over the river—they did not heave about directly—we saw hands on board—if there had been a look-out, I think it might have been prevented—I saw the wreck of the Peter-boat—I do not know who navigated the lighter.
Crosss-examined. Q. A ballast-lighter does not answer the helm so well as yours? A. Yes; it will come round quicker in a brisk wind, as there was, but not generally speaking—I thought they lost time—they were a good distance behind us—I knew the boy, his name was James Brown.
GEORGE TILBROOK . I am master of the smack, I saw the boy in the Peter-boat, and spoke to him a few minutes before the accident occurred—I was walking the deck, and the lad said, "The boy is run over"—I turned, and saw the boat—I consider, if the lighter had been looking out, it might have been prevented—I can venture to say, if I had been looking out in my boat, and they told me they saw a boat in time, I could have cleared her—it is the duty always to keep a look-out—they got out a boat afterwards.
Cross-examined. Q. Their boat was not long after yours? A. No—it might have been the act of a moment—I had spoken to the boy three or four minutes before—there was no Vessel in the way to obstruct just at that time.
WILLIAM BLACKBURN . This boat belongs to my brother—the boy worked for me—he left me at Woolwich on the 20th of May about seven o'clock in
the morning, going to Rainham—he was able to navigate such a boat—the body was afterwards found.
JOSEPH BRITTEN . I was in the ballast-lighter on this day—the prisoner was steering her—I saw the Peter-boat when we were a yard or two from it, and called to the prisoner—he did not hear me—I called again, and told him to look out forward for the boat—he answered me, but I did not hear what he said, it blew so hard—I then saw the boat come in contact with the lighter—I directly ran after the boat-hook, and when I came forward the boat was gone—directly afterwards I and the prisoner got into the boat—the man who chucked the chain dropped it overboard, and we could not haul it in—we lost time by that—I saw nothing to blame in the prisoner—we supposed the boy was going to take a tow, as he tried to take a tow of the lighter a-head of him, and the man would not allow him, because he was afraid he would be pulled out of the boat.
NOT GUILTY .
Before Mr. Common Sergeant.

2025. THOMAS SKILLIN, RICHARD SMITH , and SARAH DAVIS , were indicted for feloniously assaulting Benjamin Lovell, on the 20th of May, and cutting and wounding him on his right hand, with intent to do him grievous bodily harm.—2nd COUNT, with intent to resist their lawful apprehension and detainer.
BENJAMIN LOVELL . I am a policeman. On the night of the 20th of May, about a quarter after one o'clock, I heard a cry of "Murder" and "Police" up Eldon's-buildings, Flagon-row, Deptford—I went up to the place—there were several constables there—I turned a light on, and saw the top of the door was broken, and saw the three prisoners, and a man and woman down stairs in a room—Skillin made a cut at Douglas, the inspector, and Smith threw a brick and hit me in the face—I got up to the door-post, turned my light on, and saw Skillin knock his father down with what I thought was a sword, but it was a chopper, and he struck him several times when he was down with the iron chopper—his mother laid hold of him and said, "For God's sake, don't"—he immediately hit her on the wrist with the chopper, and she fell—they were all drunk—Smith struck them both while they were down—seeing them bleeding, I broke in the bottom of the door and entered—Skillin immediately hit me with this bill-hook on the head, and knocked me down on the bed in the room—he must have hit me rather sideways, as it cut the top of my hat—in endeavouring to get out he cut me over the hand—Smith struck me four or five times with a poker while I was down—the poker did not make a wound, it only bruised my side, and blood came from my side—there was no incision—two more constables came into the house—Smith and Davis ran up stairs—I went and took them—this is the poker Smith struck me with.
EDWARD DOWNING . I am a surgeon. I dressed the prosecutor's wound which was on the knuckle of his middle finger.
WILLIAM THORN . I am a policeman. I went to the house with Lovell—he went into the door, and was knocked down by Skillin with this bill-hook, and after he was down, Smith struck him several times with the poker.
JOSIAH VINCENT . I am a policeman. From information I received I went to the house—I did not see Lovell knocked down, but after he was down I saw him receive several blows—I cannot say who from.
Skillin. I am very sorry it occurred.
SKILLIN — GUILTY .
SMITH — GUILTY .
Of an Assault.— Confined Six Months.
DAVIS — NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18430612-2026]
2026. THOMAS SKILLIN, RICHARD SMITH , and SARAH DAVIS , were again indicted for feloniously assaulting Stephen Skillin, on the 20th of May, and cutting and wounding him on his left arm, with intent to do him some grievous bodily harm.
ESTHER HILL . I am the wife of Thomas Hill. On Saturday night, the 20th of May, I heard cries of "Murder," went to the bottom of the court, and took two policemen to Stephen Skillin's house—when the door was opened Davis was on the bed, on the top of Stephen Skillin, beating him with her hand—he was drunk—his wife was lying on the floor—Davis shut the door, and said, "There are only two of those b----s, we will soon do for them"—the other two prisoners were in the room with her—there was a great noise, and the policemen got in—after they were got out there were brick-bats flying—Mrs. Skillin is since dead—she was very ill after this, from a blow she received in the scuffle—her finger was broken.
STEPHEN SKILLIN . I was at home on the night of the 20th of May, in bed alongside my wife—my son was out drinking with Smith and Davis, who is my daughter-in-law—she was the instigation of all this disturbance—I was not given to drink, but they coaxed me to come and have some beer with them that night—I was beat and pulled out of bed—I do not recollect being struck or beaten by my son.
CAROLINE MACE . I heard Davis beating her father on the bed with her hand in the evening—Thomas Skillin and Smith were not in the room at that time.
EDWARD DOWNING . I am a surgeon. I dressed four lacerated wounds on Stephen skillin's left arm.
DAVIS — GUILTY of an Assault. — Confined Six Months.
SKILLIN and SMITH — NOT GUILTY .

2027. THOMAS SKILLIN, RICHARD SMITH , and SARAH DAVIS were again indicted for stealing, on the 20th of May, 2 shillings, 2 sixpences, 30 pence, and 46 halfpence; the monies of Stephen Skillin.
STEPHEN SKILLIN . On the night of the 20th of May I had 7 s. 2 1/2 d. in copper, and 2 s. in silver, and two sixpences wrapped in a piece of paper, which I intended to give my old woman in the morning when she was sober—the 2 s. were black—I put it all in the cellar, behind the water-butt, in the evening—I afterwards went down to look at the rabbits, and the money was gone, but I was so intoxicated I did not know what I was about.
Skillin. Q. Did not I ask you for some money to get beer, and did not you say if I went down in the cellar, and looked behind the water-butt, I should find some there? A. I do not remember, I was so tipsy.
CAROLINE MACE . I heard the prosecutor say he should go and feed his rabbits—he went into the cellar, came up, and went in doors—the old lady fell down—I saw the three prisoners go down into the cellar, and come away—I think the old man told them the money was there.
NOT GUILTY .
Before Mr. Recorder.
[trialAccount t18430612-2028]
2028. ABRAHAM CUNNINGHAM was indicted for stealing, on the 12th of March, 15 l. promissory-note, the property of our Lady the Queen.—2nd Count, stating it to be the property of Thomas Campbell.—3rd Count, stating it to be the property of Thomas Reynolds.—4th Count, stating it to be the property of Joseph Handley.—5th COUNT, stating it to be the property of Elizabeth Laing Campbell.
MESSRS. ADOLPHUS, WADDINOTON, and GURNEY, conducted the Prosecution.
ELIZABETH LAING CAMPBELL . I am the wife of Thomas Campbell, and
live in Kerr-street, Edinburgh. on February last my husband was sent on board the hulks at Woolwich—before he surrendered to take his trial, at Edinburgh, he gave me 25 l. in 5 l. notes of the Bank of Scotland—on the 8th of March I received a letter, purporting to be written in my husband's name—this is it.—( This letter was signed Thomas Campbell, dated on board the Warrior, complaining of the severity of the weather, representing himself to be in a deplorable condition; that 300 men were to be sent abroad in a fortnight; that the man set over him at work had the power of keeping him in the country, and shortly getting him his liberty; that they were not allowed to have money if known, and requesting his wife to send 5 l. by return of post, which would be the means of his getting liberated in twelve months; that she must never mention what she had done in any future letters, and that it must be sent in a parcel, directed for James M'Greggor, to be left at the Edinburgh Castle, Woolwich, till called for.)—On receiving this I immediately wrote to my husband, and took a 5 l. note to Mr. James, a neighbour, who assisted me to put it in the letter—it was folded and placed between two pieces of oil-cloth, wrapped in paper, sealed, and tied with a string, directed "James M'Greggor, Edinburgh Cattle, Woolwich, to be left till called for"—Mr. James directed it in my presence, and curried it to the post-office—the letter I received is not my husband's writing—he can write—eight days afterwards I received this other letter.—( This letter merely acknowledged the receipt of the 5 l. note, and desired her never to mention in any future letter having sent it.)—I afterwards received other letters of the same description, in consequence of which I applied to the Procurator Fiscal in April—he wrote a letter, put his initials to it, and I put mine—this is the letter—it is directed "Mr. M'Greggor, Shakspeare Inn, Power-street, Woolwich," as that was desired in this letter I received, dated the 1st of May.—( This acknowledged the receipt of a letter of the 29th of April, stating that his friend Mr. M'Greggor had sent a petition to Sir James Graham, that he should not leave the country, and requesting her to send what money she coould, as it would do great things for him; that he had got a friend to write, not being allowed to write himself, and again cautioning her not to mention in sub-sequent letters having sent any money, and to direct for Mr. M'Greggor at the Shakspeare.)
COURT. Q. How came you to send the money, if you did not before the letters to be your husband's writing? A. Because the letters desired me to do so, and mentioned the assistance of a kind gentleman.
WILLIAM JAMES . I am a shoemaker, and live at Edinburgh. Early in March, Mrs. Campbell called on me, and I assisted her in making up a letter—it was, perhaps, the 8th or 9th of March, and a 5 l. note of the Bank of Scotland was put into it, which she gave me—I directed that letter to "James M'Greggor, Edinburgh-Castle, Woolwich, to be left till called for,"—at Mrs. Campbell's request I took it to the General Post-office, and paid 6 d. for it.
MRS. CAMPBELL cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. Where did you get this 5 l. note from? A. My husband, who drew it from the saving's bank.
JAMES OVER . I am a postman at Woolwich. The Edinburgh Castle is in my beat—I was on that beat in March last—I remember delivering a parcel at the Edinburgh Castle in that month, directed, "James M'Greggor, Edinburgh Castle, Woolwich, to be left till called for"—I cannot say what day it was—I delivered it to Mrs. Reynolds, the landlady—it was rather a thick letter—a remark was made about it at the time.
FRANCES REYNOLDS . My husband keeps the Edinburgh Castle, Woolwich. I remember having a letter in my possession, directed to M'Greggor, to be be left at the Edinburgh Castle—I do not remember taking it from Over—I have known the prisoner two or three months—he had been in the
habit of coming to our house—I never knew hit name till the letter came—before it arrived he had inquired if there was a letter left there for him—I said not—he said would I take one in if it came—I asked hit name—he laid, "M'Greggor"—he did not call after the letter arrived, and I gave it to Joseph Handley, my pot-boy, to take down to his house—I knew where he lived—he had called a day or two before it arrived.
JOSEPH HANDLIY . In March last I was pot-boy at the Edinburgh Gastle—mistress gave me a brown paper parcel, tied round with string—by her direction I took it to No. 1, Brockley-street, Woolwich—a woman came to the door—I asked her if Mr. M'Greggor lived there—she said no such person lived there, and shut the door—the prisoner then came out, closed to the door, and said, "It is for me"—I gave it him—I knew him before by sight, but not by name.
JOHN MULLIS REYNOLDS . I am shopman to Mr. Robinson, a grocer at Woolwich—he keeps the post-office there—I have seen the prisoner at master's—he used to coins) often, to inquire whether there any letters directed to the Post-office for him—he did not give his name, but I knew it to be Cunningham—he once asked me to give him change for a 15 l. Bank of Scotland note, which he bad in his hand—I did not take it out of his hand—I said I could not give him change—he asked me where I could recommend him to get change—I said I thought he could get it at the Bank by paying something.
LEWIS DAVIS . I am a pawnbroker, and live at Woolwich. On the 13th of March the prisoner came to my shop with a 5 l. Scotch note, saying he wished it changed—we are silversmiths and exchangers of foreign money as well—he said he was told he could get it changed here—I changed it for him, and gave him 4 l. 15 s.—I said we did not usually chaige them, unless they purchased something—he said he did not want to purchase anything—I said, "It will be 5 s. expense to you, you had better send it back "—he said he would readily pay for it rather than send it back, and I gave him 4 l. 15 s.—it is not payable in London—I am under the impression that I asked his name, and wrote it on the note—I rather think it was Cunningham—I paid the note away to my bankers, the Commercial Bank of London.
HENRY MASTERMAN . I am principal officer of the Warrior convict ship, at Woolwich. I have a Thomas Campbell in my custody there, and he was so in Match last—he came on the 13th of Feb.—the prisoner might possibly have had communication with Campbell in the yard, but not in the ship—I do not believe he ever had any communication with him—it was not to my knowledge—Parry brought the prisoner on board, and confronted him with Campbell—Parry asked Campbell if he ever authorised the prisoner to write to his friends for money—he said, "I never saw him, I never had any conversation with him, and have not authorised him at any time"—the prisoner made no reply—he had ample time to do so if he chose—he was there some minnles—I knew the prisoner by the name of Abraham Cunningham.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSOK. Q. How long have you been in your situation? A. I have had the sole charge since the 5th of Oct., but I have been engaged there fifteen years—I have known the prisoner two years and a half—I knew nothing against him previous to this—there are three convict ships at Woolwich—the same three are there now—there have not been any convicts sent abroad from the Warrior since the 29th of March—I know nothing of any other vessels—I have a register of the names of the prisoners on board—there are better than seven hundred on board the Warrior now; only one of the name of Campbell, I am certain—I know of no other name of Campbell—I have no other person of that name in my custody—I know nothing of the
other ships—I have not heard of any persons being suspended for writing letters for prisoners.
WILLIAM LEE . I am a convict keeper at Woolwich. Thomas Campbell was under my care in March last—I know the prisoner—I never saw him in conversation with Campbell while he was under my charge, which was from the 14th or 15th of February last, up to the present time—he came on board on the 13th—he could not communicate with him in the day-time, while under my eye.
Cross-examined. Q. You do not mean to say two persons never have communication together? A. I mean I never saw the prisoner have any conversation with Campbell—he might have done so while I was away.
JAMES PARRY . I am a police-sergeant. On the 9th of May I went to the Shakspeare public-house, at Woolwich—between six and seven o'clock in the evening the prisoner came to the bar and asked the landlord if there was a letter there in the name of M'Greggor—Bland, the landlord, said, "Yes," and asked him how he came to have his letters directed there without his leave—I did not hear his answer—Bland said, "How am I to know you are the person? is your name M'Greggor?"—the prisoner said, "Yes"—Bland said, "I must have a receipt for it; it may contain money, for aught I know"—the prisoner said, "Very well, I will give you a receipt"—I stepped forward and asked him who authorised him to send or write to Mrs. Campbell for money—he said, "Her brother"—I then said, "You must go with me"—I allowed him to take possession of the letter, and took it from him directly—on the way to the station he said, "Can't this be settled, sergeant, without its going any further?"—I said, "No; I don't know the exact particulars of the case, but it is said you had 5 l. before"—he said, "Yes; I am a runed man; what a fool I must be"—I asked him where her brother was—he said, "On board our hulks; I mean her husband"—I asked him who wrote the letters—he said, "A man named Jones, who went away in the last Bay ship," (meaning Botany Bay)—I asked him what he did with the money—he said Jones had it—I said, "You did all this for nothing, then"—he said, "Only for good nature"—I found this little book on him, and all these letters—( the book contained entries of different names, among them "Campbell, Edinburgh, Scotland. Shakspeare Inn, Power-street. Campbell, Edinburgh Scotland, to be left at the Shakspeare Inn, Woolwich, till called for. 22nd April")
MRS. CAMPBELL re-examined. I have got one of the notes here, similar to the one I put into the letter—this is one my husband gave me—these notes are in common circulation in Edinburgh—they were all five from the same bank and the same form.
MR. CLARKSON called
THOMAS WOOLF . I am a convict keeper at Woolwich. I have known the prisoner two years and a half—he always bore an honest character, and was a humane man towards the convicts—I know that convict keepers and officers in the hulks have been suspended for being parties to sending letters—about eighteen months ago, one Hubbard was suspended for six weeks for receiving 5 l. from a prisoner's friend, till it was made good to the friends again—I have known parties discharged for sending communications from prisoners to their friends—I have detected two myself—we are not allowed to send or receive letters for prisoners without communicating with the authorities—it does not require much management to conceal it.
GUILTY . Aged 36.— Transported for Seven Years.
Before Mr. Recorder.

2032. JAMES TOHEY and JAMES GEORGE were indicted for stealing, on the 6th of June, 1 handkerchief, value 4 d., the goods of a man unknow, from his person.

On the 6th of June, I was in Greenwich fair, and saw the prisoners together—I saw George put his hand into several gentlemen's pockets, and at length he took a handkerchief from a gentleman's pocket, and passed it to Tohey, who was behind him—I endeavoured to get to the gentlemen, but the crowd came between us and he got away—I took the prisoners afterwards—I found two handkerchiefs on Tohey, and I believe this is the one that George took from the gentleman—I found a small knife in George's hand, which was open—I had been watching them through the fair for half an hour.

GEORGE — GUILTY . Aged 18.

2035. MICHAEL CAVANNAH was indicted for uttering counterfeit coin.
MR. BODKIN conducted the Prosecution.
MARGARET KENSEY . I keep the Castle public-house at Eltham, in Kent. On the evening of the 12th of May, the prisoner came for half a pint of porter—it came to 1 d.—he tendered me 6 d.—I saw it was bad—I returned it, and he went away.
LOUISA HORD . I keep the Greyhound at Eltham. On the 12th of May, about a quarter before eight o'clock in the evening, the prisoner came for half a pint of porter—he gave me 6 d.—I appealed to a customer, who said it was bad—I returned it, and he went away.
ELIZABETH COPPER . My husband keeps a beer shop at Eltham. Between seven and eight o'clock in the evening of Friday, the 12th of May, the prisoner came and asked for half a pint of beer—he gave me a 6 d.—I gave him 5 d. change—I put that 6 d. in the till—there was no other there—in a few minutes Mitchell came for a pint of beer, and wanted change for a 1 s.—I gave him in change the 6 d. I had taken from the prisoner—it was the only 6 d. in the till—I am confident it was the same—the policeman came in while Mitchell was in the house, and Mitchell produced the 6 d. to him.
WILLIAM MITCHELL . I am messenger to the master shipwrights at Woolwich. I was at Copper's house, and got the 6 d. in change for a 1 s.—soon after, the policeman came in—he asked the landlady about this, and I said "Perhaps I have got it"—I had no other 6 d. but that—I am sure it was the one I got from her.
JOHN LAMBERT ( police-constable R 212.) I was on duty on the 12th of May. I saw the prisoner in Mrs. Copper's beer-shop—soon after, I went by the Greyhound, and saw him, and took him—he had this bad 6 d. in his hand—I told him what I took him into custody for—he said if a man had a bad 6 d. he ought to attempt to pass it, if he could not at one place, he ought to go to another, and pass it if he could—I then went to Copper's, and got this 6 d. from Mitchell. MR. JOHN FIELD. I am inspector of coin to the Mint—these sixpences are both counterfeit, and both cast in one mould.
Prisoner's Defence. I was out of work, and the men collected me 1 s. 8 d. I did not know they were bad.
GUILTY . Aged 61.— Confined Six Months.

2039. ANDREW MAHONEY was indicted for breaking and entering the dwelling-house of Charles Wynn Davis, on the 15th of May, at Lambeth, with intent to steal, and stealing therein, 1 reel of cotton, value 1 d.; 1 fork, 5 s.; 1 parasol, 6 s.; and 2 yards of cord, 2 d.; the goods of Susan Coe.
SUSAN COE . I am servant to Charles Wynn Davis, of Stamford-street, Lambeth. On the 15th of May I went to bed at eleven o'clock—I left the kitchen all secure—I am quite sure the window was down—I got up next morning at twenty minutes after seven, found the kitchen window open, and a square of glass broken—the plate drawer in the kitchen was partly open—nothing was taken out of it—there was six forks in it, but they were left—I missed the articles stated—this thimble and cotton are mine.
Cross-examined by MR. PRENDERGAST. Q. The plate drawer was open, but nothing taken out? A. No, there was a great deal of property there—these things are all trifling—the parasol belongs to a friend of mine—the things were all lying together the night before—I heard no noise in the night.
THOMAS AKERS . I live in Thomas's-street, Stamford-street, about thirty yards from the prosecutor. About four o'clock on Tuesday morning I heard a noise, looked out of my window, and saw the prisoner running away—I ran down stairs, ran after him, and caught him—he had been drunk over night—it was evident he was under the influence of drink, but knew what he was doing—he spoke rationally enough at the police station.
Cross-examined. Q. Did not the Magistrate discharge him, because he was drunk? A. No, the inspector did—he was covered with mud, having been rolling about the street—he said if he bad done wrong, he ought to suffer the loss of his country—he said he had wandered down there to go to the privy—he gave his address—in fact he pointed to where he was going.
JOHN PAYNE . I am a policeman. I went after the prisoner—I found him in bed—the thimble, a small piece of the dress, and an umbrella were in the next room.
Cross-examined. The parasol and dress have not been found? A. No—I found him all over mud—he had not washed himself.
DENNIS STANTON ( policeman.) I searched the prisoner, and found this small bit of the stuff in his trowsers pocket, and a reel of cotton.
NOT GUILTY .

[trialAccount t18430612-2044]
2044. WILLIAM MONAHAN was indicted for feloniously assaulting George Luxton, on the 22nd of March, and stabbing and wounding him in and upon his left leg, with intent to do him some grievous bodily harm.
MR. PAYNE conducted the Prosecution.
GEORGE LUXTON . I am a glover, and live in Church-street, Lambeth. On Wednesday morning, the 22nd of March, about one o'clock, I was at the Coburg Arms—I saw the prisoner there—he and I were fighting—a man named Bonner was fighting with him—when I went in at the bar, and as I was passing through the bar, I was shoved against him by Bonner—he struck me, and I struck him again—he struck me in the face several times—I went away, and had something to drink at the bar, and in ten minutes afterwards I found I was stabbed just above the ankle—I examined my trowsers next morning, and found they were cut through in four or five places—I had only one wound—when I discovered that I was wounded the prisoner was gone—I searched for him before I found myself wounded, as I found Bennett was wounded—he was taken to the hospital, and so was I.
Cross-examined by MR. HORRY. Q. There was a number of persons there? A. Yes, twenty at least—I believe there was a general scuffling and fighting going on—in passing between them I was shoved up against the prisoner, who was fighting with Bonner, and while they were fighting I got the blow—I know the Coburg Arms, by going three or four times there—I have often gone there, but not lately—I live in the neighbourhood—I had been to the theatre that night, and went there to have something to drink—I do not go to the theatre very often—I work for Mr. Bolton, but have not been able to work for him for three months—my wife works for him now—I had hardly time to see whether the prisoner and Bonner were drunk—I was not there a minute before I was fighting with the prisoner—it appeared a general row —I walked away from the prisoner—I did not see him fight with anybody else—I stood at the bar, having something to drink—the scuffling appeared to stop—I hit him several times, and he struck me—he fell down—I was not knocked down.
MR. PAYNE. Q. When the prisoner was down was there anybody as near you as he was? A. Yet, they were standing all round—I did not notice any one else on the ground—there were others round him—I believe he was hustled down more than by my blow—there was a quantity of people rushing about the bar.
ALFRED COLLINS . I am a house painter, and live in South-street, Hercules-buildings. I was in the tap-room of the Coburg Arms, and heard a noise in front of the bar—I went there, and saw the prisoner in the act of striking Luxton, who returned it, and a fight commenced, which lasted some minutes—Bennett was alongside me, and witnessed it—Luxton was a friend of Bennett's, and Bennett said he would put a stop to the fighting—I saw nothing happen to the prisoner more than the exchange of blows between Luxton and himself—they were standing up fighting all the time till the prisoner slipped down.
MICHAEL PINE . I live in Park-street, Kennington. I was at the Coburg
Arms, and saw the prisoner fighting with Luxton—I saw something in his hand, but could not see what it was—it was brown, like a piece of wood—he was on his knee—Luxton was standing over him, arm's length off—the prisoner was hitting out with one hand, and had something in the other—Luxton was about a foot off him at the time.
Cross-examined. Q. How long had you been there? A. About half an hour—I was not in the row, but was looking on—I am eighteen years old, and was hammerman to a blacksmith two years ago—I have not been able to work since—I have been in the hospital, and am not well now—I had come from the theatre to this house—there was no acquaintance of mine there—I do not go to the house often—I have been in it once or twice.
MARK WILLIAM GREEN ( police-constable L 2.) I went into the Coburg Arms on the morning of the 22nd of March, about a quarter to two o'clock—there were from fifty to sixty persons outside—I saw Luxton bleeding from the leg—Moody took him to Mr. Brooks, a surgeon—I went shortly after, and found him there—the surgeon endeavoured to stop the blood—nothing was done but tying a handkerchief round his leg—I searched for the prisoner, but could not find him—I knew he lived in Holland-street—I went there twice, and could not find him.
Cross-examined. Q. What sort of a house is the Coburg Arms? A. I think it is the worst house in our division, frequented by girls of the town and thieves.
WILLIAM MOODY ( police-constable S 8.) 1 took the prisoner on the 18th of April, near Chalk-farm—I told him I had a charge against him for stabbing two men near the Victoria Theatre some time ago—he said, "I know nothing about it."
HARRY BENJAMIN LAWRENCE BROOKS . I am a house-surgeon at Westminster Hospital. I examined Luxton—he was bleeding a great deal from a wound in the leg, near the ankle, inflicted apparently by a sharp instrument—I stopped the bleeding, and took him into the hospital—he remained there six or seven weeks—Bennett is there still.
Cross-examined. Q. Was it such a wound as a dinner-knife would produce? A. Yes—it was a large knife—he was kept there for that wound during that time.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18430612-2045]
2045. WILLIAM MONAHAN was again indicted for feloniously assaulting Samuel Bennett, on the 22nd of March, and stabbing and wounding him on his left leg, with intent to do him some grievous bodily harm.
MR. PAYNE conducted the Prosecution.
ALFRED COLLINS . I was at the Coburg Arms on the morning of the 22nd of March, and saw the prisoner there—I saw him and Luxton fighting for some minutes, from one end of the bar to the other—I went to the bar, hearing the noise, and Bennett and I were together, within two or three paces of the prisoner and Luxton—Bennett went to part them—they were standing fighting, but when Bennett got to them through the crowd, the prisoner had slipped down, or been hit down—I saw Bennett in the act of parting Luxton and the prisoner—a quarter of a minute had scarcely elapsed before Bennett joined me again, and said, "They have done it for me," or, "It is done for me," or, "I am hurt"—something to the effect of having his leg hurt—I said, "Nonsense, you can't have had anything done to it more than from the kegs in the bar"—he said, "It is worse than that"—he put his arm on my shoulder, came into the tap-room, pulled up his trowsers, and a stream of blood issued from his left leg, just below the knee—I went in pursuit of
the prisoner, but did not find him—there might have been others besides the prisoner near enough to Bennett to have wounded him, but I did not see them—I saw the prisoner in a kneeling position, or a half-falling position, but a person being before me, I could not ascertain the exact position—Bennett most at that moment have been against him—there was no one but the prisoner down in that particular part—there might have been, a pace or two to the right or left—it was all so sudden.
MICHAEL PINE . I was at the Coburg Arms on the morning of the 22nd of March—I saw the prisoner down on his knees, and saw something brown in his right hand, but what it was I could not say exactly—he was hitting with his left hand, and the other was on the ground—Bennett was half a yard from him at the time, holding some one's coat—I saw a wound on his leg when he went into the tap-room, and pulled up his trowsers—it bled a great deal—I could not see how it happened—they were all scrummaging.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18430612-2046]
2046. JAMES WALDERS was indicted for feloniously breaking and entering the warehouse of Joseph Mahon, on the 25th of April, at St. George the Martyr, Southwark, and stealing therein 2 tons weight of paper, value 57 l.; 1 sack, 6 d.; and 1 pair of scales and beam, 2 l.; his property; and DANIEL REARDON for feloniously inciting him to commit the said felony; and also for receiving the said goods, well knowing them to have been stolen; against the Statute, &c.
MR. CLARKSON conducted the Prosecution.
GRIFFITH EDWARDS . I am a green-grocer, and live at No. 43, Feather-stone-street, St. Luke's—I keep a van and two horses—I know the two prisoners. On Tuesday, the 25th of April, between six and seven o'clock in the evening, Reardon came to me and asked if my van and two horses were at home—I said, "Yes"—he said, "Put them to, for my master wants you"—I said, "Where?"—he said, "On Saffron-hill, Mr. Cohen's"—I immediately put the horses to, and away we rode together to Cohen's house—I stopped there about ten minutes—Cohen said, "Go along with these three men"—I saw Walders there, and two other men, who I do not know—I have not seen them since—Reardon staid behind at his master's, Cohen's house—I asked them where they were going—they said, "To Great Suffolk-street, overthe water"—I said, "I don't know Suffolk-street"—they said, "If you will go on, we will tell you which way to go"—Walders jumped on the top of my pony, and rode all the way over till we came to Suffolk-street, nearly to the bottom—when we came to the yard, they told me to stop—Walders told me to go and get something to drink, and they would pay for it—I did not go to the public-house, but stopped to look at a picture shop close by—I did not go into the yard then, but stopped outside in Great Suffolk-street—while looking at the pictures, Walders came and called me, and told me to bring the van up the yard, and the two horses—I did not see any difficulty in getting into the yard at first—it was open—there were no gates to it that I saw—I took the van and horses into the yard, and backed the van close to the warehouse ladder, by Walders' direction—I have since learnt that ware-house to be Mr. Mahon's—when I backed the van I saw the two men up stairs in the loft of the warehouse, chucking bundles of paper down to Walders, who helped to load the van—when I thought they had nearly enough for my two horses to draw, they put a bag of paper, tied up in small parcels, about the width of my hand, on the tail of the van—the other paper was secured in bundles about a quarter of a hundred weight each—I should judge by my springs that there was near upon two tons of paper put into the van
—they also put in some scales, ropes, and a beam, from the warehouse—they were large scales, similar to warehouse scales—I immediately put my pony to the van, and we drew out of the yard—just as we got out of the archway one of my traces broke—Walders assisted me to mend it, and during the time there was a cart waiting to come up—I pulled my van out into the main road to let the cart come by—when we mended the trace, we came on—this was about half-past seven or a quarter to eight, between seven and eight—I did not see the other two men—they did not join the van any more—Walders kept me company all the way over the bridge till we came to the Old Bailey—he told me to stop at a public-house there—I said, "No, I don't want to stop here, because it is up a hill for my horses"—he said, "Stop and have half a pot of beer"—I stood outside, and had one drink, and away I came—I saw the two men in that public-house, who were in the loft chucking the paper down—they drank after me, and Walders also—Walders and I then came on across Smithfield, up Thomas-street to Saffron-hill, to Cohen's house—the other two men did not come on with us—we got to Cohen's house at half-past eight or a quarter to nine—Cohen and Reardon were outside the door—I did not see Walders and the other two any more that night—Cohen, and Reardon, and a little boy, whose name I do not know, helped me unload the van—it might be some strange boy about there—the paper was unloaded, and put into the cellar with the scales, beam, and rope—after the van was unloaded, Cohen asked me how much—I looked at the time, and said, 3 s. 6 d., which was 18 d. an hour—he changed a half-sovereign at the bar, and paid me—next day, Wednesday, between twelve and one, Reardon came to me, and said, "My master wants you"—he was going to wait till I put my horses to—I told him to go on—I overtook him facing St. Luke's church, in Old-street—he was along with one of the other men that had been along with me over night—that man was drunk—they got up into the van, and I drove to Cohen's house—when I came there, Cohen blowed him up, and said he would kick his backside for stopping so long—some of the paper was put into the van—I should think sixty or seventy bundles, and the rest in bags—it had been put into bags since the over night—Cohen told me to take them down to a yard down the hill, and be and Reardon accompanied me to the house—when I got there it was an empty room in Pump-court, Saffron-hill—Reardon, Cohen, and a lot of boys about there unloaded the van into the room—I then went to Cohen's house again, and Reardon asked me what was to pay—I said 18 d., and he paid me 2 s.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. How do you know that the paper which you took in the van to Pump-court, was the same you had brought the day before, if the external appearance was altered? A. They were tied up in the same bundles—some were in bundles and some in bags—that in the bags was of the same quality as what I had seen the day before—I saw the paper, because the bags were not tied close—it appeared to be the same sort.
Cross-examined by MR. PRENDERGAST. Q. You did not see Walders on the 26th? A. No, I did not know him before—I might have seen him in the market, and not noticed him—I do not know that he is employed as a porter in the market—I did not observe anything remarkable in this employment.
REBECCA MARTIN . I am the wife of Francis Martin, and live in Kesterton's-yard, Great Suffolk-street, Southwark—the prosecutor's warehouse adjoins our house—I know Walders—I saw him in the yard shortly before the 25th of April—on the evening of the 25th, from about half-past seven to eight o'clock, I saw a van brought down the yard to Mr. Mahon's warehouse door, and loaded with paper—I saw Walders there, helping to load the van—
I did not see any persons above in the loft, but I heard them, and saw the paper come down—I saw Edwards there, backing the van to the warehouse—I had seen Walders about once before that night—I cannot say that I saw him more, to notice him.
Cross-examined by MR. PRENDERGAST. Q. Did he appear to you like a porter, a common working man? A. Yes, like a man plying for business.
JOAQUIM JOSE DE OLIVEIRA . I am in the service of Mr. Mahon. I went into the stable-yard on the morning of the 26th of May, about ten or eleven o'clock, in company with a boy—I found the place broken open—it had been fastened ten or eleven days before—there was a padlock on the warehouse-door—the piper was all right then—when I went on the 26th, I found the lock still on the door, but it was broken—when I went to take hold of it, I found it open—I sent a boy for my master—I missed the scales, beam, and a large quantity of paper.
Cross-examined by MR. PRENDERGAST. Q. Had you the key with you? A. Yes, the padlock was still banging on the staple, and was shut down; but when I took hold of it, it gave way and came open—this is the padlock.
JOHN WOODHOUSE ( police-sergeant M 13.) On Wednesday, the 26th of April, in consequence of information, I went to the prosecutor's warehouse—the padlock was given to me by Mr. Mahon—I found it had been forced—on the following Friday, in consequence of information from Edwards, I went with him to No. 4, Pump-court, and in an unoccupied room there found nearly two tons of paper—I produce one of the bundles—some of them bore Excise marks outside—I took it into my possession, and have had it ever since.
Cross-examined by MR. PRENDERGAST. Q. Did you know Walders before? A. No; my interference was in consequence of the prosecutor's application, not in consequence of Edwards's information.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. You went for the paper in consequence of Edwards's information? A. Yes.
JAMES TIDMARSH ( police-constable M 51.) On Friday, the 5th of May, I took Reardon into custody, in Rose-lane, Spitalfields—he was living at a coalshed there—I had been looking for him for nearly a fortnight—I inquired where he was at work—his master said he was gone out with some straw—I found him in a lane opposite Rose-lane, and told him I was a police-constable (I was then in private clothes), and said I apprehended him for being concerned in stealing a quantity of paper belonging to Mr. Mahon—I then told him he had better not say anything, that whatever he said to me I should give in evidence against him—in going to the station, he said all the Cohens ought to be apprehended, and the three thieves were gone to Scotland—on the following Wednesday I took Walders into custody—he was pointed out to me in Farringdon-market by Edwards—I told him I was a police-constable, and that I apprehended him for stealing a quantity of paper from the ware-house of Mr. Mahon—he said, "All that I know of it is, that Cohen employed me."
Cross-examined by MR. PRENDERGAST. Q. Cohen kept a rag-shop, I believe? A. I believe he did—I found Walders in Farringdon-market—he was not dressed as a porter, but in a brown Taglioni coat—I received information from Edwards that he would probably be found there—I expected him to be there, as a place that he frequented—he was standing near a cart—he did not say he was employed for a Mr. Roberts.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. You went where Reardon was employed, and inquired for him there? A. Yes, and I found him within twenty yards of his master's, coming towards the house—I had searched for him at
Cohen's—I found that Cohen had departed—Reardon came up to me and spoke to me first—he said, "I understand that you and somebody else want to see me"—he made no resistance—Edwards was present when he said the Cohens ought to be apprehended, and the three thieves were gone to Scotland—I had said nothing to him before he said that, except that I was a policeman—I did not put any question to him—it was his own voluntary statement going along.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. Had you any knowledge whether Walders was working for anybody or not? A. No more than from Edwards telling me that we should be likely to find him there, as he generally portered in the market.
COURT. Q. When you applied to Reardon's master for him, did you say what you wanted him for? A. No, nor who I was.
JOSEPH MAHON . I am a stationer, carrying on business in Blackman-street, Borough. I have a warehouse in a stable-yard in Great Suffolk-street, where I mostly deposit waste paper, done up in bundles and bags, a variety of millboards and rags—in the years 1839, 1840, and 1841. I had deposited about thirty tons of large bundles of paper, which I purchased of the Excise—about a week before Wednesday, the 26th of April, I saw that paper in my warehouse all safe, except a portion that I had removed myself—I am not in the habit of selling the paper in the state of that produced—that is the state in which I buy it—in consequence of information from Oliveira, I went to the warehouse on the morning of the 27th, (I was not at home on the 26th)—I missed about seven tons of paper, and very near two tons in bags—I missed my scale-beams and rope, a quantity of linen rags and millboard—I had the paper in stacks according to their size—there was seven large stacks, and one or two dwarf ones—there was about three tons and a half gone out of one stack of one particular size, and others out of different stacks—the value of the whole seven tons I consider to be about 200 l., the bags about 30 l., averaging 1 l. a bag, the rags about 3 l., and the scales and beam about 2 l.—I have since been shown by the officers some paper, said to be found in an unoccupied room in Pump-court—it was part of the paper I lost—my ware-house is in the parish of St. George-the-Martyr, Southwark—it is called St. George.
JOHN WOODHOUSE re-examined. It in the parish of St. George-the-Martyr—Tidmarsh and Edwards went with me to No. 4, Pump-court.
GRIFFITH EDWARDS re-examined. The house to which I took the policeman, was the same in which I had deposited part of the paper which I took from Cohen's.
(Thomas Quin, of No. 24, Plough-conrt, Fetter-lane; John Talbot, of No. 10, St. John's-court, King-street, Snow-hill; James Klyne, of No. 21, Plumtree-court, Shoe-lane; John King, salesman, of Newgate-market; and Thomas Kelly, of Chapel-place, Crown-street, Soho, deposed to Walders' good character: and Thomas Sanderson, of No. 12, Flower and Dean-street, Spitalfields, coal-shed keeper; and John Thorogood, Russia mat salesman, of Bermondsey, to that of Reardon.
WALDERS — GUILTY . Aged 25.— Confined Six Months.
REARDON — GUILTY . Aged 19.— Confined Three Months.
of Larceny.
Recommended to mercy by the Jury.
Before Mr. Baron Gurney.

[trialAccount t18430612-2048]
2048. WILLIAM SMITH was indicted for breaking into the warehouse of Joseph Mahon, on the 22nd of April, and stealing therein 392 pounds of piper, value 4 l., his property.
JOSEPH MAHON . I am a wholesale stationer, and live in Blackman-street, Borough. I have a warehouse and stable-yard in Great Suffolk-street, about one hundred yards from my dwelling-house—on or about Tuesday, the 19th of April, there was about twenty-five tons of paper, tied up in quarter of hundreds, and about thirty tons tied up in bags, about seven tons of old millboard, and various packages of rags—I had made considerable purchases of waste paper from the Excise-office shortly before—I had seven stacks consisting wholly of that paper—it was worth 32 s. a cwt., in quarter of cwt. parcels—that in the bags was worth about 20 s.—the warehouse was secured by a padlock—you ascend a flight of steps from the stable-yard up to the warehouse door—no body lives in it—I merely send to it occasionally as I want things—in consequence of information, on the morning of the 27th, I went to the ware-house and missed about seven tons, or rather more, of the excise paper at 32 s. a cwt, from the stack, about twenty sacks of that packed in bags—the value of the paper stolen was about 210 l. or 220 l.—there were other things gone besides—I have seen some paper in the constable's possession—I never sell it in the state in which it has been found—I can undertake to say it never has been sold—I saw the warehouse safe when I was there on the 18th or 19th.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. Is anybody authorised to sell besides you? A. Yes, a man named Denny—he is not here—I can answer for it he never sold it in this state, nor any other person—Denny has nothing to do with the warehouse—he could not sell it without my knowledge.
JOAQUIM JOSE DE OLIVEIRA . I am in the employ of Mr. Mahon, I went to the warehouse on the morning of the 26th of April—the padlock was on the stable door as if it was safe—I put my hand-to unlock it, and it opened—I found it had been forced—I sent the boy to call my master.
PETER AUSTIN . I am a cheesemonger, and live in St. John-street, Clerkenwell. On the 22nd of April, about six in the evening, the prisoner and another walked into the shop, produced lcwt. 5lbs. of paper, and asked me to boy it—I believe they brought it in a truck—I agreed to give 24 s. a cwt., which was what he asked—he said he was selling it for Cohen, of Saffron-hill, that it belonged to Cohen—I did not know Cohen—I did not make inquiry how he got it—the officer came to me about a week after—I delivered what I had purchased, to him, three parcels—this now produced is a portion of it—I believe I paid the prisoner the money—I took no bill and receipt—I did not know where he lived—I knew him before, and had bought paper of him for some years before.
Cross-examined. Q. How much did you pay him? A. 25 s. for Mr. Cohen—on the previous occasions he sold on his own account.
JOHN HARDCASTLE . I am a cheesemonger, and live in Aylesbury-street, Clerkenwell. On the 24th of April, about ten o'clock in the morning, the prisoner came to my shop, and offered paper for sale—he had it in a cart—there was another person with him—he brought in a few sheets as a sample, and asked me to purchase it—he said he had about 4 cwt. and 1 qr., at 28 s. a cwt.—I said I was not a buyer—he went away—in about two hours he came back again, and pressed me very much to make an offer for it—I said I was fall of paper, but if 20 s. a cwt. would buy it I would take what he had left, as he said he had sold part—he went away, and in half an hour he brought me 2cwt. 2qrs. 13lbs.—I gave him 20 s. a cwt. for it—I asked him who he was selling it for—he said, "Lazarus Cohen, of Saffron-hill"—he made no bill, but I made a memorandum, as bought of Cohen, which he put his mark to—I have it here—I did not go to see Cohen—I know there are several
Cohens in the neighbourhood—I do not know whether there was one on Saffron-hill—I knew the prisoner before.
Cross-examined. Q. Was he in the habit of selling paper? A. He sold me a great many lots on his own account previous to this—there was time for him to go from my house to Saffron-hill between the offer and the purchase.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. Did you ask if he had been to Saffron-hill? A. No—I always gave him a fair price for my purchases—I got it as cheap as I could—I did not know where he lived.
JOHN WOODHOUSE ( police-sergeant M 13.) On Wednesday, the 26th of April, I went to examine the prosecutor's warehouse, and the following day I took the prisoner into custody in White Horse-court, Cow-cross—I did not know him before—I said I wanted him on suspicion of stealing a quantity of paper from Mr. Mahon's premises—he said, "All I know about it is, Cohen employed me to sell it"—I have not been able to find Cohen—he has run away—I found more of this paper in an unfurnished room or cellar in Pump-court, which was taken by Cohen—I produce part of the paper from Austin and Hardcastle.
GRIFFITH EDWARDS . I am a vanman, and live in Featherstone-street, City-road. On the 25th of April I was hired by Reardon to go to Cohen's—I went and there found a person named Walders, and Cohen, and two others, by whose direction I went to the stable-yard in Great Suffolk-street—Cohen told me to go with these men, and they directed me to Great Suffolk-street-I brought away nearly two tons of pager from there—it was brought to Cohen's, put in the cellar, and afterwards removed to Pump-court.
MR. MAHON re-examined. These two parcels of paper were safe in the warehouse with the rest on the 18th or 19th of April, and had not been sold.
NOT GUILTY .

2049. WILLIAM SMITH was again indicted for feloniously receiving of an evil-disposed person, 2341lbs. weight of paper, value 25 l., the goods of Joseph Mahon, well knowing it to be stolen.
MESSRS. CLARKSON and DOANE conducted the Prosecution.
(The evidence given by Joseph Mahon, Joaquim Jose de Oliveira, Peter Austin, and John Hardcastle, in the last case, was read over by the Court.)
WILLIAM JAMES STYCH . I am a waste paper-dealer, and live in White Horse-alley, Clerkenwell. On the 25th of April, between two and three o'clock, the prisoner came into my shop with a sample of paper, and asked 22 s. a cwt.—I told him to leave it, and call again, which he did, about four o'clock—I offered him 18 s. a cwt.—he said he would go and see—he came back about five o'clock, and said if I would give him 18 s. 6 d. a cwt., and allow him 6 d. for commission, be would take me to the place where it was for sale—I agreed, and he took me to Cohen's marine-store shop on Saffron-hill—I there saw a large quantity of waste paper in bundles in the shop—I bought 20 cwt. 3 qrs. 17lbs., which was all that was in the shop—I paid 19 l. in gold and 6 s. in silver, and received this receipt from Cohen—I had the paper brought away that same evening, and afterwards gave it up to the police the same day.
Cross-examined. Q. What sort of paper is it? A. (Looking at a bundle produced by the officer) this is not it—it was a great deal smaller—it was all like these small bundles—I gave a fair and reasonable price for it.
JOHN WOODHOUSE ( police-sergeant M 13.) On the 26th of April I examined the prosecutor's warehouse, and saw the padlock had been forced—I took the prisoner into custody on the 27th, and told him I wanted him, on suspicion of stealing a quantity of paper from Mr. Mahon's, in the Borough—
he said "All that I know about it is, that Cohen employed me to sell it"—I went to Mr. Stych's on the 26th, and received from him the paper I now produce—this large bundle is one of the bundlet that were among it—I received altogether 1 ton 3qrs. 21lbs.—there were a great many of these large bundles among—Mr. Mahon was present.
Cross-examined. Q. How many small bundles were there? A. A great many, and some of these large ones—the rest is locked up at the station—then were, I suppose, not more than half a dozen large bundles.
MR. MAHON re-examined. I saw the paper taken from Stych's—I think I only saw three or four large bundles—the rest were in 1/4cwt. bundles—I sell this paper at 32 l. a ton wholesale—it is excise paper—the value of the paper I found at Stych's was between 28 l. and 30 l. to me—I should sell it at 32 l. wholesale.
NOT GUILTY .
Before Mr. Recorder.
[trialAccount t18430612-2050]
2050. WILLIAM SHARP was indicted for burglariously breaking and entering the dwelling-house of William Woodward, about three in the night of the 10th of May, at Lambeth, and stealing therein, 1 hat, value 1 l.; 1 spoon, 6 s.; 1 kettle, 2 l.; 1 thimble, 6 d.; 1 top of a pepper-castor, 6 d.; and 1 candlestick, 4 s.; his goods.
WILLIAM WOODWARD . I am a hatter, and live at No. 128, Lambeth-walk, in the parish of St. Mary, Lambeth—I keep the house. On the 10th of May I missed several things—the prisoner lodged in the house, up stairs—I had a wooden partition, which fenced off my shop and parlour from the passage, and on coming down, about half-past seven o'clock in the morning of the 11th, I noticed that the partition had been moved and altered, and the screws drawn out, which would admit anybody into the shop—I found the prisoner under the counter in the shop—I had locked up that shop overnight, so that the partition would exclude anybody, unless by drawing the screws—I missed a best beaver hat, a silver caddy spoon, a large brass brewing kettle, a silver thimble, a pepper top, and several other articles—I had missed several things the day before, but those I am now speaking of were safe the overnight, when I went to bed—I asked the prisoner what he did there—he said he got there to protect the shop—the policeman afterwards found a silver thimble of mine in his possession at the station—I searched his room in the house, and found a saw, and the top of a pepper castor, which had been safe overnight in the parlour, which is inclosed in the same partition as the shop—I cannot say how long the saw had been there—I also lost spades, pick-axes, trivets, and other things—the parlour and shop communicate by a door—the partition is a moving one—one part of it is moved every day, and stands against the wall, and the other part goes back on hinges, to throw part of the passage into the shop—it is closed and bolted at night, so that no one can get in without breaking it open, or drawing the screws—I missed property both from the parlour and shop, which were both fastened up overnight—I found the screws drawn—I occupy the bottom part of the house, and sleep on the first floor, over the shop—I have not found anything but the thimble and this candle-stick—one part of it was concealed under the counter, where the prisoner was, and the other part was found in the privy.
Prisoner. I work in a dust-yard, and I picked up the thimble and pepper-top in the dust; he had no such thing as a pepper-top; I gave him a glass pepper-box myself; there is the dirt on the top now as I picked it up; my father gave me the saw when I was first married; he has taken that and other things since I have been here for trial. Witness. The pepper-top is
just the same as it was on my mantel-piece, in one of my mugs, in the parlour—I have had it by me for years—the kettle was kept in a cupboard underneath the stairs, in the back parlour, and the handle of it was left behind in the wash-house—I generally came down about seven o'clock, or half-past—I know the saw by a crack in it, which was made years ago—I never went into the prisoner's room without the policeman.
Prisoner. I made that crack in the saw myself, by cutting a piece of wood. Witness. It was done at Egham, in sawing off the limb of a damson tree.
WILLIAM HALL ( police-constable L 88.) I searched the prisoner—I found this silver thimble in his pocket—I afterwards went to his room, and found this saw and this top of a pepper-castor—I found this part of the candlestick under the counter, where he was concealed—the prosecutor had allowed him to go to the privy—I found him there—the privy was afterwards emptied, and this other part of the candlestick was found there.
Prisoner's Defence. I was never under the counter; I was by the side of it; he told me he had lost a hat about a week before, and he blamed a man that worked for his brother; I said I was very sorry; I even brought two dogs to the place, bepause no one should get in; there was a hole in the back yard, and he said they must have come through there; I have lived in his house nine months, and he never knew me touch a thing; I have a witness outside that gave my father the saw.
WILLIAM WOODWARD re-examined. I am state I found the prisoner under the counter—he was on his knees, and this part of the candlestick was close to him—he went to the privy directly, before I called the constable—I allowed him to go—this other part of the candlestick was found there, and a screw-driver by the side of it, belonging to me, and which had been in a cupboard in the back parlour.
Prisoner. He said at Union-hall that the two screw-drivers were in the bottom cupboard. Witness. No—the constable has those two—this it the one I found in the privy.
Prisoner. You have got up all manner of things, and brought them up out of spite—other people live in the place—the door is open at four o'clock in the morning, and people go in and out as well as me—a coalheaver goes out every morning at four o'clock. Witness. That person has lived with me ten years, and I never lost a thing—I am certain the screw-driven were in the top cupboard, and in the morning they were moved into the bottom one—I found the caddy, which was in the back parlour window, had been broken open, and the spoon was gone—he might get into the back parlour without going to the shop, but he had got into the shop first—the marks on the caddy exactly corresponded with the screw-driver—the candlestick was whole the night before, safe in the back parlour cupboard.
Witnesses for the Defence.
---- SHARP. I am the prisoner's mother—I know this saw—it is broken rather on the slant in the small teeth—it has been in my place three years, and was kept in my son's box with his tools—he worked in a dust-yard—he had hammers and other tools for his own use, such as hammering his shoes—the saw was given him by his father, twelve months ago, in case he should want a bit of wood, as he began housekeeping—I live at Mr. Tilliard's, No. 4, Vine-street, York-road, Lambeth—it might be five or six months since I saw the saw—I noticed this mark, as we have had it so long in our own use—my husband used it when he wanted it for a bit of wood, or any odd thing—it was just as it is now when we first had it—it is a perfect saw—it was never any longer since we have had it.
ABRAHAM SHARP . I am a waterman and lighterman—I gave my son a saw, which was broken off at the point—there was no other mark on it that I noticed—there was no flaw in it—it was not damaged or split, to my knowledge—I had it nearly three years—it was given me by a Mrs. Grove, who lives close against Lambeth-church—she used to live in the same house I live in now, and when she moved she said the saw was no good to her, and I might have it—and when my son got married, I got a little saw of my own out of pledge, and gave him this—( looking at it)—this is the saw—I have not seen it before to-day for six months—the prisoner is in no way of business—he sometimes used to work at the dust-yard, or get a job where he could—I gave him the saw for his own use—Mrs. Grove was here, but is very unwell and is gone home.
WILLIAM WOODWARD re-examined. The prisoner never did any work for me in which he would make use of a saw—I never knew him do any carpenter's work, or use a saw—he never said he had a saw—this saw was found hide underneath his box.
Prisoner. Q. Did not you lend my wife a chopper to save her using a saw? A. No—she asked me one morning to lend her a chopper, to cut a bit of wood, which I did—I never saw you with a saw—this saw was my uncle Fletcher's, who lived at Egham, and I brought it up ten years ago—I have not used it for two years—I cannot be mistaken in it, I have had it in my hand so many times.
WILLIAM HALL re-examined. I found the saw concealed underneath the prisoner's box.
GUILTY of Larceny only. Aged 22.— Transported for Seven Years.
Before Mr. Baron Gurney.
[trialAccount t18430612-2051]
2051. GEORGE CLARKE was indicted for feloniously assaulting Mary Ann Clarke, on the 28th of May, by casting, and throwing her down to and against the floor of a certain room, and with his hands and feet beating, striking, and kicking her upon her head, &c., with intent to murder her.—2nd COUNT, for cutting and wounding her on the head, with like intent.—3rd COUNT, stating his intent to be to do her some grievous bodily harm.
MART ANN CLARKE . I am the prisoner's wife. I have been living lately at the Bell Tavern, Wellington-street, strand, as cook—my husband was living during that time at Wimbledon-common—on Thursday the 25th of May, I received this letter from him—it is his handwriting—"Mary Ann Clarke, at the Bell Tavern, Wellington-street, North Strand, May 23, 1843, 9 o'clock. My dear Wife, I just received your letter, but you are so fond of that feller as you were a living with, as I wonder your sending to me, as you do not intend to come home to live with me; but I assure you that I have known no woman since you have been away from me; for I have the confidence still to think you will return home and make me happy once more. I have one thing to tell you, that is, you have heard me speak of my uncle, and at his death I should receive 20 l. I have had a letter from the lawyer to say he is dead, and died on the 25th of August last, and we all brothers and sisters are to meet on the 25th of August next to receive our dividends, but he has not said how much: but my brother wrote to me on Saturday to tell me that the lawyer told him that it was above 800 l. each, and be it right I will bid farewell to sawing. So now, my dear wife, if you have got the love for me, as I always thought you had, return home to me and make me happy once more as before you left me. I thought there was no man so happy as myself; and since then, I have been so unhappy, I hope you will consider yourself and come home, and be comfortable, and make me the same. Several people have tried to persuade me to take a warrant out against you; but how can I hurt
the woman I love better than my own self? it is impossible for me to do it, as the love I have for you, no person knows besides myself. My housekeeper is a going to leave me, for she has a cook's place in London, and she is going to it in a fortnight. So, now, my dear wife, let me know your mind by the return of post. So no more at present, from your ever dutiful and loving husband,
" GEORGE CLARKE."
Q. What was the reason of your being away from home? A. His ill treatment, the fortnight I was his wife—I sent an answer to the letter, and, in consequence of the letter I went to Putney by the steam-boat on Sunday—I found my husband waiting for me, looking over the bridge, when I arrived by the steamer—I walked with him towards home—I said, "You do not seem well, my dear"—he said, "I am uneasy in my mind"—we walked along till we came to the Green Man—he said, "Let us go in and have something to drink"—before that he had said, "You will never go home to London again"—he did not say why—I said, "Yes, my dear, I must go home and give mistress a month's warning, and I will pay the woman that keeps your house a month's wages to stop with you"—after we had been to the Green Man, he wanted me to go by the Bald Faced Stag way, but I told him it was two miles out of the way, and I would not—that would have taken me down a bye-lane—he said he was down there in the morning, and he wanted to go that way—I went home with him—we quarrelled all the way home—after I got home I took off my shawl and things, and put them up in the bed-room—I turned towards him and was quite cheerful to see my bouse so clean and comfortable—I said it gave me great pleasure to see him so comfortable—a boy came up—I gave him some halfpence, and he directly said, "Ma'am, I do not like your proceedings"—he then jumped up, got the tongs, and swore he would smash my brains out—I turned round and saw him and the woman scuffling, and I made my escape—the woman was trying to take the tongs from him—I ran out, and he dragged me in by the hair of my head, flung me on the floor, kicked me in the mouth, and broke my jaw-bone—he broke my ribs and kicked me—I am black all over—the woman ran out for assistance—some persons came—the door was shut but not locked—he burst the door against the person who tried to open it—while he was doing this, he said, "You b----, I have got you, and will be the death of you"—I cannot recollect any thing more.
Prisoner. I never said I would be the death of her—she said she would not come home—I said, "Now, I have got you, I will never part with you; I cannot be happy without you."
REBECCA SINNOTT . I have kept house for the prisoner for four months—I remember Mrs. Clarke coming home on Sunday, the 28th of May—I was going to get tea for them—there was some beer—she asked Mr. Clarke to drink—words ensued—she asked him to drink some beer—he said he did not want any beer, it was her money and her life, and then he seized the tongs—he did not strike her—I got them away somehow—he gave her blows with his fist—she ran out, and he dragged her in by the hair, knocked her down on the floor, kicked her, and beat her with his fist—soon after the police came and took him away—I did not hear what he said while he was doing this.
Prisoner. I did not have the tongs; I believe my wife got them, and hit me on the head; I have the wound here. Witness. She did not hit him at all—I am sure he had them.
HENRY MITCHELL . I am a surgeon, and live in Chancery-lane. The prosecutrix was brought to town, and consulted me on the Wednesday after the injury—I found severe contusions from the crown of her head to the lower part of her person—the legs were materially bruised, and have evidence of it
now—she had a fracture of the under jaw, and of the sixth rib, on the left side, besides contusions—I have no hesitation in saying, that one-fifth part of the injury she has received has on many occasions destroyed life.
RICHARD GOLDING ( policeman.) On Sunday, the 28th May, from the information I received, I went to the prisoner's residence, on Wimbledon-common—his wife was sitting in a chair, suffering from bruises on the neck and face—the prisoner was in the bed-room—I said, "Halloo, my man, what's this about?"—he stood quite motionless—his wife came into the room—some words ensued—I said, "I cannot allow you to stop here, I must send you over to Wandsworth"—his wife went and charged him—his reply was, "You cannot top me for it"—I understood him to mean she could not hang him for it—I sent him by another constable—when he stood motionless I thought him in liquor, but afterwards I did not.
Prisoner. Q. Did you hear me say I was sorry for it, because I wished she would come home? A. I do not recollect any thing of the kind.
JESSE ROBERTS . I am a policeman. On Sunday evening, the 28th of May, I was called to the prisoner's house—his wife sat on a chair, all over blood, and there was blood on the floor—my sergeant crime in, and told me to take him to the station.
WILLIAM HUDNOTT . On the 28th of May I saw the prisoner and his wife come home—I live near him—I went into my back garden, and heard Mrs. Clarke scream out, saw her run out, and the prisoner after her—he caught her at the end of the house, and dragged her back again—I ran to her assistance, the door was shut, I opened it, and went in—she was lying bleeding on the floor—he was standing over her—I lifted her up, and put her in a chair—two persons came, and took him out of the house—we washed her face, and sent for a policeman.
( The prisoner, in an unconnected address, stated that he had lived some time comfortably with his wife, and always took her home from 25 s. to 30 s. a-week; that she represented she had 20 l. a-year coming to her from her brother, who was coming on the 26th of January to sell some houses, which would produce 700 l. or 800 l., but on returning home to dinner that day she had ran away, and taken all the furniture; that he heard no more of her till the 19th of March, when she wrote to him to fetch her; that he went on the 26th, and found her in apparently a bad house, where he was assaulted by a man: that three weeks afterwards she again wrote to him, begging forgiveness, which he granted, on condition of her coming home, although she had incurred debts to the amount of 30 l.; that he met her, and they drank together; on arriving home they quarrelled about a parcel which she had sent, which he thought was stolen; that she called him a d----d thief, hit him on the head with the tongs, and ran a knife into his hand; that as she went out he pulled her back; she fell and cut her head against the table, but he did not strike her at all, and that he had since been informed she cohabited with another man during his absence at work.)
HENRY MITCHELL re-examined. The fracture of the jaw produced, an internal wound from the fractured portions of the bone—it is not healed yet—it must have been done by a kick or blow of considerable violence.
GUILTY on the 3rd Count .— Transported for Fifteen Years.
Before Edward Bullock, Esq.
